                  Decomposition-aware global optimization:
                certified coordinate grids, conditional recourse,
                              and structural limits



                                                 Abstract
          Small treewidth alone does not make nonconvex mixed-integer optimization tractable: box-
     constrained nonconvex quadratic programming is strongly NP-hard at treewidth two. We show
     that two further quantities make tree decompositions useful for certified global optimization:
     upper bounds Li on the curvature of the objective F along each coordinate, and quadratic growth.
     Subtracting Li w2 /8 at each grid node whose longest adjacent grid interval has length w turns
     exact dynamic programming over a product grid into a valid lower bound. Min-marginals then
     filter the grids, and the stage grids form a certificate, checked by recomputation, √that needs no
     growth assumption. Under growth in the Li -weighted norm, graded grids keep O( κ̄ log(n + 2))
     nodes per coordinate at every accuracy, where κ̄ is a scale-invariant ratio of curvature to growth.
     For mixed-integer box quadratic programs with bag size p and input length I, this certifies
     accuracy 2−q in f (p, κ̄)(I + q + 1)5 bit operations; under growth at a unique minimizer x∗ , with
     the Euclidean ratio κ ≥ κ̄, an exact minimizer takes f1 (p, κ)I O(1) . The growth constant is never
     needed. Growth with ratio κ forces the Hessian block of the interior continuous coordinates
     to have smallest eigenvalue at least 2 maxi Li /κ, and every other local minimizer x′ to satisfy
     F (x′ ) − min F ≥ maxi Li ∥x′ − x∗ ∥2 /κ. Unless P = NP, neither parameter can be dropped
     and the dependence on κ cannot be polylogarithmic; under the randomized exponential-time
     hypothesis, the exponent of κ in a bound f (p)κa(p) I O(1) cannot be o(p). We extend the method
     to exact partial minimization, totally unimodular coupling constraints and several minimizers,
     and test an exact-arithmetic implementation.


1    Introduction
Optimization models in process systems engineering, energy networks and optimal control are often
built from local pieces. A flowsheet links unit models along streams, a multiperiod model links
consecutive time stages, and a network model links quantities at adjacent nodes. The objective is
then a sum of terms that each depend on a few variables, and the graph recording which variables
share a term often has small treewidth. For variables with finitely many values, small treewidth is
exactly what dynamic programming needs: nonserial dynamic programming, bucket elimination
and junction-tree message passing find a global optimum in time exponential only in the width of a
tree decomposition [20, 50, 114].
    Mixed-integer nonlinear models have continuous variables and nonconvex terms, and there
the situation is less clear. Spatial branch-and-bound solvers relax individual nonconvex terms by
convex underestimators and branch on variable domains [16, 17, 139, 171]; they do not use a tree
decomposition to organize the search. Discretizing the continuous variables and running a dynamic
program gives neither a valid bound nor a running time that scales well with the accuracy: a uniform
grid of mesh h on a unit interval has Θ(1/h) points, so tables grow like h−p in the bag size p. Sparse


                                                     1
polynomial optimization exploits the same structure through linear and semidefinite hierarchies
[24, 121, 184]; the known bounds on their size are polynomial in the inverse accuracy [24, 117].
    Small treewidth alone does not make continuous nonconvex problems tractable. Del Pia
and Khajavirad [53, Theorems 1–3] recently showed that box-constrained nonconvex quadratic
programming (box QP) is solvable in strongly polynomial time on forests, but strongly NP-hard at
treewidth two even with small integer coefficients, and that quartic minimization is strongly NP-hard
on paths. This paper asks which additional quantitative information makes tree decompositions
useful for certified global optimization of mixed-integer problems, and what fails when it is absent.

Results. We consider min{F (x) : x ∈ X}, with optimal value F ∗ , where F = a fa (xSa ) is a sum
                                                                                     P

of factors, X is a box in which some coordinates are integer, and a tree decomposition of the scopes
Sa with maximum bag size p is given. Two quantities govern the results: upper bounds Li on the
curvature of F along each coordinate (for a quadratic, Li = max{Hii , 0}), and quadratic growth
of F away from a minimizer x∗ . Point growth F (x) − F ∗ ≥ g∥x − x∗ ∥2 on X gives the condition
number κ = max{1, L/g} with L = maxi Li . Weighted growth F (x) − F ∗ ≥ γ i Li (xi − x∗i )2 on X
                                                                                P

gives κ̄ = max{1, 1/γ}. Point growth implies weighted growth with κ̄ ≤ κ, and κ̄ does not change
when continuous coordinates are rescaled (Definition 3.2).
    Choose finite grids in the coordinates and subtract, at each node v of coordinate i, the unary
correction Li w2 /8, where w is the length of the longest grid interval ending at v. The minimum of
the corrected objective over the grid equals the best vertex lower bound of Bajaj and Hasan [12],
taken with per-coordinate curvature bounds, over all cells of the product grid (Proposition 4.2),
so it is a lower bound on F ∗ . Because the corrections are unary, dynamic programming on the
given decomposition computes this minimum and all coordinate min-marginals without enumerating
cells. Grid intervals whose endpoint min-marginals exceed the value of a known feasible point are
removed, and the grids of the successive stages form a path certificate (Theorem 4.7).
    Growth enters only the running time. Under weighted growth, grids whose spacing grows
linearly away from the current corrected minimizer (graded grids), combined with this filtering,
keep O(θ−1 log(n + 2)) nodes per coordinate at every stage, independently of the accuracy, when
                                                                                      √
the grading θ satisfies 8κ̄θ2 ≤ 1 (Lemma 5.5); filtered uniform grids can need order nκ nodes per
coordinate (Corollary 5.11). The algorithm CT (Algorithm 5.6) tries θ = 2−µ for µ = 2, 3, . . . with
a cap on the grid size, so the growth constant need not be known. Below, I denotes the input length
and q ≥ 0 an integer.

Theorem 1.1 (Main results). Let F be an explicit rational quadratic on a mixed box X, given as a
sum of factors with a tree decomposition of maximum bag size p, and let Li = max{0, ∂ii F }.

 (a) On every instance, CT(2−q ) returns a feasible rational point x̂ and a path certificate showing
     β ≤ F ∗ ≤ F (x̂) ≤ β + 2−q . The certificate is checked by recomputing one dynamic program per
     stage in exact arithmetic, and its validity uses no convexity, uniqueness or growth (Theorems 4.7
     and 5.7).

 (b) If F has                                                                             −q
               √ weighted growth with condition number κ̄, every grid on which CT(2−q ) builds tables
     has O( κ̄ log(n + 2)) nodes per coordinate, independently     √ p of q, and 2CT(2 ) uses at most
                         5
     f (p, κ̄)(I + q + 1) bit operations, where f (p, t) = c0 (c1 p t) t(1 + log2 t) for absolute constants
     c0 , c1 . The certificate has size, and is checked in time, within the same bound. Neither the
     growth constant nor κ̄ is an input (Lemma 5.5 and Theorem 5.7).

 (c) EX (Algorithm 6.13), which runs CT(2−q ) for q = 1, 2, 4, . . . and snaps each incumbent to
     a rational stationary point of a nearby face of the box until an exact test accepts it, returns


                                                    2
      a global minimizer and F ∗ on every instance, with a certificate whose validity uses neither
      growth nor uniqueness. If F has point growth with condition number κ, EX uses at most
      f1 (p, κ)(I + 1)C1 bit operations, for a computable function f1 and an absolute constant C1
      (Theorem 6.14).

    In parameterized terms, part (b) is a fixed-parameter bound in the parameters p and ⌈κ̄⌉, which
CT neither receives nor computes; here κ̄ is the smallest weighted condition number of the instance,
which exists (Section 3 and Remark 5.8). To the best of our knowledge, no earlier result gives
a running time of the form f (p, κ) poly(I + q) for certified approximation, or f (p, κ) poly(I) for
exact solution, of nonconvex or mixed-integer box quadratic programs (Section 2). Under point
growth, the approximation result extends, with κ in place of κ̄, to factors that are polynomials of
fixed degree (Corollary 6.22), but continuous polynomial objectives can have irrational minimizers
(Example 6.23). Exactness itself needs no uniqueness: every instance has a growth constant gS > 0
towards its optimal set, and a certified gap of 2−k with k = poly(I) + max{0, log2 (1/gS )} lets the
snapping step return an exact minimizer (Theorem 6.9 and Remark 6.10); point growth in part (c)
only bounds the time needed to reach this accuracy.

Scope of the growth hypothesis. For a box QP, minimality alone confines nonconvexity:
at every minimizer, the Hessian block HJ0 J0 of the set J0 of continuous coordinates strictly
inside their bounds is positive semidefinite, so every direction of negative curvature involves
active bounds or integer coordinates. Growth adds quantitative conditions (Lemma 3.3). Locally,
point growth forces HJ0 J0 ⪰ 2gI, so κ ≥ 2L/λmin (HJ0 J0 ) if J0 ̸= ∅, and weighted growth forces
HJ0 J0 ⪰ 2γ diag(Li )i∈J0 . Globally, every other local minimizer x′ satisfies F (x′ ) − F ∗ ≥ g∥x′ − x∗ ∥2 ,
so κ ≥ L∥x′ − x∗ ∥2 /(F (x′ ) − F ∗ ). Within these conditions an instance can have an indefinite Hessian,
κ ≤ 21
     4 and exponentially many strict local minima (Example 5.12). The condition number can be
exponential in I, already for n = 2 (Section 3). We do not claim that κ or κ̄ is moderate for the
application models mentioned above; they bound only the running time.

Lower bounds. Parts (a)–(c) of Theorem 1.2 assume P ̸= NP, the exponential-time hypothesis
                                                                                  ′
(ETH) that, for some c > 0, no algorithm decides 3-SAT on n′ variables in time 2cn [101], or its
randomized version (rETH), which makes the same claim for algorithms with error probability at
most 1/3 [56]; part (d) is unconditional.

Theorem 1.2 (Lower bounds).

 (a) Unless P = NP, no algorithm computes, for every rational continuous box QP with a supplied
     tree decomposition of maximum bag size three and a unique minimizer, F ∗ within 2−q in time
     polynomial in I + q + log κ. The hard instances have log κ = O(I) (Proposition 10.1 and
     Corollary 10.2).

 (b) Computing F ∗ within 12 is NP-hard for box QPs with κ̄ ≤ κ ≤ 2, continuous or all-binary, by
     a reduction from maximum independent set; under ETH it cannot be done in time 2o(p) I O(1)
     (Proposition 10.5).

 (c) Under rETH there are no computable f and computable nondecreasing unbounded ψ ≥ 1 such
     that some algorithm outputs, for every integer box QP with a supplied tree decomposition
     of maximum bag size p and a unique minimizer, a feasible point within 12 of F ∗ in time
     f (p)(κI)p/ψ(p) (Proposition 10.6).

 (d) For p ≥ 1 and κ ≥ 8p, every deterministic algorithm that accesses F only through its values and
     returns a point with gap below Lp/κ for all functions on [0, 1]p with upper coordinate curvature
     L, a unique minimizer and point growth L/κ makes at least (κ/(8p))p/2 − 1 evaluations on

    Thus, unless P = NP, neither parameter can be dropped and the dependence on κ cannot be
polylogarithmic; under ETH the dependence on p is exponential even for κ ≤ 2; and under rETH
the exponent of κ in a bound f (p)κa(p) poly(I) cannot be o(p), already for integer box quadratics,
whereas Theorem 1.1(b) has exponent p/2 + O(1). Because κ̄ ≤ κ, the same holds for κ̄. In the
value-oracle model of part (d), CT attains the optimal exponent p/2 of κ (Remark 10.4).
    Section 10 proves further limits. The expanding-box chain of length m (Example 5.13) has bag
size three and κ ≤ 80, and CT certifies accuracy 2−q on it in time polynomial in m and q, but
the exact Bellman message for its last state has at least 2m−1 quadratic pieces (Proposition 10.7).
Growth towards a continuum of minimizers does not bound the grids produced by filtering, and

                                                       4
under growth towards an unknown optimal set, a deterministic algorithm that queries values and
derivatives needs at least of order ε−n/2 queries for accuracy ε, already when κS = 1. A path of local
affine equalities makes the problem NP-hard at bag size three and κ = 1, and matching separator
moments of any finite order gives no local error bound in terms of negative curvature. As far as we
know, the expanding-box chain, the unique-minimizer hardness with explicit growth (a refinement
of [53, Remark 2]), the rETH bound (a quadratic encoding of multicolored clique with a unique
minimizer) and the moment obstruction are new; the other statements adapt standard constructions,
among them the running-sum encoding of Subset Sum of Bienstock and Muñoz [24, Appendix A]
(see also [38, Example 1.1]).

Conditional recourse. A stiff convex term inflates the condition number even when the subprob-
lem it defines is easy. Exact minimization over some variables for fixed values of the others (recourse)
preserves upper coordinate curvatures and growth, so the certificate and the growth analysis apply
to the value function whenever it is a sum of factors that can be evaluated exactly in polynomial
time (Theorem 7.2). Piecewise-affine optimal responses of private convex quadratic blocks certify
a reduction of the coordinate curvatures of the value function that is the largest possible for the
block (Theorem 7.9 and Proposition 7.8), but recourse does not reduce the parameter to the ratio
of negative curvature to growth in general (Proposition 7.11). For a quadratic whose recourse
problem is concave in each coordinate and submodular after sign changes, each recourse value is one
minimum cut with a flow certificate (Theorems 7.15 and 7.18), and for balanced box quadratics the
grid problem itself is a minimum cut, so no tree decomposition is needed (Theorem 7.24). Paramet-
ric quadratic programming, cut representations of submodular quadratic functions and threshold
encodings of ordered labels are classical; what is new is the curvature and growth accounting that
keeps them inside the certificate.

Coupling constraints. The certificate rests on rounding each coordinate independently, which
breaks under coupling constraints. Totally unimodular (TU) coupling constraints with mesh-aligned
data admit exactly feasible correlated rounding, which restores the certificate and, for quadratics,
exact output. Under set growth with finitely many optimal values per coordinate, the algorithm
is polynomial for fixed bag size p when the condition number κc (built from a curvature bound
for the continuous variables and the set-growth constant), the box widths measured in mesh units
(s/η) and the number r of optimal values per coordinate are polynomially bounded (Theorem 8.9).
Because its meshes are uniform, it is not fixed-parameter tractable in (p, κc ) (Section 8.7), and its
pseudopolynomial first grid cannot be removed unless P = NP (Proposition 10.10).

Several minimizers. One graded grid can need exponential time already with two isolated
                                                                                            √
minimizers (Proposition 9.1). If the optimal set is finite, unions of uniform cells keep O(r nκS ) nodes
per coordinate, where r bounds the number of optimal values of a coordinate and κS = max{1, L/gS }
uses the growth constant gS towards the optimal set (Theorem 9.4). For quadratics with nonpositive
Hessian diagonal on a mixed box, a dynamic program over the box endpoints describes the whole
optimal set as a constraint problem on the same tree decomposition. From it, uniqueness of the
minimizer, the dimension of the optimal set and, if it is finite, the number of minimizers are
computed in 2O(p) poly(I) bit operations (Theorem 9.7 and Corollary 9.8). This extends Rosenberg’s
face description of the optimal set of a multilinear function [161] and zero-residual descriptions of
all optimal labelings of discrete problems [183, 187] to strictly concave coordinates and mixed boxes,
in factored form. For continuous box quadratics with a classical diagonal Lagrangian certificate
of optimality [106, 125], which is the same at every minimizer, an exact minimizer and an exact


                                                   5
description of the optimal set are found in f ′ (p, κS ) poly(I) bit operations, for a computable function
f ′ , without knowledge of gS (Theorem 9.14).

Organization. Section 2 reviews related work, and Section 3 fixes the model, the curvature bounds
and the growth conditions. Sections 4–6 prove Theorem 1.1, Sections 7–9 treat the extensions of
Table 1, Section 10 proves Theorem 1.2 and the further limits, Section 11 reports experiments, and
Section 12 lists open problems. The appendices follow the order of the sections and contain the
longer proofs and secondary results.


2    Related work
Tractability of sparse nonconvex quadratic programs. For binary quadratic and pseudo-
Boolean optimization, bounded treewidth gives polynomial algorithms by nonserial dynamic pro-
gramming [20, 42, 50] and exact lift-and-project or linear formulations of size exponential only in
the width [25, 122, 181]; sparse moment and sum-of-squares hierarchies exploit the same structure
for polynomial problems [121, 184]. For continuous box QP, the tractable classes of unbounded
treewidth of Del Pia and Khajavirad [53] and of Khajavirad [110] take variables with nonpositive
diagonal to be binary (Remark 7.10 compares Khajavirad’s elimination of the continuous components
with our value factors). The exact piecewise-quadratic messages of Del Pia and Khajavirad’s forest
algorithm, also used for total-variation problems on trees [116, 120], are compared with our grids in
Section 10.4. Box QP is also polynomial for fixed Hessian rank [93]. For convex quadratics with
indicator variables, Bhathena et al. give dynamic programs on trees [22] and on graphs of bounded
treewidth, the latter linear in n for fixed width, margin, volume growth and condition numbers [21];
this is the closest treewidth-and-conditioning result we know (see also [23]). For integer QP, bounded
treewidth together with bounded domains gives fixed-parameter tractability [65]; parameterized
by the number of variables, the problem is fixed-parameter tractable with bounded coefficients
[126] and W[1]-hard without them [91]; see [74] for integer linear programming. In fixed dimension,
integer QP in the plane [54] and, according to a recent preprint, mixed-integer QP (MIQP) [6]
are solved exactly in polynomial time. Approximation schemes polynomial in 1/ε exist in fixed
dimension [49, 92], for sparse polynomial problems with constraints [24, Theorem 4], for QP with
a fixed number of negative eigenvalues in exact real arithmetic [178], and for MIQP with fixed
rank and a fixed number of integer variables [51] or, in the bit model, a fixed number of negative
eigenvalues and of integer variables [52]. Several of these works explain why, in their settings, the
dependence on 1/ε cannot be polylogarithmic unless P = NP [24, 52, 178], from the NP-hardness of
Subset Sum or of QP with one negative eigenvalue [151]. Running times that depend on a condition
number of the instance are standard in numerical computation [33].

Grid and vertex bounds, filtering and certificates. Lower bounds from function values
on a grid are classical for Lipschitz functions [147, 157, 167]; with second-order information the
error is quadratic in the mesh [30, 48]. Separable quadratic perturbations that convexify a function
(αBB) have maximum separation 14 i αi (ui − ℓi )2 [2, 4, 134]; perturbations of the opposite sign
                                      P

make it edge-concave, with a vertex-polyhedral envelope [89, 136, 138, 170], and Bajaj and Hasan
evaluate such an underestimator, built from an upper bound on the diagonal Hessian entries, at
the 2n vertices of a box [12]. Piecewise-linear and sawtooth relaxations of squares apply the same
second-order error termwise inside a mixed-integer program (MIP) [13, 14, 59].
    Removing a region whose relaxation value exceeds an incumbent is optimality-based bound
tightening and branch-and-reduce [16, 79, 158, 163], cost-based domain filtering in constraint pro-


                                                    6
gramming [70] and dead-end elimination in protein design [57, 72]; thresholded min-marginals,
computed by two passes of message passing [3, 47, 113, 183], prune states in structured prediction
cascades [186]. Exact rational MIP [41, 66] and independently checkable branch-and-bound cer-
tificates [37] guard MIP results against floating-point error, and our path certificate follows the
latter, with a tree that is a path; interval methods and safe bounds control rounding errors instead
[86, 87, 149]. Rational sum-of-squares certificates give exact lower bounds for polynomial objectives
[107, 155], but these bounds are values of relaxations, whereas a path certificate brackets F ∗ within
any prescribed 2−q .

Accuracy-independent work under growth. With second-order convergent bounds, the
number of branch-and-bound boxes near a nondegenerate minimizer is independent of the tolerance
(the cluster problem), but local, asymptotic and possibly exponential in the dimension and in the
conditioning of the Hessian [61, 108, 148, 185]. Lemmas 5.4 and 5.5 are a global, non-asymptotic form
of this phenomenon in which the exponential dependence falls on the bag size, and 8κ̄θ2 ≤ 1 plays
the role of the bound on the second-order prefactor. Optimistic optimization attains exponential
rates when the growth order at the optimum matches the assumed smoothness [141]; for Lipschitz
functions with small near-optimal sets, branch-and-bound needs O(log(1/ε)) function values [154],
and Bachoc, Cesari and Gerchinovitz characterize, instance by instance, the number of evaluations
needed to find and certify an ε-optimal point [11]. Quadratic growth and error bounds give linear
convergence of local methods [60, 109, 130, 145], and restarts remove the need to know the growth
constant [162], as our capped trials do.
    Proximity and scaling give algorithms logarithmic in the domain size for linear and separable
convex integer programs [40, 67, 80, 96, 100]. The closest classical analogue of our stages is the scaling
algorithm of Hochbaum and Shanthikumar. For a separable convex objective over {x : Ax ≥ b},
each iteration minimizes a piecewise-linear interpolation of the objective on a grid of O(n∆A ) points
per variable, where ∆A bounds the absolute values of the subdeterminants of A. The grid lies in
a box around the previous solution, and a proximity theorem allows the box to be halved. For
continuous variables, ⌈log2 (s/(2ε))⌉ such grids, with s the initial box width, give a point within
ε of an optimal solution in the maximum norm, guaranteed but not certified [96, Section 1.3 and
Theorems 1.1, 3.8 and 4.4]. For totally unimodular A and integral data, the integer problem is
solved by one linear program over the interpolation at all integer points [137], or by one such linear
program per scale [96, Theorem 4.3]; Lemma 8.3 uses the same integrality. Our objectives are
nonseparable and nonconvex. The retained region comes from min-marginal filtering, which needs
no assumption and yields a certificate; growth only bounds its size. The grid problems are solved
by dynamic programming over a tree decomposition.

Adaptive discretization. Coarse-to-fine dynamic programming refines superstates with opti-
mistic costs along the current optimal path, so that each coarse problem is a lower bound [68, 160].
Interval methods give discrete lower bounds for continuous Markov random fields [153] and, for
optimal power flow on trees, a number of bound evaluations polynomial in 1/ε [62, Theorem 2].
Decision diagrams give outer approximations of integer nonlinear programs [45] and, on partitions
of continuous domains with spatial branch-and-bound, a global method for bounded mixed-integer
nonlinear programs [46]. Continuous distributed constraint optimization [94, 175], variable-resolution
control [142], corridor methods [90], two-level dynamic programming [124], adaptive multivariate
partitioning with bound tightening [144] and re-centered discretizations of fixed size [83] also refine
discretizations adaptively. None of these methods certifies a given accuracy with a number of states
independent of that accuracy.


                                                    7
Exact rational output. A rational QP that attains its minimum has a global minimizer of poly-
nomial encoding length [55, 177]; the stationary-polytope argument of Section 6 follows Vavasis, and
Remark 6.11 discusses NP membership. Rounding to an exact rational is classical [82, Section 5.1],
[119], and in linear programming the optimal face is identified once the gap is below a threshold set
by the encoding length [135, 188]; Lemma 6.8 does this for box QP, with set growth turning a small
gap into proximity to the optimal set. Uniqueness of a QP minimizer implies quadratic growth [39],
and growth towards the optimal set of a QP over a polytope follows from the error bound of Luo
and Sturm [129, Theorem 3.3]. For polynomials of degree three or more, irrational minimizers and
encoding-size phenomena limit exact output [26].

Value functions and multiparametric programming. Eliminating private variables through
their optimal response is standard in Benders decomposition [19, 78] and in condensing for model
predictive control [7, 27, 105]. Multiparametric quadratic programming gives piecewise-affine
minimizers and piecewise-quadratic values on polyhedral critical regions [18, 172], also with nonunique
minimizers [152, 169]; value functions of parameters that enter the objective linearly over a fixed
feasible set are concave [69, 133], and infima preserve semiconcavity [34]. We use these facts for a
global upper coordinate curvature bound of the reduced objective. Low negative inertia with convex
recourse is treated by spatial branching in [128, 178].

Cuts and submodularity. Binary quadratic functions with nonpositive pair coefficients are
minimized by one minimum cut [29, 103, 115, 156], also after sign switching when the signed
interaction graph is balanced [84, 88, 150]. Variables with nonpositive diagonal can be fixed
to endpoints [53, 110, 161], so continuous quadratics that are submodular and concave in each
coordinate reduce to such cuts [32], and pairwise problems submodular with respect to label orders
reduce to cuts on threshold encodings [95, 102, 164]. For general continuous submodular quadratics,
Burer, Natarajan and Willemsen show that a semidefinite relaxation is exact up to dimension three
and give a four-variable gap; whether the problem is polynomial when the dimension is part of
the input is open [32, footnote 2] (for each fixed dimension it is, by enumerating faces [177]). A
sign-restricted case is exact [112], and discretization methods [8–10] are polynomial in 1/ε; for
balanced box quadratics under point growth, Theorem 7.24 is polynomial in I, κ and log(1/ε), which
does not settle the open question because κ can be exponential in I. Partial minimization preserves
submodularity [173, 174], which underlies the mixed continuous–discrete methods of [31, 85], with
certificates from greedy extreme bases [43, 63, 104].

Constraints, optimal sets and limits. Integrality of totally unimodular fibers is the Hoffman–
Kruskal theorem [97, 165], and marginal-preserving rounding under hard constraints is classical
[35, 73]; Remark 8.11 compares Section 8 with the linear programs of Bienstock and Muñoz [24].
Section 9.2 relates our description of the optimal set to Rosenberg’s face criterion and to tree
reparameterizations [161, 183, 187]. Diagonal Lagrangian certificates for box QP are given in
[15, 106, 125], and exactness of relaxations of box QP is characterized in [159]; sparse second-order
cone and semidefinite formulations are exact under graph conditions [58, 111, 168], and chordal
sparsity guarantees positive semidefinite completions [81, 176]. Marginals that agree on separators
glue into a joint measure on tree-structured families [180, 182], but locally consistent moments
need not [166, 182], and complete parametric descriptions can have exponentially many pieces
[77, 131, 143].




                                                  8
3     Problem class, decompositions and conditioning
3.1    Model
We study
                                               X                           n
                                                                           Y
                               min F (x) =           fa (xSa ),      X=          Xi ,                  (3.1)
                               x∈X
                                             a∈A                           i=1

where Xi = [ℓi , ui ] for i ∈ IC (continuous coordinates) and Xi = [ℓi , ui ] ∩ Z for i ∈ IZ (integer
coordinates). Integer endpoints are rounded inward, an empty domain makes the problem infeasible,
and a coordinate with ℓi = ui is substituted out; so we assume ℓi < ui for all i, with ℓi , ui ∈ Z
for i ∈ IZ . We write si = ui − ℓi , s = maxi si , and X̄ = i [ℓi , ui ] for the continuous hull of X.
                                                            Q

Each factor fa depends only on the coordinates in its scope Sa ⊆ [n] = {1, . . . , n} and is defined
and continuous on the projection of X̄, including fractional values of integer coordinates. We write
F ∗ = minX F , S for the set of global minimizers, and Si = {xi : x ∈ S} for its projection on
coordinate i.

Tree decompositions. A tree decomposition of the scopes is a tree T with node set T , N = |T |,
and bags Bt ⊆ [n] such that every scope Sa is contained in some bag and, for each coordinate i, the
tree nodes whose bags contain i form a nonempty connected subtree. Each factor is assigned to one
bag containing its scope and each coordinate to one home bag containing it. We write p = maxt |Bt |
for the maximum bag size, so the width of the decomposition is p − 1. The decomposition is part of
the input; its validity is checked directly. A decomposition of width at most 2 tw + 1, where tw is
the treewidth of the interaction graph, can be computed in time 2O(tw) n [118], and good heuristic
decompositions are standard [28]. No bound on the number of bags containing a coordinate is
assumed.

Encoding. For complexity statements the data are rational: box endpoints, and factors given
as explicit lists of monomials with rational coefficients. The input length I is the binary length of
these data, including the decomposition and the assignment of factors to bags. Running times are
counted in bit operations, and a target accuracy is written 2−q with q ∈ Z≥0 .

3.2    Coordinate curvature
The certificate of Section 4 needs only one-dimensional upper curvature information.

Definition 3.1 (Coordinate curvature). Numbers L1 , . . . , Ln ≥ 0 are upper coordinate curvatures of
F if, for every i and every x ∈ X̄, the function t 7→ F (x + tei ) − L2i t2 is concave on {t : x + tei ∈ X̄}.
We put P = {i : Li > 0}, nP = |P |, L = maxi Li , and
                                               X
                                     ∥d∥2L =         Li d2i       (d ∈ Rn ).
                                               i∈P

    The condition concerns the sum F , not the individual factors, and it must hold on the continuous
hull, including fractional points between integer values. If F is twice differentiable it says ∂ii F ≤ Li
on X̄. For a quadratic F (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c the smallest valid choice is Li = max{Hii , 0}; the
off-diagonal entries of H are unrestricted, so F can be far from convex. For explicit polynomials,
valid rational bounds follow from interval evaluation of ∂ii F over X̄ (Lemma 6.20). A coordinate
with Li = 0 is concave along its own direction, so replacing xi by the better of ℓi and ui never
increases F ; this classical endpoint property [53, 161] is the case of zero correction below.

                                                         9
3.3    Quadratic growth and conditioning
The running-time analysis uses a growth condition at a minimizer or at the set of minimizers.

Definition 3.2 (Growth and condition numbers). For L′ ≥ 0 and g ′ > 0 put κ(L′ , g ′ ) =
max{1, L′ /g ′ }. Problem (3.1) has

  (a) point growth with constant g > 0 if there is x∗ ∈ X with

                                    F (x) − F (x∗ ) ≥ g∥x − x∗ ∥2           for all x ∈ X;                          (3.2)

       then x∗ is the unique minimizer, and we put κ = κ(L, g);

 (b) weighted growth with constant γ > 0 if there is a minimizer x∗ with

                                     F (x) − F ∗ ≥ γ∥x − x∗ ∥2L           for all x ∈ X,                            (3.3)

       and we put κ̄ = κ(1, γ) = max{1, 1/γ};

  (c) set growth with constant gS > 0 if

                                    F (x) − F ∗ ≥ gS dist(x, S)2           for all x ∈ X,                           (3.4)

       and we put κS = κ(L, gS ).

    Quadratic growth refers to any of these conditions. The constants are not unique, and a larger
constant never gives a larger condition number. Each condition number is defined from a valid
constant: a result that assumes growth holds for every valid constant, and a statement that an
instance has, for example, κ ≤ c means that some valid constant gives this bound. If a condition
holds, its largest valid constant is the infimum of the ratio of F (x) − F ∗ to ∥x − x∗ ∥2 , ∥x − x∗ ∥2L or
dist(x, S)2 , respectively, over the x ∈ X where this denominator is positive (every positive constant
is valid if there is no such x); for weighted growth it does not depend on x∗ , because (3.3) forces
x′P = x∗P for every minimizer x′ . So each condition number has a smallest value, determined by
the instance and the curvature bounds. As a parameter of the instance, κ̄ denotes this value,
max{1, 1/γ ∗ } with γ ∗ the largest valid γ (κ̄ = 1 if P = ∅).1 Distances and ∥·∥ are Euclidean.
    Point growth with constant g is set growth with constant g and S = {x∗ }, so κS = κ. Since
∥d∥L ≤ L∥d∥2 , it also implies weighted growth, with constant g/L if L > 0 (then κ̄ = κ) and with
    2

every constant if L = 0. So κ̄ can always be taken at most κ, and it has two further advantages.
It does not change when a continuous coordinate is rescaled, xi = αyi , because Li is multiplied
by α2 and Li (xi − x∗i )2 is unchanged; for a separable quadratic i L2i (xi − ci )2 with all Li > 0 on
                                                                     P

a continuous box containing c, the largest constants give κ̄ = 2 and κ = 2 maxi Li / mini Li . And
coordinates with Li = 0 need no growth at all: minimizers may differ outside P .
    The conditions are global and imposed only on the feasible set X. They do not require convexity:
F may have many local minima, provided each lies above F ∗ by at least a fixed multiple of its
squared distance from x∗ (Example 5.12); Remark 3.4 below states what growth restricts for box QPs.
When integer coordinates are present, nearly tied integer assignments make the growth constants
small even if F is strongly convex in the continuous coordinates. For quadratics, uniqueness of the
minimizer implies point growth for some g > 0 (Lemma 6.12), and set growth always holds for some
gS > 0 (Remark 6.10). Neither fact bounds the condition number, which can be exponential in I
   1
     Our κ is unrelated to the integer κ in [53, Lemma 20] (a result of [110]): the least number such that every principal
submatrix of order n − κ of the Hessian is positive semidefinite.


                                                           10
even for n = 2. The function F = (x1 − x2 )2 + 2−k x21 on [0, 1]2 has input length O(k) and the unique
minimizer 0. Valid curvature bounds satisfy L1 ≥ 2 + 21−k and L2 ≥ 2, and F (t, t) = 2−k t2 , so
every valid constant satisfies g ≤ 2−k−1 and γ ≤ 2−k /(L1 + L2 ) < 2−k−2 . Hence κ ≥ L2 /g ≥ 2k+2
and κ̄ > 2k+2 . Our algorithms use the curvature bounds explicitly; they never need g, γ, gS or x∗ ,
and the validity of every bound they return depends only on Definition 3.1.
    For box QPs, the next lemma certifies growth from the gradient and the Hessian at a point, and
shows what minimality and growth imply for the Hessian. For a point x∗ ∈ X let J0 = {i ∈ IC :
ℓi < x∗i < ui } be its continuous coordinates strictly inside their bounds and J∂ = {i : x∗i ∈ {ℓi , ui }}
its coordinates at a bound.

Lemma 3.3 (Growth certificates for box QPs). Let F = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c be a quadratic on a
mixed box X, x∗ ∈ X and ζ = ∇F (x∗ ).

 (a) Suppose that ζi = 0 for i ∈ J0 , and that for every i ∈ IC \ J0 the gradient points inward:
     ζi ≥ 0 if x∗i = ℓi and ζi ≤ 0 if x∗i = ui . Let M = diag(µ) with µi = 0 for i ∈ J0 , µi = |ζi |/si
     for i ∈ J∂ with inward ζi , and µi = −|ζi | for the remaining coordinates, which are integer
     coordinates. Then

                        F (x) − F (x∗ ) ≥ 21 (x − x∗ )T (H + 2M )(x − x∗ )      (x ∈ X).               (3.5)

      Consequently, if H + 2M ⪰ 2g0 I for some g0 > 0, then x∗ is the unique minimizer and F has
      point growth with constant g0 .

 (b) Conversely, let x∗ be a minimizer. Then ζJ0 = 0 and HJ0 J0 ⪰ 0. If F has point growth
     with constant g, then HJ0 J0 ⪰ 2gI; if it has weighted growth with constant γ at x∗ , then
     HJ0 J0 ⪰ 2γ diag(Li )i∈J0 . In particular, under point growth g ≤ 21 v T Hv/∥v∥2 for every
     nonzero v supported on J0 , and if J0 ̸= ∅, every valid curvature bound satisfies L ≥ 2g, so
     κ ≥ 2. If J0 = [n], then F is convex, and strongly convex under point growth.

Proof. (a) Let x ∈ X and d = x − x∗ . Since F is quadratic, F (x) − F (x∗ ) = ζ T d + 12 dT Hd exactly,
and it suffices to show ζi di ≥ µi d2i for every i. For i ∈ J0 both sides vanish. If x∗i = ℓi and ζi ≥ 0,
then 0 ≤ di ≤ si , so ζi di = |ζi |di ≥ |ζi |d2i /si = µi d2i ; the case x∗i = ui , ζi ≤ 0 is symmetric. For
the remaining i ∈ IZ , di ∈ Z and |di | ≤ d2i , which gives ζi di ≥ −|ζi ||di | ≥ −|ζi |d2i = µi d2i . If
H + 2M ⪰ 2g0 I, the right side of (3.5) is at least g0 ∥d∥2 , which is positive for d ̸= 0.
    (b) For i ∈ J0 both directions ±ei are feasible from x∗ , so minimality gives ζi = 0. Let v ̸= 0 be
                                                                                               2
supported on J0 . For all sufficiently small |t|, x∗ + tv ∈ X and F (x∗ + tv) − F ∗ = t2 v T Hv. This is
nonnegative by minimality; point growth bounds it from below by gt2 ∥v∥2 , and weighted growth
by γt2 ∥v∥2L = γt2 i∈J0 Li vi2 ; this gives the three matrix inequalities. Taking v = ei with i ∈ J0
                    P

gives Hii ≥ 2g under point growth, and every valid curvature bound satisfies L ≥ Li ≥ Hii , because
t 7→ F (x + tei ) − Li t2 /2 has second derivative Hii − Li . If J0 = [n], then H = HJ0 J0 .

Remark 3.4 (What growth restricts). For a box QP, the block HJ0 J0 at a minimizer is positive
semidefinite (Lemma 3.3(b)), so at every minimizer, with or without growth, every direction of
negative curvature involves active bounds or integer coordinates. Point growth with some g > 0
is equivalent to uniqueness of the minimizer (Lemma 6.12), so what restricts the instances is the
size of the constants. Point growth gives HJ0 J0 ⪰ 2gI, hence κ ≥ 2L/λmin (HJ0 J0 ) if J0 ̸= ∅, and
weighted growth gives HJ0 J0 ⪰ 2γ diag(Li )i∈J0 . For every x′ ∈ X with F (x′ ) > F ∗ , (3.2) gives
κ ≥ L∥x′ − x∗ ∥2 /(F (x′ ) − F ∗ ) and (3.3) gives κ̄ ≥ ∥x′ − x∗ ∥2L /(F (x′ ) − F ∗ ). Since ∥d∥2L ≤ L∥d∥2 ,
nearly optimal points far from x∗ in the norm ∥·∥L make both condition numbers large. Within
these conditions an instance can have an indefinite Hessian, κ ≤ 21      4 and exponentially many strict


                                                     11
local minima (Example 5.12). Part (a) certifies growth when the first-order terms ζi di at active
bounds supply the growth that the Hessian lacks. At integer coordinates it uses integrality only to
bound ζi di below by −|ζi |d2i .

4     Corrected coordinate grids
This section shows how a finite dynamic program yields a valid lower bound for the continuous
mixed-integer problem, and how its coordinate min-marginals remove parts of the domain without
losing any global minimizer. Only Definition 3.1 is used: no convexity, uniqueness or growth.




                                                      12
4.1    Corrected grids and cell bounds
A subbox is a product X ′ = i Xi′ ⊆ X with Xi′ = [ℓ′i , u′i ] for i ∈ IC and Xi′ = [ℓ′i , u′i ] ∩ Z with
                                  Q

integers ℓ′i , u′i for i ∈ IZ , where ℓ′i ≤ u′i . A grid for X ′ is a product G = i Gi of finite sets Gi ⊆ Xi′
                                                                                 Q

that contain ℓ′i and u′i . The elements of Gi are nodes, and |Gi | is the number of nodes per coordinate.
Consecutive nodes a < a′ of Gi form a grid interval J = [a, a′ ]; if ℓ′i = u′i , the single node a forms
the grid interval [a, a]. The effective width of a grid interval J = [a, a′ ] of coordinate i is w(J) = 0 if
i ∈ IZ and a′ − a = 1, and w(J) = a′ − a otherwise; an integer interval of length one has no feasible
point in its interior.

Definition 4.1 (Corrections, bound and min-marginals). For v ∈ Gi let wi (v) be the largest
effective width of a grid interval with endpoint v. Put
                                                n
                   Li                           X
           di (v) = wi (v)2 ,         D(y) =          di (yi ),     Q(y) = F (y) − D(y)         (y ∈ G),   (4.1)
                   8                            i=1

                  β = min Q(y),           mi (v) = min{Q(y) : y ∈ G, yi = v} (v ∈ Gi ).                    (4.2)
                       y∈G

The function mi is the min-marginal of Q in coordinate i.

   The corrections are unary, so Q has the same scopes as F and the same tree decomposition
                                           Q
applies to it. A cell of G is a product C = i Ji of grid intervals Ji of Gi ; its cell bound is
                                            X Li                                Y
             β(C) =      min      F (v) −           w(Ji )2 ,       vert(C) =       {endpoints of Ji }.

Subtracting L2i (xi − mid Ji )2 in every coordinate with w(Ji ) > 0 makes F concave along each of
these coordinates, so its minimum over C ∩ X is attained at a vertex, and β(C) is a lower bound
for F on C ∩ X. For a single cell this is the vertex bound of Bajaj and Hasan [12], who evaluate
at the vertices the edge-concave underestimator of Hasan [89], built from an upper bound on the
diagonal Hessian entries; we use one bound Li per coordinate. The term L8i w(Ji )2 is the maximum
separation of the αBB underestimator with αi = Li /2 [2, 134], used with the opposite sign. The
following observation shows that the unary correction (4.1) computes the best of these bounds over
all cells of a product grid at once.

Proposition 4.2 (Cellwise form of the corrected grid). Let G be a grid for a subbox X ′ .

 (a) For every cell C and every x ∈ C ∩ X, F (x) ≥ β(C). Every x ∈ X ′ lies in some cell.

 (b) β = minC β(C), the minimum over all cells. In particular β ≤ minX ′ F .

 (c) For a grid interval J = [a, a′ ] of coordinate i, put βi (J) = min{β(C) : Ci = J}. Then

   Enumerating the cells and their up to 2n vertices is the box-by-box computation of branch-
and-bound. Since Q is factorized like F , Section 4.2 instead computes β and all min-marginals by
dynamic programming on the given tree decomposition.

Families of intervals. The proof of Proposition 4.2 uses only that each grid interval has endpoints
in Gi and that every node is an endpoint of one. The proposition therefore holds for finite sets
Gi ⊆ Xi and, in place of the grid intervals, finite families Ji of intervals with these two properties,
with wi (v) the largest effective width of a member with endpoint v, cells the products of members,
and X ′ replaced by X ∩ i Ji . The bounds of Lemma 9.3 for unions of uniform cells are an
                            Q S

instance.

Randomized rounding. The bound β ≤ F (x) for x ∈ X ′ also follows from randomized rounding.
Round each xi independently to an endpoint of a grid interval Ji ∋ xi , with mean xi , to obtain a
grid point Y . Jensen’s inequality for the concave functions t 7→ F (. . . , t, . . . ) − L2i t2 , one coordinate
at a time, gives EF (Y ) ≤ F (x) + i L2i Var(Yi ), and L2i Var(Yi ) ≤ L8i w(Ji )2 ≤ di (Yi ) (Yi = xi if
                                     P

w(Ji ) = 0), so EQ(Y ) ≤ F (x). Section 8 uses a correlated version of this rounding that keeps
coupling constraints satisfied (Lemma 8.3).

4.2    Dynamic programming and min-marginals
Let qt be the sum of the factors assigned to bag t minus the corrections di of the coordinates whose
                                              Q
home bag is t, regarded as a table on GBt = i∈Bt Gi ; its entries are the table entries of bag t, and
Q = t qt on G. For adjacent tree nodes t, u let Stu = Bt ∩ Bu and define messages
     P

                                                    h             X                      i
                           Mt→u (yStu ) = min qt (yBt ) +                    Mv→t (ySvt ) ,                (4.3)
                                         yBt \Stu
                                                               v∈N (t)\{u}

where N (t) is the set of neighbors of t in T and an empty separator carries a single scalar. Put
         P
At = qt + v∈N (t) Mv→t .

                                                          14
Lemma 4.3 (Two-pass dynamic programming). For every bag t and every yBt ∈ GBt , At (yBt ) =
min{Q(z) : z ∈ G, zBt = yBt }. Hence β = min At , and mi (v) is the minimum of At over yi = v
for any bag t ∋ i. With K = max        |G |, all messages, β, a minimizer and all min-marginals are
                                    i i
obtained with O (|A| + n + N )K p additions       and comparisons of table entries and O(p) index work
                                              p
                                                
per table entry, hence O p(|A| + n + N )K operations, plus the evaluation of every factor at the at
most K |Sa | grid points of its scope.
Proof. For an oriented edge t → u let Tt→u be the component of T \ {tu} containing t, and Wt→u
the set of coordinates occurring in bags of Tt→u but not in Stu . By induction on |Tt→u |, Mt→u (yStu )
is the minimum of t′ ∈Tt→u qt′ over yWt→u : the trees Tv→t , v ∈ N (t) \ {u}, partition Tt→u \ {t}, and
                   P

by running intersection a coordinate occurring both in Tv→t and outside it lies in Svt , so the sets
Wv→t are disjoint from each other and from Bt and the minimization splits. The same argument
with all neighbors of t gives the formula for At . Messages are computed toward a root and back;
at bag t the sum of all incoming messages is formed once, and each outgoing message is obtained
from At − Mu→t by one pass, with no product over children. A minimizer is recovered from the
root outward.

    Lemma 4.3 is classical nonserial dynamic programming, bucket elimination or junction-tree
computation [20, 50, 123], and two passes give all min-marginals [113, 183]. The only new element
is that its input is the corrected objective Q. Any exact method for β, a minimizer and the
min-marginals can replace the messages; Section 7.5 uses minimum cuts for balanced quadratics
without a width bound.

4.3    Filtering and certificates
Definition 4.4 (Filtering step). Let G be a grid for a subbox X ′ with corrected minimum β, and
let U ≥ β. For i ∈ P , retain a grid interval [a, a′ ] of coordinate i if

                                        min{mi (a), mi (a′ )} ≤ U,                                      (4.4)

and let Xi′′ be the smallest interval containing the retained intervals (intersected with Z for i ∈ IZ ).
      / P let Xi′′ = Xi′ . The filtered subbox is X ′′ = i Xi′′ .
For i ∈
                                                        Q

Proposition 4.5 (Safe filtering). Every x ∈ X ′ \ X ′′ satisfies F (x) > U . Hence X ′′ contains every
x ∈ X ′ with F (x) ≤ U , and every minimizer of F over X ′ if U ≥ minX ′ F . Every minimizer y of
Q over G lies in X ′′ ; in particular X ′′ is a subbox.
Proof. If x ∈ X ′ \ X ′′ , some i ∈ P has xi ∈
                                             / Xi′′ , so every grid interval containing xi was not retained,
and Proposition 4.2(c) gives F (x) ≥ min{mi (a), mi (a′ )} > U for such an interval. For a minimizer
y of Q and i ∈ P , mi (yi ) ≤ Q(y) = β ≤ U , and yi is an endpoint of a grid interval of coordinate
i, which is therefore retained. So yi ∈ Xi′′ for i ∈ P , and trivially for i ∈     / P . Hence each Xi′′ is
nonempty, and for i ∈ IZ its endpoints are nodes, hence integers.

   A method that filters repeatedly produces nested subboxes. The following certificate records
only what is needed to check the result.
Definition 4.6 (Path certificate). A path certificate consists of grids G(0) , . . . , G(k) , a number β and
                                                                (j)         (j)         (j)         (j)   (j)

are integers, X (0) = X, X (j+1) ⊆ X (j) , and, with Q(j) and m(j) computed from G(j) and the
curvature bounds,

                                                       (j)                              (j+1)         (j+1)
(C1) for j < k, every grid interval J = [a, a′ ] of Gi       that is not contained in [ℓi          , ui        ] satisfies
                                               (j)           (j)
                                          min mi (a), mi (a′ ) ≥ β,
                                (j)                                                 (j+1)      (j+1)
      or has w(J) = 0 and mi (v) ≥ β for each endpoint v of J outside [ℓi                   , ui          ];

(C2) minG(k) Q(k) ≥ β.

    The second alternative in (C1) covers filters that remove single integer nodes, such as the
integer-label filter of Lemma B.1.

Theorem 4.7 (Path certificates). If a path certificate is valid, then β ≤ F ∗ ≤ F (x̂). Its validity
is checked by at most k + 1 runs of the dynamic program of Lemma 4.3 in exact arithmetic, given
the curvature bounds; for a quadratic the checker computes Li = max{Hii , 0} itself. No growth or
uniqueness assumption is involved.

Proof. Let x ∈ X and let j be the largest index with x ∈ X (j) . If j = k, Proposition 4.2(b) and
                                               (j+1)              (j+1) (j+1)

                                                             / [ℓi     , ui   ], because xi is an integer if
                                 (j)                                                  (j+1) (j+1)

                                                 (j+1) (j+1)

                 (j)        (j)        (j)

Corollary 4.8 (Filtering runs give valid certificates). Let G(0) be a grid for X (0) = X and, for
j < k, let G(j+1) be a grid for the subbox X (j+1) obtained from G(j) by the filtering step with a
threshold Uj ≥ F ∗ , for example the value of a feasible point. Let β be the corrected minimum of
G(k) and x̂ ∈ X. Then every threshold satisfies the condition of Definition 4.4, every minimizer of
F lies in every X (j) , and (G(0) , . . . , G(k) , β, x̂) is a valid path certificate.

Proof. Let βj be the corrected minimum of G(j) . If every minimizer lies in X (j) , as it does in
X (0) = X, then Proposition 4.2(b) gives βj ≤ F ∗ ≤ Uj , so the filtering step is defined, and
Proposition 4.5 puts every minimizer in X (j+1) . By induction this holds for all j, and β = βk ≤ F ∗ .
Grids for subboxes have integer nodes in integer coordinates, X (j+1) ⊆ X (j) , and (C2) holds
                                                                 (j)                     (j+1) (j+1)
with equality. For (C1), let J = [a, a′ ] be a grid interval of Gi not contained in [ℓi        , ui  ].
                                                                                          (j+1) (j+1)
Coordinates outside P are not filtered, so i ∈ P , and J was not retained, since [ℓi           , ui   ]
                                                 (j)     (j) ′             ∗
contains every retained interval. Hence min{mi (a), mi (a )} > Uj ≥ F ≥ β.

   So a filtering run is certified by its stage grids (or the boxes with the centers, meshes and grading
that generate them), one bound and one feasible point. Messages, min-marginal tables, intermediate
incumbents and stages other than the last that remove nothing need not be stored; the stages
that remove points are needed, because the last grid bounds F only on X (k) . A checker runs the
dynamic program once per recorded stage, the same table work as the filtering steps that produced
the certificate; Section 11 reports measured replay times.
   The certificate is a branch-and-bound proof whose tree is a path, in which filtering acts as
optimality-based bound tightening (Section 2); all interval bounds of a stage come from one pair of
message passes.




                                                      16
5     Accuracy-independent grids under quadratic growth
Theorem 4.7 holds for any choice
                             √ of grids. This section chooses them so that, under weighted growth,
every coordinate grid has O( κ̄ log(n + 2)) nodes at every refinement stage, independently of the
accuracy reached. The growth constant is used only in the analysis.

5.1     Graded grids and one trial
Definition 5.1 (Graded grid). Let Xi′ = [ℓ′i , u′i ] (or its integer points if i ∈ IZ ), a center ci ∈ Xi′ , a
mesh h > 0 and a grading 0 < θ ≤ 1/4 be given. Put

                   σ(t) = h + θt (i ∈ IC ),        σ(t) = max{1, ⌊h + θt⌋}       (i ∈ IZ ).              (5.1)

Starting from t0 = 0, set tk+1 = min{tk + σ(tk ), u′i − ci } until tk+1 = u′i − ci , and take the nodes
ci + tk ; proceed in the same way towards ℓ′i . These nodes form the graded grid of Xi′ .

   Spacing grows linearly with the distance from the center, so a grid of radius R needs only
O(1 + θ−1 log(1 + θR/h)) nodes.

Lemma 5.2 (Graded grids). (a) wi (v) ≤ h + θ|v − ci | for every node v. (b) If an endpoint is at
distance R > 0 from ci , the number of nodes strictly on that side is at most ⌈(4/θ) ln(1 + θR/ĥ)⌉,
where ĥ = h for i ∈ IC and ĥ = max{h, 1} for i ∈ IZ . (c) If h ≥ u′i − ℓ′i , the grid has at most three
nodes.

    The proof is in Appendix A. The meshes are chosen per coordinate so that every coordinate
sees the same correction scale. For i ∈ P let ϖi ∈ Z satisfy 4ϖi −1 < Li ≤ 4ϖi and put ri = 2−ϖi , so
that 14 < Li ri2 ≤ 1. Let E be the least integer with 2E ri ≥ si for all i ∈ P , and put ηj = 2E−j and
hij = ηj ri ; every hij is a power of two.

Algorithm 5.3 (TRIAL: one trial). The parameters are a grading θ ∈ (0, 1/4], a cap K, an accuracy
ε > 0 and a stage limit jmax . Start with X (0) = X, center c(0) = ℓ, and the incumbent x̂ = ℓ with
value U = F (ℓ), or a better feasible point and its value if one is known. For j = 0, 1, . . . , jmax :
                       (j)                             (j)               (j)
    (i) for i ∈ P let Gi be the graded grid of Xi with center ci , mesh hij and grading θ; for
                   (j)
        i∈/ P let Gi = {ℓi , ui }; if some grid would exceed K nodes, abort the trial before forming
        any table;

 (ii) compute βj , a minimizer y (j) of Q over G(j) and all min-marginals by Lemma 4.3; if F (y (j) ) < U ,
      set x̂ = y (j) and U = F (y (j) );

 (iii) if U − βj ≤ ε, stop with success; otherwise filter with threshold U (Definition 4.4) to obtain
       X (j+1) , and set c(j+1) = y (j) .

The trial fails if stage jmax ends without success.

   By Proposition 4.5 the centers stay feasible and inside the current subbox, and by Corollary 4.8
every stage yields a valid path certificate. The analysis allows any first center in X (Lemma 5.4(ii)
holds at j = 0 for every c(0) ∈ X). Later centers need to be close to x∗ : by Lemma 5.4(iii), the
corrected minimizer y (j) is as close to x∗ as Lemma 5.4(ii) requires of the center of stage j + 1.
Because the centers are ℓ and grid points, denominators do not compound from stage to stage
(Appendix A).

5.3   Unknown growth: capped trials
The algorithm cannot use 8κ̄θ2 ≤ 1 directly because γ is unknown. It tries geometrically decreasing
gradings and relies on the caps to bound the work of unsuitable trials.

Algorithm 5.6 (CT: capped trials). The input is an accuracy ε > 0. If P = ∅, minimize F over the
grid i {ℓi , ui } by Lemma 4.3 and return a minimizer x̂, β = F (x̂) and this grid. Otherwise let jmax
     Q

be the least integer jmax ≥ 0 with 169
                                       nP η02 4−jmax ≤ ε, and for µ = 2, 3, . . . run TRIAL with grading
      −µ                   −µ
θ = 2 , cap Kµ = K(2 , nP ), accuracy ε and stage limit jmax , keeping the incumbent from trial
to trial, until a trial succeeds. Return the incumbent x̂, the bound β = βj of the successful stage j
and the grids of the successful trial.

Theorem 5.7 (Certified approximation). Let (3.1) have explicit rational quadratic factors given as
monomial lists, a tree decomposition of maximum bag size p, curvature bounds Li = max{0, ∂ii F },
and let ε = 2−q .

      also holds with any larger number in place of κ̄, such as the Euclidean condition number κ

Remark 5.8 (In what sense the bound is fixed-parameter). As a parameter, κ̄ is the smallest weighted
condition number of the instance, which exists by Section 3. It is a real number, not part of the
input, and CT never computes it. Since f is increasing in t ≥ 1, the bound of Theorem 5.7(b) is
at most f (p, ⌈κ̄⌉)(I + q + 1)5 , a fixed-parameter bound in the parameters p and ⌈κ̄⌉, which the
algorithm does not need to know. The exponent 5 reflects schoolbook arithmetic and depends on
neither parameter, and the accuracy enters only through q = log2 (1/ε). The factor ⌈log2 (nP + 2)⌉p
is absorbed by (5.4), and no bound on the number of bags containing a coordinate, on Hessian norms
or on the number of local minima is used. If only a treewidth bound is known, the decomposition
of [118] gives the same result with p = 2 tw + 2. Theorem 1.2 states the lower bounds, proved in
Section 10; since κ̄ ≤ κ, the bounds stated for κ also apply to κ̄.

A common mesh. The implementation of Section 11 and the exact-output results of Section 6.5
and Appendices B.1 and B.2 use one mesh for all coordinates and point growth with the Euclidean
condition number κ.

5.4    Sharpness of the localization radius
The slack U − β can be as large as a sum of n correction terms, while a single coordinate interval is
removed only when its own excess exceeds the whole slack. The following family shows that the
         √
radius Θ( nκ hj ) of Lemmas 5.5 and 5.9 is therefore attained, so that filtering alone leaves
                                                                                       √      at least
        √
of order nκ nodes per coordinate on uniform grids; the graded grids reduce this to O( κ̄ log(n + 2))
(Lemma 5.5), which makes Theorem 5.7 a fixed-parameter bound (Remark 5.8).
Proposition 5.10 (Sharpness of localization). Let m ≥ 1, n = 2m, Λ > 0 and 0 < g ≤ Λ/2, and
on X = [−1, 1]n , with all coordinates continuous and named (u1 , v1 , . . . , um , vm ), let
                                        m h
                                        X   Λ                                 i
                              F (x) =             (u2k + vk2 ) + (Λ − 2g)uk vk .
                                        k=1
                                              2
Then x∗ = 0, F ∗ = 0, the smallest valid curvature bounds are Li = Λ, the largest growth constant is
g, and κ = Λ/g takes every value in [2, ∞). Let G be a grid for a subbox that contains [−R0 , R0 ]n ,
and let every Gi contain 0 with wi (0) ≥ h. Then for every incumbent value U ≥ 0, every coordinate
i and every node a ∈ Gi with
                                                  n      q               o
                                   |a| ≤ min R0 , h (n − 2)κ/16

we have mi (a) ≤ U . Hence every grid interval with such an endpoint is retained.

Corollary 5.11 (Filtered uniform grids need order nκ nodes). Take the instance of Proposi-

                √

√
  n per coordinate it is not a fixed-parameter bound.

5.5    Examples
The first example shows that the theorem covers nonconvex problems with many local minima and
treewidth larger than one.

                                           m points with (u , v , ω ) ∈ {(0, 0, 0), (1, 1, 1 )} in all m
                                                            b b    b                       2
blocks is a strict local minimizer; (d) a tree decomposition of G of width w yields one of F with
p = max{w + 1, 3}. Each block Hessian is indefinite and the minimizer is not a vertex. For G
an m1 × m2 grid graph with m = m1 m2 ≥ 2, Theorem 5.7 applies with p = max{m1 + 1, 3} and
κ̄ ≤ κ ≤ 21                              m
          4 , although the instance has 2 strict local minima. The proof is in Appendix A.

   The minimizer of this family is easy to guess block by block; by (a), F − F ∗ is at least 98 times
the number of blocks at (0, 0, 0). The second example separates the grids from exact parametric
dynamic programming.
Example 5.13 (Expanding-box chain). The family of Proposition 10.7, a chain of length m whose
tth state has a box of width 2t − 1, has p = 3, a unique minimizer and κ ≤ 80 for every m. By
Theorem 5.7, a certified 2−q -approximation takes time polynomial in m and q, whereas the exact
Bellman message for the last state has at least 2m−1 quadratic pieces. Rescaling the tth state by 2−t
moves every coordinate into [0, 1] and does not change κ̄, which stays at most 80, but the rescaled
instance has L = 4m+1 and a largest growth constant between 18 and 20, so every valid growth
constant gives κ ≥ 4m /5, and the largest gives κ ≤ 32 · 4m (Appendix A). Section 11 reports the
certified computation up to m = 64.


6     Exact output
A certified gap below 2−q is not an exact solution. For rational quadratics, a global minimizer
and the optimal value have bounded rational height, so a sufficiently small certified gap can be
converted into an exact minimizer that is checked against the original data. This section proves a
height bound for minimizers and for the optimal value (Section 6.2), gives an acceptance rule that
needs neither uniqueness nor growth, and shows that an exact minimizer can be recovered from
any feasible point close to the set of minimizers (Section 6.3). Under point growth this yields exact
output in f1 (p, κ) poly(I) bit operations (Theorem 6.14). Section 6.5 gives an acceptance test that
uses the filtering history, and Section 6.6 treats explicit polynomial factors. Several minimizers are
treated in Section 9.

6.1    Scaled data
In this section, except in Section 6.6, F is a quadratic with rational data,

                                      F (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c,

where H is the symmetric Hessian, on a bounded mixed box X = i Xi with rational endpoints,
after fixed coordinates have been substituted and integer endpoints rounded inward; thus ℓi < ui

                                                  22
for every i. The curvature bounds are Li = max{Hii , 0}, so P = {i : Hii > 0}. Let ∆ be a positive
common denominator of the monomial coefficients of F , namely Hii /2, Hij (i < j), bi and c, and of
all endpoints ℓi , ui . Put

The matrix Ĥ is integral and symmetric, since Ĥii = 2∆(Hii /2) and Ĥij = ∆Hij ; this choice of ∆
absorbs the factor two of the off-diagonal entries. Each Ĥii with i ∈ IC+ is a positive integer, and
Ĥii = ∆Li ≤ ∆L, so log2 R ≤ log2 ∆ + |IC+ | log2 (∆L) is polynomial in I. Every positive Hii is an
integer multiple of 2/∆, so L ≥ 2/∆ if L > 0. The numbers R, Ω and τ are computed from the
original data; refined boxes never enter them.
     Two elementary facts are used repeatedly. If every coordinate of x ∈ X lies in ρ−1 Z for a positive
integer ρ, then ∆ρ2 F (x) ∈ Z, because ∆ clears every monomial coefficient. If a/W ̸= a′ /W ′ are
rationals with positive denominators, then |a/W − a′ /W ′ | ≥ 1/(W W ′ ).

6.2   Stationary polytopes and rational height
The argument follows Vavasis [177] (see also [55]); we need its explicit constants. For x ∈ X let
J0 (x) = {i ∈ IC : ℓi < xi < ui } be its interior coordinates.

Definition 6.2 (Stationary polytope). For x◦ ∈ S let

       P(x◦ ) = x ∈ Rn : xi = x◦i (i ∈
                                     / J0 (x◦ )), ℓi ≤ xi ≤ ui and ∂i F (x) = 0 (i ∈ J0 (x◦ )) .
                


Lemma 6.3 (Stationary polytopes). Let x◦ ∈ S and J0 = J0 (x◦ ).

 (a) ∇J0 F (x◦ ) = 0 and HJ0 J0 ⪰ 0.

 (b) P(x◦ ) is a nonempty polytope contained in X, and P(x◦ ) ⊆ S.

 (c) Let v be a vertex of P(x◦ ) and Jv = J0 (v). Then Jv ⊆ J0 ∩ IC+ , ĤJv Jv ≻ 0, every coordinate
     of v lies in ρ−1 Z for ρ = ∆ det ĤJv Jv , and ∆ ≤ ρ ≤ R (with det ĤJv Jv = 1 if Jv = ∅).

Proof. (a) For w ∈ Rn supported on J0 , the point x◦ + tw lies in X for all small |t|, because
the coordinates in J0 are continuous and strictly inside their bounds. Since F (x◦ + tw) = F ∗ +
                2
t∇F (x◦ )T w + t2 wT Hw has a minimum at t = 0, we get ∇F (x◦ )T w = 0 and wT Hw ≥ 0.
    (b) By (a), x◦ ∈ P(x◦ ). The set is defined by finitely many linear equations and inequalities
inside a bounded box, so it is a polytope. Its points keep the integer coordinates of x◦ and satisfy
all bounds, so P(x◦ ) ⊆ X. For x ∈ P(x◦ ), d = x − x◦ is supported on J0 , and HJ0 J0 dJ0 =
∇J0 F (x) − ∇J0 F (x◦ ) = 0. Hence

            F (x) − F (x◦ ) = ∇F (x◦ )T d + 12 dT Hd = ∇J0 F (x◦ )T dJ0 + 12 dT
                                                                              J0 HJ0 J0 dJ0 = 0,

so x ∈ S.
   (c) A continuous coordinate outside J0 is fixed at x◦i ∈ {ℓi , ui } in P(x◦ ), so Jv ⊆ J0 . Suppose
w ∈ RJv is nonzero and HJ0 Jv w = 0. Extend w by zero to w̃ ∈ Rn . Then v ± tw̃ ∈ P(x◦ ) for small
t > 0: the equations are preserved, the coordinates in Jv have slack, and all other coordinates are

                                                    23
unchanged. This contradicts that v is a vertex, so HJ0 Jv has trivial kernel. The matrix HJv Jv is a
principal submatrix of HJ0 J0 ⪰ 0; if w ∈ RJv \ {0} had wT HJv Jv w = w̃T H w̃ = 0, then HJ0 J0 w̃J0 = 0,
that is HJ0 Jv w = 0. Hence HJv Jv ≻ 0 and ĤJv Jv = ∆HJv Jv ≻ 0. In particular Hii > 0 for i ∈ Jv , so
Jv ⊆ IC+ .
    The coordinates of v outside Jv are integers or endpoints, so they lie in ∆−1 Z. The rows i ∈ Jv
of ∇F (v) = 0, multiplied by ∆2 , read

                             ĤJv Jv (∆vJv ) = −∆(∆bJv ) −           ĤJv j (∆vj ),
                                                             j ∈J

whose right-hand side is integral. Cramer’s rule gives ∆vJv ∈ (det ĤJv Jv )−1 ZJv , so every coordinate
of v lies in ρ−1 Z. Finally det ĤJv Jv is a positive integer, and by Lemma 6.1 and Jv ⊆ IC+ ,
