             Q            Q
                               C


Corollary 6.4 (Rational height). (a) Some global minimizer has all coordinates in ρ−1 Z for an
integer ρ with ∆ | ρ and ρ ≤ R; every isolated global minimizer has this property. The endpoints
ℓi , ui then also lie in ρ−1 Z. (b) F ∗ = a/W in lowest terms with 1 ≤ W ≤ Ω.
Proof. (a) Take any x◦ ∈ S. The nonempty polytope P(x◦ ) has a vertex, which is a global minimizer
by Lemma 6.3(b) and has the stated height by Lemma 6.3(c). If x◦ is isolated in S, then the convex
set P(x◦ ) ⊆ S contains no other point near x◦ , hence equals {x◦ }, and x◦ is its vertex. The endpoints
lie in ∆−1 Z ⊆ ρ−1 Z. (b) Evaluate F at the minimizer of (a): ∆ρ2 F ∗ ∈ Z, so W ≤ ∆ρ2 ≤ Ω.

Remark 6.5 (Other admissible height constants). Any bound on the minors of Ĥ restricted to
IC also works in Lemma 6.3(c) and Corollary 6.4, for example |IC |! max{1, maxij |Ĥij |}|IC | by the
Leibniz expansion, or i∈IC max{1, j∈IC |Ĥij |} by Hadamard’s inequality for rows. Both also
                        Q             P

bound nonprincipal minors, which arise if a vertex of P(x◦ ) is described by a nonprincipal set of
stationarity rows. Part (c) shows that the principal system ĤJv Jv always suffices, and Lemma 6.1
then gives the diagonal bound in (6.1), which is never larger than either alternative and depends
only on the coordinate curvatures. All these constants have polynomial bit length; only the threshold
values below change.

6.3    Acceptance and recovery
Proposition 6.6 (Exact acceptance). Let F attain its minimum F ∗ over the feasible set X, and let
F ∗ be a rational number with denominator at most Ω′ . Let β ≤ F ∗ and let x ∈ X with F (x) = a/W
in lowest terms. If F (x) − β < 1/(Ω′ W ), then x is a global minimizer. For the quadratics of this
section, Ω′ = Ω is admissible by Corollary 6.4(b).
Proof. If F (x) > F ∗ , then F (x) − F ∗ ≥ 1/(Ω′ W ) by the second elementary fact, because the
denominator of F ∗ is at most Ω′ . With β ≤ F ∗ this contradicts F (x) − β < 1/(Ω′ W ).

    The rule uses the denominator of the candidate itself. It does not assume that feasible values lie
on a lattice, which is false when continuous coordinates vary. The proof uses only the rationality of
F (x) and the denominator bound for F ∗ ; Sections 7 and 8 apply the proposition with other feasible
sets and other values of Ω′ .
Algorithm 6.7 (REC: snapping recovery). The input is a point y ∈ X. Fix every integer coordinate
at yi . For every continuous coordinate, fix it at ℓi if yi − ℓi ≤ τ , else at ui if ui − yi ≤ τ , and
otherwise call it free; let Jf be the set of free coordinates. Find by exact linear programming a point
x̃ that agrees with the fixed values and satisfies ℓJf ≤ x̃Jf ≤ uJf and ∇Jf F (x̃) = 0, or report failure.

                                                   24
    The data of this linear system are original coefficients, endpoints and integer values of y, so it is
solved in time polynomial in I [82, Theorem 6.4.12]. If HJf Jf is nonsingular, Gaussian elimination
suffices.
Lemma 6.8 (Snapping recovery). If y ∈ X and dist(y, S) ≤ τ /2, then REC(y) does not fail, and
every point it can return lies in S.
Proof. Let x◦ ∈ S be nearest to y; it exists since S is compact. As ∥y − x◦ ∥ ≤ τ /2 < 1, the integer
coordinates of y and x◦ agree. The two endpoints of a continuous coordinate differ by at least
1/∆ ≥ 1/R = 4nτ > 2τ , so no coordinate is within τ of both endpoints. If x◦i = ℓi then yi − ℓi ≤ τ /2
and coordinate i is fixed at ℓi ; similarly for x◦i = ui . Hence every continuous coordinate at a bound
in x◦ is fixed at its value in x◦ , and Jf ⊆ J0 = J0 (x◦ ). Let J ′ = J0 \ Jf be the remaining fixed
coordinates, each fixed at an endpoint ξi ∈ {ℓi , ui }, and define on P(x◦ ) the linear function
                            X                          X                               X
                   ϕ(x) =           |xi − ξi | =                    (xi − ℓi ) +                    (ui − xi ) ≥ 0.
                            i∈J ′                  i∈J ′ , ξi =ℓi                  i∈J ′ , ξi =ui

For i ∈ J ′ we have |yi − ξi | ≤ τ and |x◦i − yi | ≤ τ /2, so ϕ(x◦ ) ≤ 32 |J ′ |τ ≤ 8R
                                                                                     3
                                                                                       < R1 . The minimum of
ϕ over the polytope P(x◦ ) is attained at a vertex v. By Lemma 6.3(c) the coordinates of v and all
endpoints lie in ρ−1 Z for an integer ρ ≤ R, so ϕ(v) ∈ ρ−1 Z. Since 0 ≤ ϕ(v) ≤ ϕ(x◦ ) < 1/R ≤ 1/ρ,
we get ϕ(v) = 0.
     Thus v ∈ P(x◦ ) ⊆ S satisfies vi = ξi for i ∈ J ′ , agrees with x◦ (hence with the fixed values of
REC) outside J0 , and has ∇Jf F (v) = 0 because Jf ⊆ J0 . So the system solved by REC is feasible.
If x̃ is any solution, then d = x̃ − v is supported on Jf and HJf Jf dJf = ∇Jf F (x̃) − ∇Jf F (v) = 0,
so F (x̃) − F (v) = ∇Jf F (v)T dJf + 12 dT
                                         Jf HJf Jf dJf = 0. Since x̃ ∈ X, it is a global minimizer. No
nonsingularity of HJf Jf is used.

Theorem 6.9 (From certified gaps to exact minimizers). Let β ≤ F ∗ be certified and y ∈ X.
 (a) If F has set growth (3.4) with constant gS and
                                                          ng τ2            1 o       n g       1 o
                                                               S                         S
                       F (y) − β ≤ εS := min                          ,        = min         ,    ,                   (6.2)
                                                                4         2Ω2         64n2 R2 2Ω2
      then REC(y) returns a point x̃ that passes the test of Proposition 6.6 with Ω′ = Ω.
 (b) Without any growth assumption there is ε0 > 0, depending on the instance, such that the
     conclusion of (a) holds whenever F (y) − β ≤ ε0 .
Proof. (a) gS dist(y, S)2 ≤ F (y) − F ∗ ≤ F (y) − β ≤ gS τ 2 /4, so Lemma 6.8 applies and x̃ ∈ S. Then
F (x̃) = F ∗ = a/W with W ≤ Ω, and F (x̃) − β ≤ F (y) − β ≤ 1/(2Ω2 ) < 1/(ΩW ). (b) The set
Y = {x ∈ X : dist(x, S) ≥ τ /2} is compact and F > F ∗ on it, so εY = minY F − F ∗ > 0 (put
εY = ∞ if Y = ∅). With ε0 = min{εY /2, 1/(2Ω2 )}, F (y) − β ≤ ε0 gives dist(y, S) < τ /2, and the
argument of (a) applies.

Remark 6.10 (Set growth is automatic). Every rational mixed box QP has set growth with some
constant gS > 0. On an integer slice whose continuous box contains a minimizer, Luo and
Sturm’s error bound for a quadratic on a polytope [129, Theorem 3.3], applied to F − F ∗ , gives
F (x) − F ∗ ≥ c dist(x, S ∩ slice)2 ≥ c dist(x, S)2 ; on the finitely many other slices F − F ∗ is
bounded below by a positive constant while dist(x, S) is bounded by the diameter of X̄. As for
point growth, no useful lower bound on gS follows. Theorem 6.9 shows that exactness costs only
log2 (1/εS ) ≤ poly(I) + max{0, log2 (1/gS )} bits of certified accuracy; the cost of reaching that
accuracy is a property of the approximation algorithm.

                                                                    25
Remark 6.11 (Relation to rational-witness results). Corollary 6.4 is the box case, with explicit
constants, of a classical fact: a quadratic program with rational data that attains its minimum has
a global minimizer of polynomial encoding length [177]. Vavasis used it to show that the decision
version of quadratic programming is in NP, and Del Pia, Dey and Molinaro extended NP membership
to mixed-integer quadratic programs with unbounded integer variables [55]; on a bounded mixed
box the integer coordinates have polynomial length, so that extension is not needed. These results
certify F ∗ ≤ t. Deciding F ∗ ≤ t for box QP is NP-complete, and NP-hard already at treewidth two
[53], so certificates of F ∗ > t of polynomial size, checkable in polynomial time, for all instances
would place an NP-complete problem in coNP. The certificates of Theorem 6.14 below have size
f1 (p, κ) poly(I): they exist for bounded (p, κ), and they are checked without trusting growth. The
explicit constants R and Ω are what make the stopping test computable.

6.4   Exact output under point growth
Lemma 6.12 (Uniqueness implies growth). If S = {x∗ }, then F has point growth (3.2) with some
constant g > 0.

    For quadratic programs over polyhedra this is part of the second-order theory of [39]; the proof
below also covers integer coordinates.

Proof. Otherwise there are xk ∈ X \ {x∗ } with (F (xk ) − F ∗ )/∥xk − x∗ ∥2 → 0. As X̄ is bounded,
F (xk ) → F ∗ , and compactness of X with uniqueness gives xk → x∗ ; so the integer coordinates of
xk equal those of x∗ for large k, and since xk ̸= x∗ , they differ in some continuous coordinate. Put
tk = ∥xk − x∗ ∥ and uk = (xk − x∗ )/tk ; pass to a subsequence with uk → u, ∥u∥ = 1, uIZ = 0. Then

                             F (xk ) − F ∗   ∇F (x∗ )T uk  1
                                    2      =              + (uk )T Huk .
                                  tk             tk        2

Since x∗ minimizes F over the convex continuous box of its integer slice and xk lies in that box,
the first term is nonnegative. The left side tends to zero and the second term is bounded, so
∇F (x∗ )T u = 0 and uT Hu ≤ 0. Each uk satisfies uki ≥ 0 when x∗i = ℓi and uki ≤ 0 when x∗i = ui ; so
                                                                         2
does u, and hence x∗ + tu ∈ X for small t > 0. Then F (x∗ + tu) − F ∗ = t2 uT Hu ≤ 0, contradicting
uniqueness.

   As noted after Definition 3.2, the lemma gives no bound on the condition number, which can be
exponential in I.

Algorithm 6.13 (EX: exact output). For q = 1, 2, 4, 8, . . . : run CT(2−q ) (Algorithm 5.6) and
obtain a feasible x̂ and a certified β; run REC(x̂); if it returns x̃ with F (x̃) − β < 1/(ΩW ), where
W is the reduced denominator of F (x̃), stop and return x̃, F (x̃) and the path certificate of CT.

   If L = 0, then P = ∅, CT is exact and returns a vertex x̂ of the box with F (x̂) = β; REC fixes
every coordinate of a vertex, so x̃ = x̂ and the test passes at q = 1.

Theorem 6.14 (Exact rational output). For every rational mixed box QP with a supplied tree
decomposition, EX stops, and its output is a global minimizer and F ∗ , with a certificate whose
validity uses neither growth nor uniqueness. If the instance has point growth (3.2) with condition
number κ, EX uses at most f1 (p, κ)(I + 1)C1 bit operations, where f1 is a computable function and
C1 an absolute constant; neither g nor κ is an input. This bound remains valid if REC skips every
call whose matrix HJf Jf is singular, so under point growth no linear programming is needed.


                                                 26
Proof. Validity. Each returned point passes Proposition 6.6 with a certified β (Theorem 5.7), so it
is a global minimizer.
    Termination. CT stops on every instance (Theorem 5.7(a)). Once 2−q ≤ ε0 of Theorem 6.9(b),
the test passes.
    Bound under growth. If L = 0, EX stops after one call of CT. Otherwise L ≥ 2/∆. Point growth
is set growth with S = {x∗ } and gS = g. By Theorem 6.9(a), EX stops at the latest at the first q
with 2−q ≤ εS , so the largest q used is less than 2q ∗ with q ∗ = ⌈log2 (1/εS )⌉ ≥ 1. Since g ≥ L/κ
and log R, log Ω, log n and log(1/L) are at most polynomial in I, q ∗ ≤ poly(I) + log2 κ. There are
O(log q ∗ ) calls of CT, each within f (p, κ)(I + 2q ∗ + 1)5 bit operations by Theorem 5.7, whose bound
holds with κ ≥ κ̄, and (I + 2q ∗ + 1)5 ≤ (1 + log2 κ)5 poly(I). REC, the evaluation of F (x̃) and
the comparison cost a polynomial in I and in the bit lengths of x̂ and β. By Appendix A these
bit lengths are O((1 + µ2µ )(I + 2q ∗ + 1)), where CT succeeds at trial    √ µ (an√incumbent kept from
an earlier trial satisfies the smaller bound of that trial), and 2µ ≤ 6 κ̄ ≤ 6 κ by Theorem 5.7(b).
Absorbing all factors that depend only on κ into f1 proves the bound.
    Finally, let J0 = J0 (x∗ ). By Lemma 6.3(a), ∇J0 F (x∗ ) = 0, so a nonzero w supported on J0
with HJ0 J0 wJ0 = 0 would make F constant on a short segment through x∗ ; hence HJ0 J0 ≻ 0. If
dist(x̂, x∗ ) ≤ τ /2, then Jf ⊆ J0 as in the proof of Lemma 6.8, so HJf Jf is nonsingular. This holds
at every call that the bound uses, so skipping singular calls does not change it.

Remark 6.15 (Coordinatewise reconstruction). Under point growth one may instead round each
coordinate of x̂ to the unique rational of denominator at most R within 1/(4R2 ) (continued fractions,
[82, Theorem 5.1.9]); this succeeds once 2−q ≤ g/(32R4 ), by Corollary 6.4(a) and the 1/R2 separation
of such rationals. Snapping recovery succeeds once 2−q ≤ g/(64n2 R2 ) (Lemma 6.8), which is the
weaker requirement when R2 ≥ 2n2 ; it does not need uniqueness, and it returns a minimizer even
when S is a continuum. In both routes, 2−q ≤ 1/(2Ω2 ) makes the recovered minimizer pass the
acceptance test.

6.5    Localized acceptance
The acceptance rule of Proposition 6.6 needs a certified gap below 1/(ΩW ), as small as about 2−315
on the instances of Section 11.4, which have at most six variables. The filtering history permits
a different test that accepts a candidate as soon as the retained box is small compared with a
first-order margin.
     Consider stage j of a filtering run, such as a trial of CT: a grid G for the current box X (j) with

 (a) If i ∈ IZ , then xi lies in the set Zi of nodes v with mi (v) ≤ U and of integers strictly inside

     most F (x).

Proof. (a) If xi is a node v, Proposition 4.2(c) gives mi (v) ≤ mi (v) + di (v) ≤ F (x) ≤ U . Otherwise

after replaying the filtering history. Its validity needs neither uniqueness nor growth, and the
candidate x̃ is arbitrary. The corrected minimizer lies in X̃: for i ∈ IZ ∩ P , (yj )i is a node with
mi ((yj )i ) ≤ Q(yj ) = βj ≤ F ∗ ≤ U , and for i ∈
                                                 / P the same inequality excludes the endpoint whose
min-marginal exceeds U . The following rule computes a candidate from X̃ and yj .

Definition 6.18 (Face candidate). Put x̃i = (yj )i for i ∈ IZ and for i ∈          / J+ . For i ∈ IC ∩ J+ , put
x̃i = ℓi if ℓi ∈ X̃i , else x̃i = ui if ui ∈ X̃i , and otherwise call i free. If the set Jf of free coordinates
has HJf Jf nonsingular, the face candidate is the point x̃ whose free part solves ∇Jf F (x̃) = 0;
otherwise stage j has no face candidate.

    The face candidate costs one exact solve of a |Jf | × |Jf | linear system. It is tested by Proposi-
tion 6.17, whose hypotheses include x̃ ∈ X̃ and F (x̃) ≤ U .

Corollary 6.19 (Acceptance of the face candidate). Let F have point growth with constant g at
x∗ . Let J0 = J0 (x∗ ) and A = J∂ ∩ IC+ , the continuous coordinates with Li > 0 at which x∗ is at
a bound, and assume strict complementarity, ∂i F (x∗ ) ̸= 0 for i ∈ A. Put λA = mini∈A |∂i F (x∗ )|
and Γ = ∥HAA ∥2 + ∥HJ0 A ∥22 /(2g), and let δX be the smallest distance |x∗i − e| over coordinates
                                                                             √
                                                       ̸ x∗i . Put h∗ = ω ∗ / nκ, where ω ∗ is the smallest
i ∈ IC ∪ (IZ \ P ) and endpoints e ∈ {ℓi , ui } with e =
of the numbers 12 (if IZ ∩ P =
                             ̸ ∅), δX /6 (if IC ∪ (IZ \ P ) ̸= ∅) and λA /(5Γ) (if A ̸= ∅); at least one of
the first two conditions holds, because IZ ∩ P and IC ∪ (IZ \ P ) cover [n]. Consider a trial of CT with
a common mesh (Lemma 5.9) with 8κθ2 ≤ 1. At every stage j with hj ≤ h∗ that the trial reaches
and ends by filtering, the face candidate exists, equals x∗ , and passes the test of Proposition 6.17.
Since δX ≥ 1/R, λA ≥ 1/(∆R) and log2 Γ ≤ poly(I) + log2 κ, the least j with hj ≤ h∗ is at most
poly(I) + O(log κ).


                                                        28
    The proof is in Appendix B.1. It also shows that at these stages X̃i = {x∗i } for every integer
coordinate and every coordinate with Li = 0, and that Jf = J0 . With several minimizers the test
can fail, for example when two optimal vertices differ in a coordinate i ∈/ P.
    In experiment S1 (Section 11.4) the test accepted the face candidate within nine stages on 29
of 30 random mixed-integer instances, whereas one CT run needed up to 72 stages to reach the
threshold of Proposition 6.6 with the constants of (6.1). The idea of certifying a neighborhood of a
candidate by local information is that of exclusion boxes in interval branch-and-bound [148]; here it
is carried out exactly on the box produced by the filtering history.

6.6    Explicit polynomial factors
Now let each factor be an explicit list of rational monomials of degree at most d ≥ 2, with d fixed
(or encoded in unary).

Lemma 6.20 (Interval curvature certificate). For each i write ∂ii F = m cm xαm and let [µm , µm ]
                                                                           P

be the range of the monomial xαm on X̄. Put L̂i = m max{cm µm , cm µm } and Li = max{L̂i , 0}.
                                                    P

Then the Li are upper coordinate curvatures, and they are computed in time polynomial in I.

Proof. The monomial xα is a product of functions xαk k of distinct coordinates, so its range on the
box X̄ is the product of the intervals {tαk : t ∈ [ℓk , uk ]}, each of which is computed exactly from
ℓαk k , uαk k and, for even αk with ℓk < 0 < uk , the value 0. Hence cm xαm ≤ max{cm µm , cm µm } on X̄,
and ∂ii F ≤ L̂i ≤ Li . For every x ∈ X̄, t 7→ F (x + tei ) − L2i t2 has second derivative ∂ii F − Li ≤ 0
on its interval, so it is concave. The endpoints are powers of degree at most d of input rationals, so
their bit length is O(dI), and there are at most as many terms as input monomials.

    The bound can overestimate when monomials cancel; the condition number in the results below
is computed with the accepted L. Any sharper bound with a certificate checkable in polynomial
time may be used instead.

Proposition 6.21 (Lattice stopping rule). Suppose every coordinate is integer, and let ΓF be the
product of the denominators of the coefficients of the objective (after substituting fixed coordinates).
If x̂ ∈ X, β ≤ F ∗ and F (x̂) − β < 1/ΓF , then x̂ ∈ S. It suffices that F (x̂) − β ≤ 2−q with
q = 1 + ⌈log2 ΓF ⌉ = O(I).

Proof. At integer points every monomial value lies in Γ−1               −1                ∗
                                                       F Z, so F (X) ⊆ ΓF Z. If F (x̂) > F then
          ∗                                         ∗
F (x̂) − F ≥ 1/ΓF > F (x̂) − β, contradicting β ≤ F .

Corollary 6.22 (Polynomial factors). For fixed d, under point growth (3.2) with the accepted
curvature bounds, CT(2−q ) returns a certified 2−q -approximation in fd (p, κ)(I +q +1)C bit operations,
where fd is a computable function and C an absolute constant. If all coordinates are integer,
CT(2−(1+⌈log2 ΓF ⌉) ) returns an exact minimizer, certified by Proposition 6.21, in fd (p, κ)(I + 1)C bit
operations.

Proof. The interpolation, filtering, contraction, localization and grid-size arguments of Sections 4
and 5 use only Definition 3.1 and growth, not the quadratic form. In the bit analysis of Theorem 5.7
(Appendix A) only the exponents that define the meshes hij (Section 5) and the factor evaluation
change. The Li of Lemma 6.20 have O(dI) bits and lie in (ΓF Γd−2       −1
                                                                   X ) Z, because the coefficients
of ∂ii F are integer multiples of coefficients of F and the endpoints of the monomial ranges are
products of at most d − 2 box endpoints. So these exponents are O(dI) in absolute value, and
every node has a denominator dividing ΓX 2α , with α as in Appendix A but with O(dI) in place


                                                   29
of O(I). A monomial of degree at most d at such nodes has a value with denominator dividing
ΓF (ΓX 2α )d , and since d ≥ 2, so has 8 times a correction Li w2 /8. Hence factor values, corrections,
table entries and messages have the common denominator 8ΓF (ΓX 2α )d , and over it they are integers
of O(d(I + α)) bits. For fixed d these bit lengths exceed those of Appendix A by a constant factor,
and the evaluation of an explicit factor costs a polynomial in its length and these bit lengths. The
second statement follows from Proposition 6.21 with q = O(I).

Example 6.23 (Limits of the polynomial   √ 2 extension).
                                                   √        (a) F√(x) = x3 − 6x on [1, 2] has ∂xx F =
6x ≤ 12 = L̂ and F (x) − F √    ∗ = (x − 2) (x +√ 2 2) ≥ 3(x − 2)2 , so g = 3 is a growth constant
and κ ≤ 4; but its minimizer 2 and value −4 2 are irrational, so no rational exact output exists
for continuous polynomial coordinates. (b) F (x) = x4 on [−1, 1] has a unique minimizer and no
                                                                                 k
g > 0, so Lemma 6.12 fails for polynomials. (c) For k ≥ 1, F (x, y) = x2 − 12 x2 + y 2 on [0, 1]2 has
                                                       k
∂xx F ≤ 2, ∂yy F = 2 and growth constant 12 , since x2 ≤ x2 ; its input has O(k) bits if exponents are
                                                     k
written in binary, but F ( 12 , 0) has denominator 22 +1 . Binary-encoded exponents therefore need a
separate evaluation analysis.

    For continuous polynomial objectives an exact answer must therefore be implicit. Appendix B.2
gives one form: under point growth and strict complementarity at the active bounds, sign tests on
the certified retained box identify the active face, and a uniform convexity test on the remaining
coordinates yields a strongly convex polynomial subproblem whose unique minimizer is the global
one. Besides p, κ and I, the cost bound depends polynomially on log2 max{2, C2 /λA }, where C2
bounds the absolute row sums of the second derivatives in the continuous coordinates on X̄ and λA
is the smallest inward derivative at the active bounds (Theorem B.4); Propositions B.5 and B.6
show that this term is intrinsic to certificates of this form and that weak complementarity defeats
them.


7    Conditional recourse
The condition numbers of Section 5 are built from the coordinate curvatures Li of F and a growth
constant. Stiff convex terms, which are easy to optimize, can make the curvatures large. A residual
problem with combinatorial structure causes two other difficulties: nearly tied endpoint assignments
of its coordinates make the point-growth constant small, and dense interactions among them make
the bags large. This section minimizes exactly over part of the variables for fixed values of the
others; we call this recourse, as for the second stage of a two-stage program, although nothing here
is random. The coordinates split into a retained set K and a recourse set R = [n] \ K, with v = xK
and y = xR . For given v, y is chosen optimally, and CT (Algorithm 5.6) runs on the value function
V of (7.1).
    Section 7.1 shows that V inherits coordinate curvature and growth from F , so that Sections 4–6
apply to V (Theorem 7.2). Section 7.3 eliminates private convex quadratic blocks and certifies
the curvature of their value factors (Theorem 7.9). Section 7.4 eliminates residual problems that
are concave in each coordinate and submodular after sign changes, whose conditional values are
minimum cuts (Theorem 7.15). These are the main results. Section 7.2 shows that a correction
charged to one bag is valid with exact or certified recourse (Theorem 7.4), but not with grid
min-marginals (Proposition C.2 in Appendix C); Section 7.4 uses its Lemma 7.3 and Theorem 7.4.
Remark 7.21 extends the cut oracle to concave–convex residuals. Section 7.5 does not use recourse:
for balanced quadratics the grid problem of CT is itself a minimum cut, so no tree decomposition is
needed (Theorem 7.24).


                                                  30
7.1    Value functions inherit curvature and growth
For the retained set K and the recourse set R = [n] \ K define

                                   V (v) = min F (v, y),                    v ∈ X̄K ,                  (7.1)
                                               y∈XR

              Q
where X̄K = i∈K [ℓi , ui ] and the feasible set XR of the recourse problem is compact and does not
                                                                             Q
depend on v; this is the only structural requirement. For model (3.1), XR = i∈R Xi ; in Section 7.3
it is a product of polytopes. We have minXK V = F ∗ , and a retained point v ∈ XK together with a
minimizer y(v) of the recourse problem is a feasible point with value V (v).

Lemma 7.1 (Partial minimization). Let V be defined by (7.1).

 (a) Let i ∈ K and Li ≥ 0. If t 7→ F (v + tei , y) − L2i t2 is concave on {t : v + tei ∈ X̄K } for every
     v ∈ X̄K and every y ∈ XR , then so is t 7→ V (v + tei ) − L2i t2 for every v ∈ X̄K . In particular,
     upper coordinate curvatures of F for the coordinates in K are upper coordinate curvatures of
     V on X̄K .

 (b) If F has point growth with constant g at (v ∗ , y ∗ ), then V has point growth with constant g at
     v ∗ on XK .

Proof. (a) For fixed v, the function t 7→ V (v + tei ) − L2i t2 is the pointwise minimum over y ∈ XR
of the concave functions t 7→ F (v + tei , y) − L2i t2 . It is finite, and it is concave because its
hypograph is the intersection of their hypographs. (b) Since F ∗ ≤ V (v ∗ ) ≤ F (v ∗ , y ∗ ) = F ∗ , we have
V (v ∗ ) = F ∗ . For v ∈ XK let y(v) attain the minimum. Then V (v) − V (v ∗ ) = F (v, y(v)) − F ∗ ≥
g(∥v − v ∗ ∥2 + ∥y(v) − y ∗ ∥2 ) ≥ g∥v − v ∗ ∥2 .

    Part (a) is the classical stability of semiconcavity under infima [34]. Lemma 7.1(a) explains
why the corrected-grid bound survives partial minimization, and Lemma 7.1(b) why the growth
analysis does. The curvature of V can be much smaller than that of F . If F (v, y) = M (y − v)2 with
v, y ∈ [0, 1], the diagonal curvature in v is 2M , whereas V ≡ 0. Section 7.3 evaluates V exactly and
certifies curvature bounds that can cancel such stiff convex terms (Theorem 7.9), though not always
(Proposition 7.11); Section 7.4 evaluates V exactly when the eliminated problem is combinatorial.
    The factor structure survives the elimination as follows. Suppose the recourse coordinates form
disjoint private blocks y (1) , . . . , y (r) with domains Y1 , . . . , Yr , so that XR = t Yt , and

   The theorem charges the work of the recourse oracle to the polynomial factor, so it is useful
when the recourse problems are easy and their elimination does not increase the width. Sections 7.3
and 7.4 give classes in which the recourse problems are solved exactly and with certificates.

7.2   Bag-local corrections with exact or certified recourse
The correction of Section 4 is global: every coordinate is rounded, and the allowance is the sum of
all coordinate corrections. A bag-local allowance is valid when the coordinates outside the bag are
minimized exactly or with a certified error.

Bag cells. Let B be a nonempty set of coordinates, for example a bag or the core of Section 7.4.

    Appendix C applies the theorem level by level to multilevel schemes and shows that filtering
alone needs only values whose error varies by at most εor over the corners (Proposition C.1). Grid
min-marginals, which minimize over the outside coordinates on the grid instead of their domain, do
not suffice: a constant correction charged to one bag then keeps a cell containing the bag projection
of the minimizer only if the constant is at least a quantity that grows with the number of outside
coordinates, already for bag size two and κ < 11 (Proposition C.2 in Appendix C).

7.3   Certified convex recourse
Large positive curvature often sits in variables that enter the objective convexly once the other
variables are fixed. This subsection eliminates such variables exactly, as convex value factors, and
shows that CT then depends only on the coordinate curvature and the growth of the reduced
objective. Proposition 7.8 covers three cases (no certificate, a globally affine response, a piecewise-
affine response), and Proposition 7.11 shows the limits of this approach.

Model.

Leaf certificates and the curvature of a value factor. Fix one block, write k = kt , and

Definition 7.6 (Leaf certificate). Let Π′ ⊆ Rk be a polytope with nonempty interior. A certificate
on Π′ consists of affine maps ȳ(w) = ȳ0 + Bw and λ(w) = λ0 + Λw ∈ Rm such that, for all w ∈ Π′ ,

                             Gȳ(w) ≤ b,       λ(w) ≥ 0,                                                (7.6)
                             C ȳ(w) + Γw + c + GT λ(w) = 0,                                            (7.7)
                                                  
                             λs (w) bs − Gs ȳ(w) = 0        (s = 1, . . . , m).                        (7.8)

A certificate on a rational box Π ⊆ Rk is a rational binary space partition of Π, in which each internal
node splits its polytope by one rational hyperplane into two closed halves with nonempty interiors,
together with a certificate on each of its leaves Π′1 , . . . , Π′NΠ . For a private box Y = [ℓY , uY ] the
rows are y ≤ uY and −y ≤ −ℓY , and λ splits into upper and lower bound multipliers.

    A certificate is checked exactly in time polynomial in its encoding length. The leaves of a
binary space partition cover its root, each leaf is described by the inequalities of Π and of the
halfspaces on its root path, and one linear program per node checks that both halves have nonempty
interiors. Stationarity (7.7) is an identity between affine maps, and complementarity (7.8) says that
a quadratic polynomial vanishes on a set with nonempty interior, that is, all its coefficients vanish.
By linear programming duality, an affine inequality α0 + αT w ≥ 0 holds on a nonempty polytope
{w : Aw ≤ a} if and only if some π ≥ 0 has AT π = −α and aT π ≤ α0 ; such rational multipliers are
part of the certificate.

Lemma 7.7 (Certificate identity and piece Hessian). Let (ȳ, λ) be a certificate on Π′ . Then:

 (a) For w ∈ Π′ and y ∈ Rnt ,

                    ϕ(w, y) − ϕ(w, ȳ(w)) = 12 (y − ȳ(w))T C(y − ȳ(w)) + λ(w)T (b − Gy).              (7.9)

     The right side is nonnegative for y ∈ Y . Hence ψ = qΠ′ on Π′ , where qΠ′ (w) = ϕ(w, ȳ(w)).

 (b) Let E = {s : λs ̸≡ 0}. Then Gs B = 0 for s ∈ E, and

                              ∇2 qΠ′ = B T CB + B T Γ + ΓT B = −B T CB ⪯ 0.


                                                      34
Proof. (a) The function ϕ(w, ·) is quadratic with Hessian C, and by (7.7) its gradient at ȳ = ȳ(w) is
−GT λ. Exact expansion gives

                      ϕ(w, y) − ϕ(w, ȳ) = λT (Gȳ − Gy) + 12 (y − ȳ)T C(y − ȳ),

and λT (Gȳ − Gy) = λT (b − Gy) − λT (b − Gȳ) = λT (b − Gy) by (7.8). For y ∈ Y both terms on the
right of (7.9) are nonnegative, since C ⪰ 0 and λ(w) ≥ 0. Equality holds at y = ȳ(w) ∈ Y .
    (b) For each s, the polynomial λs (w)(bs − Gs ȳ0 − Gs Bw) vanishes on Π′ , which has nonempty
interior, so it is the zero polynomial. The polynomial ring is an integral domain; for s ∈ E the first
factor is a nonzero polynomial, so the second is zero and Gs B = 0. The w-linear part of (7.7) reads
CB + Γ + GT Λ = 0, and row s of Λ vanishes for s ∈   / E. Hence
                                                   X
                                B T (CB + Γ) = −         (Gs B)T Λs = 0,
                                                   s∈E

so B T Γ = −B T CB, which is symmetric; therefore also ΓT B = −B T CB. Substituting y = ȳ0 + Bw
in ϕ gives ∇2 qΠ′ = B T CB + B T Γ + ΓT B = −B T CB.

   By (C.1) in Appendix C, (B T CB)jj is the energy that the private response spends to follow a
unit change of wj .

Proof. (a) For fixed y, ϕ(w, y) is affine in w. Thus ψ is a pointwise minimum of affine functions over

    (c) Take any leaf Π′ and an interior point w′ of Π′ . For small |t|, ψ(w′ + tej ) = qΠ′ (w′ + tej ),
whose second derivative is −(BΠ T CB ′ ) . Concavity of ψ(w ′ +te )+σ ′ t2 /2 forces σ ′ ≤ (B T CB ′ ) .
                                  ′    Π jj                        j                         Π′   Π jj
Minimizing over the leaves gives σ ′ ≤ σj . Applying this to two certificates shows that each σj is at
most the other.

   Part (a) alone certifies σj = 0 for every block; this is the direct bound. Part (c) shows that
a piecewise certificate loses nothing: the cancellation it certifies is the largest one possible for
coordinatewise semiconcavity of this factor. For a private box, a certificate on every rational box
always exists, so this largest cancellation is always certifiable (Proposition C.4 in Appendix C). The
construction can be exponential; no bound on the number of leaves is claimed.

a time by a better endpoint does not increase V , so a vertex of X̄K , which lies in XK , is optimal.

quadratic program per block.

with a fixed exponent. So Theorem 7.2(a),(b) apply with LV = L+ ; the encoding length of the L+          i ,
and hence log(1/L+ ), is polynomial in I. There are at most rK p value-factor evaluations per stage.
    Exact output. Lemma C.6, applied to the integer slices of the retained box, bounds the

Remark 7.10 (Three instances and prior work). Without certificates, Li = (H0 )ii : changing active
sets are handled exactly, but direct stiffness is not cancelled. A one-leaf certificate is a globally
affine response; then ψt is an explicit quadratic with Hessian −BtT Ct Bt that can be merged into
F0 (for M (y − v)2 on [0, 1]2 it gives σ = 2M and Lv = 0). A certificate with several leaves is a
piecewise-affine response and certifies the largest possible σj . The partitions of different blocks are
never overlaid; their common refinement can have exponentially many cells. Parametric-QP critical
regions are classical [18]; the contribution here is their curvature accounting inside CT. Khajavirad
[110] eliminates the components formed by variables with positive diagonal into value functions of
their neighbors; the neighbors have nonpositive diagonal, can be taken binary, and their values are
enumerated. Here the neighbors vEt are gridded continuous or integer coordinates, and what is
certified is the coordinate curvature of each value factor.
    Appendix C derives an affine condensation (Corollary C.9), the bound κV ≤ max{1, C0 } ·
max{1, ν/g} when L+ ≤ C0 ν with ν = max{0, −λmin (∇2 F )} (Corollary C.10), a block whose
certified curvature is 8 while its direct curvature is 2M + 8 (Example C.11), and a ladder with m
negative eigenvalues whose certified ratio L+ /g is at most 123 for all m and M (Proposition C.12).
For a block whose domain is a box, one exact convex quadratic program and one linear program
decide whether some affine map is an optimal response on the whole scope box, and if so return it
with a one-leaf certificate (Theorem C.7 in Appendix C).

Limits of global recourse curvature. Call a retained set K ⊆ [n] admissible if the principal
Hessian block of the coordinates in R = [n] \ K is positive semidefinite, so that they form convex
private blocks. For admissible K the value function VK is intrinsic: it does not depend on how the
private part is grouped or certified. Let LK be the least L for which Definition 3.1 holds for VK
in every retained coordinate, and gK the largest growth constant of VK . Since every certified Li is
valid (Theorem 7.9(i)), L+ ≥ LK , and every growth constant g of VK , in particular every growth
constant of F (Lemma 7.1(b)), satisfies g ≤ gK ; so no certificate yields a parameter below LK /gK .

Proposition 7.11 (Recourse cannot replace L/g by ν/g in general). For M ≥ 1 let x ∈ [0, 2],
y ∈ [1, 3] and
                    FM (x, y) = M (x − y)2 − 18 (x + y − 2)2 + 2(y − 1).
Then (1, 1) is the unique minimizer, F ∗ = 0, FM (x, y) − F ∗ ≥ 81 (x − 1)2 + (y − 1)2 , and ν = 12 ; thus
                                                                                      

ν/g ≤ 4 with g = 18 . Nevertheless, for every admissible retained set K and every positive diagonal
rescaling of the retained coordinates,

                                    LK /gK ≥ (4M − 12 )/3 ≥ M.

    The proof is in Appendix C, which also locates the obstruction in a clipped piece of the private
response. Disjoint copies give arbitrarily many variables with the same constants and bag size
two. The instance is trivial to solve; the proposition limits the parameter of Theorem 7.9, not the
problem.




                                                   37
7.4    Separately concave residuals and minimum cuts
In this subsection F (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c is a rational quadratic, the retained set K is a core of k
continuous coordinates, and the recourse set R is called the residual. Assume

      (R1) Hii ≤ 0 (i ∈ R),        (R2) oi oj Hij ≤ 0 (i ̸= j, i, j ∈ R) for some o ∈ {−1, 1}R .          (7.11)

Nothing is assumed about the entries of H in core rows, and the residual interaction graph may
be dense. A residual coordinate may be continuous or integer; integer bounds are rounded inward
first, and an empty domain is rejected. Signs o as in (R2) are found, or shown not to exist, by the
following lemma applied to HRR .

Lemma 7.12 (Recognizing balance). For a symmetric m × m matrix H, signs o ∈ {−1, 1}m with
oi oj Hij ≤ 0 for all i ̸= j can be found, or shown not to exist, in time linear in m plus the number
of nonzero off-diagonal entries.

Proof. The conditions are oi = oj when Hij < 0 and oi = −oj when Hij > 0. Breadth-first search
fixes one sign in each connected component of the graph of nonzero off-diagonal entries, propagates
the conditions along a spanning tree and checks every other edge. If an edge violates its condition, it
closes a cycle with an odd number of positive entries, around which the conditions are inconsistent
[88]; otherwise all conditions hold.

Lemma 7.13 (Endpoint reduction). Fix v ∈ X̄K and a box i∈R [ℓi , ui ] whose bounds are integers
                                                                       Q

for integer coordinates. Under (R1), the minimum of F (v, ·) over this continuous box is attained at
a point with yi ∈ {ℓi , ui } for all i ∈ R, and it equals the minimum over the mixed residual domain.

Proof. Start from a minimizer, which exists by compactness. For each residual coordinate in turn,
F as a function of that coordinate alone is 12 Hii t2 plus an affine function of t, which is concave
by (R1); replace the coordinate by an endpoint with no larger value. The result is a minimizer
whose residual coordinates are endpoints. Its integer coordinates are integral, so it lies in the mixed
domain, which is contained in the continuous box.

    For i ∈ R put αi = ℓi , δi = ui − ℓi if oi = 1, and αi = ui , δi = −(ui − ℓi ) if oi = −1. For a label
z ∈ {0, 1}R let y(z) have coordinates y(z)i = αi + δi zi ; as z ranges over {0, 1}R , y(z) ranges over all
residual endpoint vectors. In a network with source s and sink t, cap(S) denotes the total capacity
of the arcs leaving a node set S that contains s but not t.

   Every node set that contains s but not t is Sz for exactly one z, so by (7.12) minimizing Φ is a
minimum s–t cut problem with nonnegative capacities.

Theorem 7.15 (Certified cut recourse). Assume (7.11). For every rational core point v, one
maximum-flow computation returns a minimizer y(v) of F (v, ·) over XR , which is a residual
endpoint vector, the exact value V (v), and a certificate: a label z with y(v) = y(z) and a feasible flow
in the network of Proposition 7.14 whose value equals cap(Sz ). The bit complexity is polynomial
in the encoding length of F and v, with an absolute exponent. Given the network, the certificate is
checked with a number of rational operations linear in |R| plus the number of nonzero entries Hij
with i ̸= j in R.

Proof. By Lemma 7.13 the conditional minimum is attained at some y(z), and by Proposition 7.14
minimizing over z is a minimum cut problem. Every feasible flow has value at most the capacity of
every cut (weak duality), so a flow of value cap(Sz ) proves that Sz is a minimum cut; checking it
needs the capacity bounds, conservation at each residual node and one comparison. A maximum
flow and a minimum cut of equal value exist and are found, for instance, by the Edmonds–Karp
algorithm [64], whose number of arithmetic operations is polynomial in the number of nodes and
arcs, here |R| + 2 and at most 2|R| plus twice the number of nonzero residual couplings. After
multiplication by a common denominator of polynomial bit length, the capacities are integers, every
augmentation is by an integer, and every flow value is an integer at most the total capacity, so
all intermediate numbers have polynomial bit length. The coefficients of Proposition 7.14(b) are
obtained by exact rational evaluation.

Remark 7.16 (Scope and prior work). Lemmas 7.12 and 7.13 and Proposition 7.14 are classical
(Section 2). With an empty core, Theorem 7.15 is the polynomial solvability of box QPs that
are submodular after sign changes and have nonpositive diagonal [32, 150]. What we use is the
conditional form: the core and the core–residual couplings are arbitrary, and each conditional value
comes with a flow certificate. Del Pia and Khajavirad also fix the coordinates with nonpositive
diagonal at endpoints; their polynomial classes [53, Theorem 4] restrict the structure of these
coordinates (logarithmic treewidth and logarithmic interfaces to the others) and the rank of their
coupling to the others, whereas (R2) restricts only signs and allows a dense residual. Condition
(R1) is what reduces the residual to endpoint vectors. Without it, and with continuous residual
coordinates, the conditional problem is a box QP that is submodular after sign changes, and
the complexity of that class is open [32, footnote 2]; Section 7.5 grids such coordinates instead
(Corollary 7.25).

Search over a continuous core. Let the core domain be XK = [0, 1]k with k ≥ 1, and let the
residual domain XR be any compact mixed box. Let L ≥ 0 be an upper coordinate curvature of F
for every core coordinate (for a quadratic, any L ≥ maxi∈K Hii ); by Lemma 7.1(a) it is one for V .
An exact oracle returns, for every dyadic v ∈ [0, 1]k , the value V (v) and a completion y(v) ∈ XR
with F (v, y(v)) = V (v), together with a certificate. Theorem 7.15 supplies one under (7.11), and
Remark 7.21 supplies one when the residual has a convex part. Level j of the search uses dyadic



                                                    39
cells of side hj = 2−j , the common mesh of Section 5 with s = 1, and

                            kLh2j
                     ej =         ,   βV (C) = min{V (v) : v a corner of C} − ej .

Algorithm 7.17 (CORE: core search). Given an exact oracle, L, an accuracy ε > 0 and a level
limit J, start with Q0 = {[0, 1]k } and U = +∞. For j = 0, 1, . . . , J:

  (i) query the oracle at every corner of a cell of Qj that has not been queried before, and let U be
      the least value V (v) of all queried corners, attained by the incumbent (vU , y(vU ));

 (ii) put λj = minC∈Qj βV (C) and retain Q′j = {C ∈ Qj : βV (C) ≤ U };

 (iii) if U − min{λj , U } ≤ ε, stop; otherwise let Qj+1 consist of the 2k dyadic children of each cell
       of Q′j .

Return the incumbent, U and the lower bound min{λj , U }.

Theorem 7.18 (Core search). At every level j of CORE:

 (a) every cell of Qj that contains x∗K for a global minimizer x∗ is retained; in particular Q′j ̸= ∅;

 (b) λj ≤ F ∗ ≤ U ≤ λj + ej ;

 (c) every retained cell has a corner v with V (v) ≤ F ∗ + 2ej ;

 (d) every x ∈ X satisfies F (x) ≥ min{λj , U }, and this can be verified from the list of cells, the
     certified queried values and the retention decisions, without any optimization.

Proof. Let C be a cell of side hj and x ∈ X with xK ∈ C. Lemma 7.3 with B = K gives
F (x) ≥ min{V (v) : v a corner of C} − eK (C) ≥ βV (C), because the core coordinates are continuous
and eK (C) ≤ ej .
    (a) The unit cube contains x∗K . If C ∈ Qj contains it, then βV (C) ≤ F (x∗ ) = F ∗ ≤ U , so C is
retained and every child of C that contains x∗K belongs to Qj+1 . (b), (c) These are Theorem 7.4
with B = K, the cells Qj , e = ej , V = V and εor = 0; its hypothesis that some cell of Qj contains
x∗K holds by (a), and λj ≤ F ∗ by the first paragraph applied to x∗ . (d) Children of retained cells
cover them, so the cells of Qj together with the cells discarded at levels i < j cover [0, 1]k . If xK
lies in a cell of Qj , then F (x) ≥ λj by the first paragraph. If it lies in a cell C discarded at level i,
then F (x) ≥ βV (C) > U (i) ≥ U , where U (i) is the incumbent value at level i. Each step uses only
recorded values.

    By (b), CORE stops at the latest at the first level j with ej ≤ ε, provided that J is at least this
level. Its cost is controlled by the number of near-optimal corners.

Exact output. Under (7.11) and for rational data, F ∗ and every value minv∈[0,1]k F (v, y) at a
residual endpoint vector y have denominators at most a constant ΩK of bit length O(I 2 ) (Lemma D.1).
Run CORE with the oracle of Theorem 7.15 and accuracy ε = Ω−2    K /2, and minimize F (·, y) exactly
over the faces of the core cube, where y is the residual part of the incumbent. The result is a
global minimizer that passes the acceptance test √of Proposition 6.6 with the denominator bound
ΩK . Under core growth the total cost is 8 (1 + kκK )k poly(I) bit operations with an absolute
                                           k

exponent, and g is not an input (Theorem D.2 in Appendix D).
Remark 7.21 (Concave–convex residuals). The oracle extends to residuals with a convex part. Split
R = R− ∪ R+ , require (R1) only on R− and (R2) on all of R, and let the coordinates in R+
be continuous with HR+ R+ ⪰ 0. For a fixed core point, the coordinates in R− can be moved to
endpoints without increasing F , as in Lemma 7.13. For each set S ⊆ R− of coordinates with
label zi = 1, what remains is a convex box QP in yR+ , which is solved exactly in polynomial
time [119]. Its value Φ(S) is a submodular function of S, because partial minimization preserves
submodularity [173], and the conditional minimum is minS Φ(S). Submodular minimization by the
ellipsoid method [82] and Edmonds’ min–max theorem [63] then give the exact conditional value, a
minimizer, and a certificate: a convex combination of at most |R− | + 1 greedy vectors of Φ. All
these ingredients are classical, and the same composition underlies the mixed continuous–discrete
methods cited in Section 2. Proposition D.4 in Appendix D states the result precisely. It supplies, for
concave–convex residuals, the exact oracle required by CORE (Theorem 7.18 and Proposition 7.19),
with a certificate checkable by its part (d) without optimization. For R+ = ∅ this is the class (7.11),
where Theorem 7.15 needs only one maximum flow.

7.5   Corrected grids without a tree decomposition
CT (Algorithm 5.6) uses its tree decomposition only to compute, on the current product grid
     Q
G = i Gi , the corrected minimum β = minG Q, one minimizer and the min-marginals mi (v) of
Q = F − D (Lemma 4.3). Any exact method for these quantities can replace the dynamic program.
For the following sign structure, minimum cuts are such a method, whatever the interaction graph.

Definition 7.22 (Balanced quadratic). A quadratic F (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c is balanced if there
are signs o ∈ {−1, 1}n with oi oj Hij ≤ 0 for all i ̸= j.



                                                    41
    Equivalently, after the substitution xi 7→ oi xi all off-diagonal Hessian entries are nonpositive, so
F becomes submodular on Rn . Condition (R2) of Section 7.4 says that F becomes balanced once
the core coordinates are fixed, and Lemma 7.12 recognizes balance in linear time. No sign condition
is imposed on the diagonal or on the linear terms. If Hii ≤ 0 for all i, then F satisfies (7.11) with
an empty core, and Theorem 7.15 minimizes it exactly with one maximum flow.

Lemma 7.23 (Grid minimization by one minimum cut). Let F be balanced with signs o, let
Gi ⊂ Q be finite and nonempty with |Gi | = ki + 1, and let χi : Gi → Q be arbitrary. Put
Fχ (y) = F (y) + i χi (yi ) for y ∈ i Gi . Then min Fχ and a minimizer are obtained from one
                 P                  Q
                                     P                                 P      P
minimum s–t cut in a network with i ki + 2 nodes and at most 3 i ki + 2 i<j: Hij ̸=0 ki kj arcs,
whose capacities are computed from H, b, the grid nodes and the values of the χi by polynomially
many rational operations. A feasible flow of equal value certifies the minimum. The same holds for
each min-marginal min{Fχ (y) : yi = v}, with Gi replaced by {v}.

    The proof (Appendix D) encodes each coordinate by threshold variables and applies Proposi-
tion 7.14(a); arcs of infinite capacity exclude the binary vectors that encode no grid node. Threshold
encodings, and the reduction of pairwise problems that are submodular with respect to label orders
to one minimum cut, are classical [164, Sections 3–4], [102]. Lemma 7.23 is this construction for
quadratic pair terms; what matters here is that the unary terms χi are arbitrary, so that they can
be the negative corrections −di on nonuniform grids.

Theorem 7.24 (Balanced box quadratics without a width parameter). Let F be a balanced rational
quadratic on a mixed box X with input length I. Run CT and EX (Algorithm 6.13) with β, a
minimizer and all min-marginals computed by Lemma 7.23 instead of Lemma 4.3.

 (a) On every instance, CT(2−q ) halts with a feasible rational point and a valid path certificate of
     gap at most 2−q , in which the dynamic programs are replaced by minimum cuts with flows of
     equal value; and EX halts with a global minimizer and F ∗ .

 (b) If F has point growth with condition number κ, then CT(2−q ) uses (nκ(I + q + 1))O(1) bit
     operations and EX uses (nκ(I + 1))O(1) bit operations.

No decomposition is used, g and κ are not inputs, and the validity of every certificate uses neither
growth nor uniqueness.

Proof. (a) In CT and EX, and in the proofs of Propositions 4.2 and 4.5, Theorem 4.7, Lemmas 5.4
and 5.5 and Theorems 5.7, 6.9 and 6.14, the tree decomposition enters only through Lemma 4.3,
which computes β, a minimizer of Q and the min-marginals mi (v) on the current grid (if P = ∅,
CT minimizes F over the grid i {ℓi , ui }, on which Q = F ). Everything else uses the data of F
                                  Q

directly: the condition of Definition 3.1 holds with Li = max{Hii , 0}, because the restriction of F
to a coordinate line has second derivative Hii , and REC (Algorithm 6.7) and Proposition 6.6 work
with F and its gradient. Since D is a sum of unary terms, Q = Fχ with χi = −di , so Lemma 7.23
computes β and a minimizer with one cut and each mi (v) with one further cut. A flow of equal value
certifies each of these values from below by weak duality, which is what conditions (C1) and (C2)
of a path certificate require. Hence the validity and termination statements of these results hold
verbatim,
        √ including the abort rule of CT and, under weighted growth, the success of a trial with
2µ ≤ 6 κ̄; only the operation counts change.
    (b) It remains to count cut computations and bit lengths in place of table operations; the count
is in Appendix D.



                                                   42
Corollary 7.25 (Balanced after deleting a supplied core). Let F and X be as in Theorem 7.24,
except that F need not be balanced. Suppose a set K of k coordinates is supplied such that oi oj Hij ≤ 0
for some signs o and all i ̸= j outside K. Then Theorem 7.24 holds for F , with the work bounds of
                                           √
part (b) multiplied by K k , where K = O( κ log n) bounds the nodes per coordinate in every trial
that CT runs.

Proof. Fixing the core coordinates at grid nodes changes only unary and constant terms of the
remaining quadratic, which is balanced. Hence β, a minimizer and each min-marginal are minima,
over at most K k vectors of core nodes, of problems solved by Lemma 7.23; a min-marginal
of a core coordinate fixes that coordinate.
                                     √       Every trial that CT runs has at most Kµ ≤ Kµ∗
                               µ ∗          √
nodes per coordinate, where 2 ≤ 6 κ̄ ≤ 6 κ (proof of Theorem 5.7(b) and Appendix D), so
              √
K = Kµ∗ = O( κ log n). The rest of the proof of Theorem 7.24 is unchanged.

   Corollary 7.25 complements Section 7.4: residual coordinates may now have positive diagonal,
but they are gridded and filtered like the core, so their curvature enters κ, and point growth of
F is needed instead of growth of V in the core coordinates; the residual graph may still be dense.
Section 2 compares Theorem 7.24 with methods for continuous submodular minimization.


8     Coupling constraints
The corrected-grid bound of Proposition 4.2 follows from rounding each coordinate independently to
an endpoint of its grid interval, and every rounded point is feasible because the domain is a product.
A coupling constraint, even one as sparse as x1 = x2 , destroys this: independent rounding leaves the
feasible set, and the minimum over feasible grid points need not be a lower bound. This section shows
that totally unimodular (TU) coupling of the continuous variables, with data aligned to a uniform
mesh, admits a feasible mean-preserving rounding. Filtering, certificates, accuracy-independent
node counts and exact output then carry over. The price is visible in two places. The rounding is
correlated, so a bound on the full continuous Hessian replaces coordinate curvature; Example 8.5
shows that this is necessary. The analysis uses a uniform aligned mesh, so the number of nodes
                            √
per coordinate is of order nc κc instead of logarithmic in n; Section 8.7 shows that the algorithm
incurs this cost, and that two natural non-uniform alternatives, misaligned coordinate grids and a
common graded pattern, give invalid bounds with curvature-only corrections. Section 8.5 states
when the algorithm is polynomial and why it is not fixed-parameter tractable. Its levels resemble
the scaling phases of Hochbaum and Shanthikumar [96] for separable convex objectives; Section 2
compares the two.

8.1     Model
Definition 8.1 (Aligned TU model). Let nc ≥ 1 and nd ≥ 0. The feasible set is

       X = (x, z) ∈ Rnc × Znd : ℓ ≤ x ≤ u, z ∈ Z, Ax + Bz ≤ b ,
             
                                                                            Z = Z1 × · · · × Znd ,

with the following data.

    (i) A ∈ {0, ±1}m×nc is totally unimodular; B and b are rational.

 (ii) ℓ < u componentwise (fixed continuous coordinates have been substituted), and every Zk is a
      nonempty, explicitly listed finite set of integers.



                                                  43
 (iii) A rational mesh unit η > 0 is given such that

                    ℓ/η ∈ Znc ,     u/η ∈ Znc ,       (b − Bz)/η ∈ Zm     for every z ∈ Z.         (8.1)

      For integral ℓ, u, B, b one may take η = 1. Write ∆η for the denominator of η in lowest terms.
                P
 (iv) F (x, z) = a∈A fa (vSa ) with v = (x, z), and a tree decomposition with N nodes and bags of size
      at most p is given such that every scope Sa and every row support {i : Ari ̸= 0} ∪ {k : Brk =
                                                                                                  ̸ 0}
      is contained in some bag.

 (v) (Directional curvature.) A linear subspace W ⊆ Rnc and a rational L̄ > 0 are given such that,
     for every z ∈ Z, F (·, z) is twice continuously differentiable on an open set containing [ℓ, u] and

                            wT ∇2xx F (x, z) w ≤ L̄∥w∥2       (x ∈ [ℓ, u], w ∈ W).                 (8.2)

      Either W = Rnc , or W = ker C, where the rows of C are the continuous parts Ar of some
      equality pairs, that is, pairs of rows r, r′ with (Ar′ , Br′ , br′ ) = −(Ar , Br , br ).
    Equalities are thus written as two opposite inequalities; this preserves total unimodularity. Only
the continuous columns A must be TU: the integer columns B are arbitrary, because the rounding
below never changes z. Throughout we write n = nc + nd ,
                                             n            o                           nc L̄h2j
            s = max(ui − ℓi ),    KZ = max 1, max|Zk | ,        hj = η 2−j ,   Ej =            ,
                 i≤nc                             k                                      8
S = arg minX F , F ∗ = minX F , and Si = {vi : v ∈ S} for the projection of S on coordinate i
(continuous or discrete). Since the sets Zk are listed, KZ ≤ I. As in CT with a common mesh
(Lemma 5.9), hj is the same for all continuous coordinates; its base is the mesh unit η instead of s,
so that every grid is aligned with the data. When a growth constant is discussed, it is a set-growth
constant gS as in Definition 3.2, for the Euclidean distance on Rnc +nd :

            F (v) − F ∗ ≥ gS dist(v, S)2    (v ∈ X),       κc = κ(L̄, gS ) = max{1, L̄/gS }.       (8.3)

Point growth at a unique minimizer is the case S = {v ∗ }. Neither gS nor S is used by any algorithm
below.
    Conditions (8.1) and (8.2) can be certified in time polynomial in I (Appendix E.1): alignment
by 1 + k |Zk | vector tests, without enumerating Z (Proposition E.2), and directional curvature, for
       P

quadratic and fixed-degree polynomial factors, by rational sufficient tests such as a bound on the
absolute row sums of the continuous Hessian (Remark E.1).

8.2   Feasible rounding on aligned cells
Definition 8.2 (Level-j domains). A level-j domain is a product D = D1 ×· · ·×Dnc ×Λ1 ×· · ·×Λnd ,
where each Di ⊆ [ℓi , ui ] is a nonempty union of finitely many closed intervals [a, a′ ], a ≤ a′ , with
a, a′ ∈ hj Z, and each Λk ⊆ Zk is nonempty. Its grid in coordinate i is Gj (Di ) = Di ∩ hj Z, its cells
are the intervals [t, t + hj ] ⊆ Di with t ∈ hj Z, and its grid is Gj (D) = i Gj (Di ) × k Λk .
                                                                           Q            Q

    Since ℓ, u ∈ ηZnc ⊆ hj Znc , the box [ℓ, u] × Z is a level-0 domain, and a level-j domain is also a
level-(j + 1) domain. Components of Di may be single points.
Lemma 8.3 (Feasible corner rounding). Let D be a level-j domain and (x, z) ∈ X ∩ D. There is a
random vector Y ∈ Rnc with finitely many values such that, almost surely, (Y, z) ∈ X ∩ Gj (D) and
Y − x ∈ W, and

                                                  44
  (a) EY = x;

  (b) if xi ∈ hj Z then Yi = xi ; otherwise, with ti = hj ⌊xi /hj ⌋, the cell [ti , ti + hj ] lies in Di and
      Yi ∈ {ti , ti + hj };

  (c) E∥Y − x∥2 ≤ nc h2j /4.

Proof. Write h = hj and ti = h⌊xi /h⌋, so x = t + hϑ with ϑ ∈ [0, 1)nc . Let I0 = {i : ϑi = 0} = {i :
xi ∈ hZ}. If i ∈/ I0 , then xi lies in a component [a, a′ ] of Di with a, a′ ∈ hZ and a < xi < a′ , so
a ≤ ti and ti + h ≤ a′ ; the cell [ti , ti + h] lies in Di .
    Consider the polytope
                      n                     b − Bz − At                                o
                 Q = ϑ′ ∈ Rnc : Aϑ′ ≤                   , 0 ≤ ϑ′ ≤ 1, ϑ′i ≤ 0 (i ∈ I0 ) .
                                                 h
The right-hand side (b − Bz − At)/h is integral: (b − Bz)/h = 2j (b − Bz)/η ∈ Zm by (8.1), and
At/h ∈ Zm because A is integral and t/h ∈ Znc . Appending unit rows to a TU matrix gives a TU
matrix [165, Section 19.1], so by the Hoffman–Kruskal theorem [97] every vertex of Q is integral,
hence lies in {0, 1}nc and vanishes on I0 . Since (x, z) ∈ X, ϑ ∈ Q, and the polytope Q is the convex
hull of its vertices, so ϑ = k λk ϑk with vertices ϑk and convex weights λk . Let Y = t + hϑk with
                              P

probability λk .
    Then EY = t + hϑ = x. For each outcome, AY + Bz = At + hAϑk + Bz ≤ b. For i ∈ I0 ,
Yi = ti = xi ; for i ∈/ I0 , Yi ∈ {ti , ti + h}, which lies in the cell [ti , ti + h] ⊆ Di ⊆ [ℓi , ui ]. Hence
(Y, z) ∈ X ∩ Gj (D) and (b) holds. If W = ker C, each row Ar of C belongs to an equality pair, so
Ar Y + Br z = br = Ar x + Br z and Ar (Y − x) = 0. Finally, for i ∈         / I0 the variable Yi takes two
values with mean xi , so E(Yi − xi )2 = (xi − ti )(ti + h − xi ) ≤ h2 /4, and coordinates in I0 contribute
zero. This proves (c).

   The rounded coordinates are dependent: they follow a vertex decomposition of the polytope Q,
not a product law. This is why the next lemma needs (8.2) rather than coordinate curvature.

Lemma 8.4 (Rounding allowance). In the situation of Lemma 8.3,

                                                   L̄
                          EF (Y, z) ≤ F (x, z) +      E∥Y − x∥2 ≤ F (x, z) + Ej .
                                                   2
Proof. Fix an outcome and put w = Y − x ∈ W. The segment from x to Y lies in the box [ℓ, u], and
ϕ(t) = F (x + tw, z) satisfies ϕ′′ (t) = wT ∇2xx F (x + tw, z)w ≤ L̄∥w∥2 by (8.2). Taylor’s formula with
integral remainder gives ϕ(1) ≤ ϕ(0) + ϕ′ (0) + L̄∥w∥2 /2, that is, F (Y, z) ≤ F (x, z) + ∇x F (x, z)T (Y −
x) + L̄2 ∥Y − x∥2 . Take expectations and use E(Y − x) = 0 and Lemma 8.3(c).

Example 8.5 (Coordinate curvature is not enough). Let 0 < h ≤ 1/2, X = {x ∈ [0, 1]2 : x1 = x2 }
and F (x) = 2x1 x2 − h(x1 + x2 ). Both coordinate curvatures are zero, so the corrections of
Proposition 4.2 vanish. On X, F (t, t) = 2t2 − 2ht has minimum F ∗ = −h2 /2 at t = h/2, whereas
every feasible point of the mesh-h grid has value F (kh, kh) = 2h2 k(k −1) ≥ 0. Corrections computed
from coordinate curvature would therefore certify the false bound 0. The Hessian has eigenvalues
±2, so L̄ = 2 is valid with W = R2 , and the allowance nc L̄h2 /8 = h2 /2 gives the correct bound
0 − h2 /2 = F ∗ ; the allowance is attained.




                                                     45
8.3   Filtering and certificates
The tree computation of Lemma 4.3 applies to F on a grid Gj (D) after each row r is assigned to a
bag containing its support, as the table ιr (y) = 0 if Ar yx + Br yz ≤ br and ιr (y) = +∞ otherwise.
The proof of Lemma 4.3 only adds and minimizes tables, so it holds for values in R ∪ {+∞}; when
outgoing messages are formed by subtracting one incoming term from a sum, one stores the sum of
the finite terms and the number of infinite ones. For a level-j domain D it returns

      µ = min{F (y) : y ∈ X ∩ Gj (D)},         mi (t) = min{F (y) : y ∈ X ∩ Gj (D), yi = t} − Ej

for every coordinate i and every node t of that coordinate (for the kth discrete coordinate we
write mk ), and a minimizing grid point, with min ∅ = +∞, using O p(|A| + n + N + m)K p table
                                                                                        

operations, where K bounds the number of nodes per coordinate. The correction is the constant
Ej , so mi is the min-marginal of Q = F − Ej over the feasible grid points, in the notation of
Definition 4.1, and µ − Ej is the minimum of Q.

Algorithm 8.6 (TU-GRID: grids under TU coupling). Input: the data of Definition 8.1 and an
accuracy ε > 0. Set D(0) = [ℓ, u] × Z. For j = 0, 1, 2, . . . :

 (1) Compute µj , a minimizer y (j) ∈ X ∩ Gj (D(j) ), and the min-marginals mi on Gj (D(j) ). If
     µ0 = +∞, stop and report X = ∅.

 (2) Put Uj = µj and βj = µj − Ej . If Ej ≤ ε, return y (j) and [βj , Uj ].
                                                   (j+1)                                       (j)
 (3) (Union filter.) For each continuous i, let Di     be the union of all cells [t, t + hj ] of Di with
                                                                                    (j)
     min{mi (t), mi (t + hj )} ≤ Uj and of all single-point components {t} of Di with mi (t) ≤ Uj .
                       (j+1)         (j)
     For each k, let Λk      = {t ∈ Λk : mk (t) ≤ Uj }.
                                  (j+1)
The hull variant replaces each Di         by its convex hull.

Proposition 8.7 (Soundness). Consider TU-GRID or its hull variant, with or without the stopping
test of step (2). For every level j that is reached, (a), (b) and (e) hold, and (c) and (d) hold if
step (3) is executed at level j:

 (a) if X ̸= ∅ then µ0 < ∞;

 (b) βj ≤ F (v) for every v ∈ X ∩ D(j) ;

 (c) every v ∈ (X ∩ D(j) ) \ D(j+1) has F (v) > Uj ;

 (d) D(j+1) is a level-(j + 1) domain that contains S and y (j) ; hence y (j) ∈ X ∩ Gj+1 (D(j+1) ) and
     Uj+1 ≤ Uj ;

 (e) βj ≤ F ∗ ≤ Uj ≤ F ∗ + Ej and Uj − βj = Ej .

Moreover, a checker that recomputes every µj and mi from the stored domains and verifies every
filtering decision obtains βJ ≤ F ∗ for the last level J from (b) and (c) alone, without using any
minimizer, uniqueness or growth.

Proof. (a) Apply Lemma 8.3 with j = 0 to any (x, z) ∈ X; its outcomes are feasible points of
G0 (D(0) ).
    (b) Let v = (x, z) ∈ X ∩ D(j) and let Y be as in Lemma 8.3. Every outcome (Y, z) is a feasible
grid point, so µj ≤ EF (Y, z) ≤ F (x, z) + Ej by Lemma 8.4.

                                                    46
     (c) Let v = (x, z) ∈ (X ∩ D(j) ) \ D(j+1) and let Y be as in Lemma 8.3. If some value
         (j+1)
zk ∈ / Λk      , every outcome has the same value zk , so mk (zk ) ≤ F (Y, z) − Ej almost surely and
                                                                                                        (j+1)
mk (zk ) ≤ EF (Y, z) − Ej ≤ F (v); removal means mk (zk ) > Uj . Otherwise some xi ∈                 / Di     . If
                                                                                               (j)
xi ∈ hj Z, then Yi = xi almost surely and as before mi (xi ) ≤ F (v). Since xi ∈ Di , either {xi } is a
single-point component, which was removed, so mi (xi ) > Uj ; or xi is an endpoint of at least one
cell, all such cells were removed, and each removed cell has both endpoint values above Uj , so again
                                                                                          (j)
mi (xi ) > Uj . If xi ∈/ hj Z, then Yi ∈ {ti , ti + hj } for the cell [ti , ti + hj ] ⊆ Di containing xi , which
was removed, and F (v) ≥ EF (Y, z) − Ej ≥ min{mi (ti ), mi (ti + hj )} > Uj . In the hull variant the
retained set is larger, so (c) holds a fortiori.
     (d) The retained sets are finite unions of closed intervals with endpoints in hj Z ⊆ hj+1 Z,
as are their hulls. X is compact and F is continuous, so S =                 ̸ ∅ when X ̸= ∅. By induction
         (j)                             ∗          (j)
S ⊆ D ; minimizers have value F ≤ F (y ) = Uj , so by (c) they are not removed. The point
y (j) has value Uj , so it is not removed either; its continuous coordinates lie in hj Z ⊆ hj+1 Z, hence
                                                                           (j+1)        (j+1)

     (e) F ≤ Uj because y ∈ X. By (d) a minimizer lies in D , so (b) gives βj ≤ F ∗ ; rounding
            ∗                  (j)                                           (j)

that minimizer as in (b) gives µj ≤ F ∗ + Ej .
     For the last claim, let v ∈ X. If v ∈ D(J) , then F (v) ≥ βJ by (b). Otherwise v is removed at a
first level j < J, and F (v) > Uj ≥ UJ ≥ βJ by (c); the inequality Uj ≥ UJ is checked directly from
the recomputed values. Hence F ∗ ≥ βJ .

    The certificate record consists of the data, a TU certificate for A (a structural certificate such
as a network or interval representation, or a run of a polynomial-time TU recognition algorithm
[165, Chapter 20]), the curvature certificate for (8.2) (Remark E.1), η, the domains, the tables, and
the points y (j) . As in Theorem 4.7, the whole history is needed: the last grid bounds F only on
X ∩ D(J) .

8.4    Accuracy-independent node counts

Theorem 8.8 (Node counts under set growth). Assume (8.3) and put aj = nc L̄/(4gS ) hj . Run
TU-GRID with or without the stopping test of step (2). For every level j at which step (3) is
executed:
                         (j+1)                                                        (j+1)
 (a) every point of Di           lies within aj + hj of Si , and every value in Λk            lies within aj of Sk ;

  (b) if a continuous projection Si is finite, then
                                   (j+1)

                        Gj+1 (Di           ) ≤ |Si | 5 + 2 nc L̄/gS        ≤ |Si | 5 + 2 nc κc ;

  (c) in the hull variant the statement on values in (a) holds; if moreover S = {v ∗ }, every point of
        (j+1)
      Di      lies within aj + hj of vi∗ , and the bound in (b) holds.
Proof. (a) A retained cell has an endpoint t with mi (t) ≤ Uj , a retained single point {t} has
mi (t) ≤ Uj , and a retained value t has mk (t) ≤ Uj . In each case mi (t) < ∞, so there is a feasible
grid point w with wi = t and, by Proposition 8.7(e),

                                 F (w) = mi (t) + Ej ≤ Uj + Ej ≤ F ∗ + 2Ej .

By (8.3), dist(w, S)2 ≤ 2Ej /gS = a2j , so some v ∈ S has |t − vi | ≤ ∥w − v∥ ≤ aj . Points of a cell are
within hj of each of its endpoints.

                                                        47
                  (j+1)
   (b) By (a), Di     lies in the union over t ∈ Si of the intervals [t − aj − hj , t + aj + hj ]. Each
           2aj + 2hj = hj+1 (4aj /hj + 4), so it contains at most ⌊4aj /hj ⌋ + 5 points of hj+1 Z, and
has length q
4aj /hj = 2 nc L̄/gS . Finally L̄/gS ≤ κc .
     (c) In the hull variant the argument of (a) applies to the retained cells, single points and
values before the hull is taken. If S = {v ∗ }, the retained cells and points lie in the interval
[vi∗ − aj − hj , vi∗ + aj + hj ], and hence so does their hull.

    The bound is per optimal coordinate value: different coordinates may be near different minimizers,
and no optimal combination is enumerated; S can have up to i |Si | k |Zk | points. Step (3) filters
                                                                       Q   Q

by unions of cells because one coordinate can have optimal values far apart: on independent copies
of a block with two minimizers, the hull variant keeps 2j + 1 nodes in one coordinate of every
block at level j, whereas the union variant keeps a number of nodes bounded independently of j
(Example E.3 in Appendix E.2). The finite-projection hypothesis cannot be dropped for TU-GRID:
                                                                   (j)
if Si contains an interval [a, a′ ] with a < a′ , then [a, a′ ] ⊆ Di for every j by Proposition 8.7(d), so
the level-j grid has at least (a′ − a)/hj − 1 nodes in coordinate i, which grows with the accuracy.

8.5    Complexity of certified approximation
Theorem 8.9 (Certified approximation with TU coupling). Assume Definition 8.1 with factors
that are explicit rational quadratics or explicitly encoded rational polynomials of fixed degree, and let
I be the binary input length, including η, L̄ and the decomposition. For ε > 0, TU-GRID(ε) either
certifies X = ∅ at level 0, or stops at
                                                           n   l          nc L̄η   2 mo
                                                                   1
                          J = min{j ≥ 0 : Ej ≤ ε} ≤ max 0,         2 log2   8ε
with a feasible rational point and an interval [βJ , UJ ] ∋ F ∗ of width EJ ≤ ε, certified as in
Proposition 8.7. All numbers have bit length polynomial in I + J, and the number of table operations
is
                                            J        
                                                   Kjp ,
                                             X
                   O p (|A| + n + N + m)                   K0 ≤ max{KZ , s/η + 1},
                                             j=0

where Kj is the largest number of nodes per coordinate at level j. Under (8.3) with |Si | ≤ r for
                                                √
every continuous i, Kj ≤ K = max{KZ , r(5 + ⌊2 nc κc ⌋)} for every j ≥ 1. The algorithm uses
neither gS nor r.

Proof. Correctness is Proposition 8.7, and the width is UJ − βJ = EJ . The bound on J follows from
Ej = nc L̄η 2 4−j /8. The table count is the tree computation above, summed over levels; the level-0
grid has (ui − ℓi )/η + 1 ≤ s/η + 1 nodes in a continuous coordinate and at most KZ values in a
                                                                                                (j)
discrete one, and values are never added. The bound K is Theorem 8.8(b) together with |Λk | ≤ KZ .
Grid points at level j lie in hj Z ∩ [ℓ, u], so their coordinates are rationals with denominators dividing
∆η 2j and numerators bounded by ∆η 2j maxi max{|ℓi |, |ui |}. Explicit fixed-degree factors evaluated
at such points have polynomial bit length, and every table entry is a sum of at most |A| factor
values or a minimum of such sums.

    Without growth the same certificate is produced, but the node counts depend on the accuracy:
a continuous
       q     coordinate has at most s2j /η + 1 nodes at level j, and if J ≥ 1,qminimality of J gives
2J < η nc L̄/(2ε). A bag of p continuous coordinates then has fewer than (s nc L̄/(2ε) + 1)p table
entries at the last level, which is polynomial in 1/ε, not in log(1/ε).


                                                     48
    Under set growth with finitely many optimal values per coordinate, TU-GRID runs in time
polynomial in I + log(1/ε) for fixed p when the condition number κc , the box widths measured in
mesh units (s/η), and r are polynomially bounded; recall that KZ ≤ I. It is not fixed-parameter
tractable: its running time is not bounded by f (p, κc ) poly(I + log(1/ε)), because K p contains the
         p/2
factor nc and Section 8.7 shows that this factor is incurred. The level-0 grid has up to s/η + 1
nodes per coordinate, which is pseudopolynomial in the data. A larger common unit η, checked by
Proposition E.2, makes this smaller, but unless P = NP the dependence on s/η cannot be removed.
Indeed, Proposition 10.10 (Section 10.6) gives instances of Definition 8.1 with p = 3, a unique
minimizer and KZ = 2 on which approximating F ∗ within 12 is NP-hard. Their objective is affine, so
every L̄ > 0 is valid, and L̄ ≤ gS gives κc = 1; of the quantities above, only s/η is not polynomially
bounded on them.

8.6     Exact output for quadratic objectives
In this subsection F (v) = 12 v T Hv + ∇F (0)T v + F (0) is a quadratic with rational data on the model
of Definition 8.1. Let ∆ be a positive common denominator of its monomial coefficients Hii /2, Hik
(i < k), the entries of ∇F (0), and F (0), and put Ĥ = ∆H, which is integral and symmetric, with
continuous block Ĥxx and mixed block Ĥxz . For z ∈ Z the continuous part of X is the polytope
                                                                                  
                                            A                                  b − Bz
                                                          ′                                m′
      Pz = {x ∈ Rnc : Âx ≤ b̂z },   Â =  I  ∈ {0, ±1}m ×nc ,       b̂z =  u  ∈ ∆−1η Z ,
                                                                                   
                                           −I                                    −ℓ

with m′ = m + 2nc , so that {x : (x, z) ∈ X} = Pz . Put cz = Ĥxz z + ∆∇x F (0) ∈ Znc , so that
∆∇x F (x, z) = Ĥxx x + cz . The TU height constants are
              n                  o                                                            1
 CĤ = max 1, max |(Ĥxx )ik | ,     RTU = (2nc CĤ )nc ,   ΩTU = ∆(∆η RTU )2 ,   τTU =               .
                  i,k≤nc                                                                 4m′ ∆η RTU
                                                                                                   (8.4)
They are computed from the original data and have bit length polynomial in I. They take the
place of the constants R, Ω and τ of Section 6, where all rows are bound rows and the stationarity
systems are principal submatrices of the Hessian; with TU rows a Hadamard bound on the rows
of a saddle-point matrix replaces the diagonal bound. Appendix E.3 proves the analogues of
Lemma 6.3 and Corollary 6.4: some global minimizer has continuous coordinates in ρ−1 Z for an
integer 1 ≤ ρ ≤ ∆η RTU , every isolated global minimizer has this property, and F ∗ = a/W in lowest
terms with 1 ≤ W ≤ ΩTU (Corollary E.6). Proposition 6.6 therefore applies with Ω′ = ΩTU : if
β ≤ F ∗ , v ∈ X and F (v) = a/W in lowest terms with F (v) − β < 1/(ΩTU W ), then v is a global
minimizer.
    TU-EXACT (Algorithm E.7 in Appendix E.4) runs TU-GRID without the stopping test of
step (2) and snaps rows instead of coordinates. At every level j it makes active the rows of Â with
slack at most τTU at the grid minimizer y (j) , imposes stationarity along the remaining face by linear
equations, finds a point of the resulting polytope by exact linear programming, and returns that
point if it passes the acceptance test above with β = βj .

Theorem 8.10 (Exact output with TU coupling). Let F be a rational quadratic on a nonempty set
X as in Definition 8.1.
