8.7    The uniform mesh: cost and non-uniform alternatives
The running time of CT (Algorithm 5.6)   √ is bounded by f (p, κ̄) poly(I + q) because its graded grids
cover the localization radius with O( κ̄ log(n + 2)) nodes per coordinate (Section 5). With TU
                                         √
coupling the radius is aj + hj , of order nc κc hj (Theorem 8.8), but the mesh is uniform. The factor
 p/2
nc in K p is incurred: Corollary 5.11 shows that filtered uniform grids keep at least (n − 2)κ + 1
                                                                                         p

nodes per coordinate on the box instance of Proposition 5.10, and its proof applies to TU-GRID,
whose allowance Ej is at least the sum of the coordinate corrections of a uniform grid. With a
                                                                                                    p/2
redundant TU row that forces a bag of size p, TU-GRID(ε) then builds a table with at least nc
entries for every ε ≤ L̄/64, while κc = 2 (Lemma E.10 in Appendix E.6).
    Two natural non-uniform alternatives, misaligned coordinate grids and a common graded
pattern, give invalid bounds with curvature-only corrections (Proposition E.11 and Example E.12 in
Appendix E.6). With misaligned grids, the multiplier of an order constraint x1 ≤ x2 is invisible
to the curvature and to the growth constant; with a common graded pattern and a sum row, a
minimizer need not be a convex combination of feasible grid points of its cell.
Remark 8.11 (Comparison with Bienstock and Muñoz). Bienstock and Muñoz [24, Theorems 4
and 15] construct, for mixed-binary polynomial problems with variables scaled to [0, 1], a linear
objective c, constraints of degree at most π and coefficient 1-norm at most F, and        a constraint
                                                                         ω+1
                                                                                       
intersection graph of treewidth ω, a linear program with O (2π/ϵ)            n log(π/ϵ) variables and
constraints whose solutions violate each constraint by at most Fϵ and are ∥c∥1 ϵ-optimal. They also
argue that, unless P = NP, neither the pseudopolynomial dependence on 1/ϵ nor the coefficient
scaling of the violation can be removed for that class. The results here differ in four respects.
(i) Feasibility is exact, but only for TU continuous columns with aligned data; their constraints
are arbitrary polynomials. (ii) A nonlinear objective is handled directly through L̄, whereas their
objective is linear and nonlinear objectives are encoded by additional constraints, which may increase
the width. (iii) Under growth with finitely many optimal coordinate values, the per-level tables of
TU-GRID do not depend on its accuracy ε, and ε enters only through O(log(1/ε)) levels; this does
not conflict with their lower bounds, which concern their whole class and hence instances without
growth or TU structure, and the factor (nc κc )p/2 can itself be exponential in I because κc can be.
                   q if J ≥ 1, a last-level table of TU-GRID for a bag of p continuous coordinates
(iv) Without growth,
has fewer than (s nc L̄/(2ε) + 1)p entries (Section 8.5), an exponent p/2 in 1/ε against their ω + 1 in
1/ϵ for comparable decompositions; the halving comes from second-order rounding error with exact
feasibility, and the comparison is only indicative because the tolerances are normalized differently.
Neither result contains the other.

                                                  50
9     Several minimizers
The growth analysis of Section 5 localizes every retained coordinate near one minimizer. When the
optimal set S is not a single point, this localization fails, although Theorem 6.9 shows that exactness
itself costs only poly(I) + max{0, log2 (1/gS )} bits of accuracy under set growth with constant gS .
This section treats three situations. With finitely many minimizers, unions of uniform cells give
                √
grids with O(r nκS ) nodes per coordinate, where r is the largest number of optimal values of one
coordinate (Section 9.1). For coordinatewise concave quadratics, the decomposition describes the
entire optimal set (Section 9.2). For continuous box quadratics certified by a diagonal Lagrangian
multiplier, an exact minimizer and an exact description of S are found in time f ′ (p, κS ) poly(I) for
a computable function f ′ (Section 9.3). Throughout, F = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c is a rational quadratic
on a mixed box, and set growth, gS and κS = κ(L, gS ) are as in Definition 3.2. Some gS > 0 always
exists (Remark 6.10), but its size is not controlled, and no algorithm below computes it.

9.1    One center cannot serve several minimizers
Theorem 6.9 reduces exact output to reaching accuracy εS . CT (Algorithm 5.6) does not reach it at
a rate controlled by p and κS when the minimizer is not unique, even with two isolated minimizers:
its grids are graded around one center, and coarse cells near a distant minimizer keep the corrected
minimum low.

          FM (x, z) = x2 − 2xz + M z       on X = [0, M ]2 (both coordinates continuous).

Algorithm 9.2 (UC: uniform cells). The input is an accuracy ε > 0. For each coordinate keep
a finite set Ci of retained cells, closed intervals of positive length; initially Ci = {[ℓi , ui ]}. Let J
be the least j ≥ 0 with 12 nLs2 4−j ≤ ε, start with the incumbent x̂ = ℓ and U = F (ℓ), and for

  (i) Refine. Let Gij = {ℓi + khj : k ∈ Z≥0 } for i ∈ IC and Gij = {ℓi + ⌈khj ⌉ : k ∈ Z≥0 } for i ∈ IZ .
      Split every retained cell [a, a′ ] at the points of Gij ∩ (a, a′ ); the resulting intervals are the stage
      cells, and Gi is the set of their endpoints. Let wi (v) be the largest effective width of a stage
                                                                                             Q
      cell with endpoint v, and define di , D, Q, βj and mi by (4.1)–(4.2) on G = i Gi .

 (ii) Solve. Compute βj , a minimizer y (j) of Q over G and all mi by Lemma 4.3; if F (y (j) ) < U ,
      set x̂ = y (j) and U = F (y (j) ).

 (iii) Filter. Let Ci be the set of stage cells [a, a′ ] with min{mi (a), mi (a′ )} ≤ U . Do not take hulls.

Return x̂, U and βJ . The domain of stage j is Xj = X ∩               Ci , with Ci as at the start of stage j.
                                                                Q S
                                                                  i

    Since Gi,j−1 ⊆ Gij (for integers, ⌈khj−1 ⌉ = ⌈2khj ⌉), every stage cell is either a segment between
consecutive points of Gij ∩ [ℓi , ui ] or such a segment clipped at ui . Hence a continuous stage cell has
length at most hj ; an integer stage cell has length at most ⌈hj ⌉, and if that length is at least two
then hj > 1 and ⌈hj ⌉ < 2hj . Consecutive points of Gij are at least hj apart for i ∈ IC and at least
max{1, ⌊hj ⌋} ≥ hj /2 apart for i ∈ IZ . Each stage cell contains at most one point of Gi,j+1 in its
interior.

Lemma 9.3 (Uniform cells: bounds and safe filtering). Assume only Definition 3.1. At every stage
j of UC, βj ≤ minXj F , and F (x) ≥ min{mi (a), mi (a′ )} for every x ∈ Xj and every stage cell [a, a′ ]
of a coordinate i with xi ∈ [a, a′ ]. Every global minimizer lies in every Xj , and βJ ≤ F ∗ ≤ U .

Proof. The stage cells of coordinate i form a finite family of intervals with endpoints in Gi , every
node is an endpoint of one of them, wi is defined from this family, and Xj is the intersection of X
with the product of their unions. The first two claims are therefore Proposition 4.2(b) and (c) in
the form for families of intervals (Section 4). If step (iii) of stage j removes a point x ∈ Xj , then
for some i every stage cell containing xi is discarded, so F (x) > U for the threshold U used at
stage j. As U ≥ F ∗ , global minimizers are never removed; by induction every Xj contains S, and
βJ ≤ minXJ F = F ∗ ≤ U .

Theorem 9.4 (Uniform cells). Assume Definition 3.1. For every ε > 0, UC(ε) returns a feasible x̂
with βJ ≤ F ∗ ≤ F (x̂) ≤ βJ + ε, together with a certificate (all stage tables and filtering decisions)
whose validity uses only Definition 3.1. Suppose moreover that S is finite, r = maxi |Si |, and F has
set growth with constant gS . Then every grid of every stage has at most

    Work and bit length. By Lemma 4.3 each stage takes O(p(|A| + n + N )KSp ) table operations,
and J = O(log(nLs2 /ε)) = O(I + q). Continuous nodes have the form ℓi + ks2−j and integer nodes
are integers, so all nodes have denominators dividing ∆2j , with ∆ as in Section 6.1, and bit length
O(I + j); factor values, corrections and messages have bit length polynomial in I + j. For exact
output, Theorem 6.9(a) applies once 2−q ≤ εS , and log2 (1/εS ) ≤ poly(I) + log2 κS as in the proof
of Theorem 6.14 (if L = 0, UC is exact at stage 0). EX doubles q, so it makes O(log(I + log κS ))
calls, each with q ≤ poly(I) + 2 log2 κS .

Remark 9.5 (What grading buys). With r = 1 the minimizer is unique and κS = κ, and Theorem 9.4
                                           √
gives accuracy-independent grids with O( nκ) nodes  √ per coordinate without gradings or caps. The
graded single-center grids of Section 5 need only O( κ̄ log(n + 2)) nodes per coordinate (Lemma 5.5),
which gives the f (p, κ̄) poly(I + q) bound of Theorem 5.7; Proposition 9.1 shows that they lose
this bound when the minimizer is not unique. Whether exact output admits an f ′ (p, κS , r) poly(I)
bound when S is finite is open. When S is a continuum, Proposition 10.8 shows that runs of filtering
                                                                            √
with thresholds Uj ≥ F ∗ , in particular TRIAL and CT, can need 1 + 1/(2 ε) nodes per coordinate
to certify accuracy ε; whether other certificates avoid this is open.

9.2    Coordinatewise concave quadratics: every minimizer at once
Write Ei = {ℓi , ui } and E(B) = i∈B Ei . For x ∈ X̄ let Y be the random endpoint vector with

Theorem 9.7 (All minimizers of a coordinatewise concave quadratic). Under the hypothesis of
Lemma 9.6, a point x ∈ X is a global minimizer if and only if

 (a) xi ∈ {ℓi , ui } for every i with Hii < 0, and

 (b) πt (v; x) = 0 for every bag t and every v ∈ E(Bt ) with rt (v) > 0.

These are at most n + N 2p factored polynomial equations of degree at most p, together with the
original bounds and integrality. They are computed and checked in 2O(p) poly(I) bit operations,
without a growth assumption and without enumerating interior integer labels or optimal components.

Proof. By (9.3), x is optimal exactly when every nonnegative term vanishes. The diagonal terms
vanish if and only if (a) holds, and the residual terms rt (v)πt (v; x) vanish if and only if (b) holds.

    Condition (b) is a support condition, not a condition on coordinate projections. For F =
x + y − 2xy = x(1 − y) + y(1 − x) on [0, 1]2 , both optimal corners (0, 0) and (1, 1) use every endpoint
of every coordinate, yet the two positive residuals give x(1 − y) = 0 and y(1 − x) = 0, which exclude
every other point. A sum of m disjoint copies has 2m isolated minimizers and a description with
2m equations.
    The equations have a finite combinatorial form. For x ∈ X̄ define its face pattern χ(x) ∈ {L, U, ∗}n
by χi (x) = L if xi = ℓi , χi (x) = U if xi = ui , and χi (x) = ∗ otherwise. For a pattern χ on B ⊆ [n]
let cube(χ) ⊆ E(B) consist of the v with vi = ℓi where χi = L and vi = ui where χi = U. For a
pattern χ on [n], the closed face face(χ) = {x ∈ X̄ : xi = ℓi if χi = L, xi = ui if χi = U} of X̄ has
the vertex set cube(χ). Then πt (v; x) > 0 if and only if v ∈ cube(χ(x)Bt ). Let Σi = {L, U}, with ∗
added when Hii = 0 and Xi has a point strictly between ℓi and ui . Call χ ∈ i Σi admissible if, for
                                                                                  Q

every bag, cube(χBt ) ⊆ Zt := {v ∈ E(Bt ) : rt (v) = 0}.

Corollary 9.8 (Optimal faces form a tree-structured constraint problem). Under the hypothesis of
Lemma 9.6:

  (i) x ∈ X is optimal if and only if χ(x) ∈
                                               Q
                                                   i Σi and χ(x) is admissible.

 (ii) Replacing any ∗ of an admissible pattern by L or U keeps it admissible, and S = χ admissible X∩
                                                                                      S
             
      face(χ) . Distinct admissible patterns give disjoint nonempty sets {x ∈ X : χ(x) = χ}.

(iii) Admissible patterns are the solutions of a constraint problem on the same tree decomposition,
      with at most three labels per variable and bag relations Admt = {χ ∈ i∈Bt Σi : cube(χ) ⊆ Zt }.
                                                                          Q

      Consequently, in 2O(p) (N + n) poly(I) bit operations one can decide whether the minimizer is
      unique, compute the dimension of S, count the admissible patterns, count |S| when S is finite,
      compute dist(y, S)2 and a nearest minimizer for a rational y, and minimize a linear function
      over S.

   The proof is in Appendix F.




                                                     54
Remark 9.9 (Two-label integer coordinates      and multilinear objectives). If Xi = {ℓi , ℓi + 1}, replacing
1      2     1
                                            
2 Hii xi by 2 Hii (2ℓi + 1)xi − ℓi (ℓi + 1) does not change F on X. Hence the sign hypothesis is
needed only for coordinates with at least three feasible values; all binary quadratic programs are
                                                     P       P
covered. The proofs also apply verbatim to F = a fa + i ψi (xi ) in which each fa is affine in each
of its variables separately and each ψi is concave on [ℓi , ui ]. Then Efa (Y ) = fa (x), and the diagonal
term of (9.3) becomes ψi (xi ) minus its chord at xi . That difference vanishes at an interior point
exactly when ψi is affine on [ℓi , ui ], which replaces the condition Hii < 0 in (a).
     Endpoint optimality for nonpositive diagonals is classical. For multilinear functions on a box,
Rosenberg characterized the optimal set as the union of the faces all of whose vertices are optimal
[161]. For a quadratic on a continuous box with Hii = 0 for all i, this is Corollary 9.8(ii), because by
(i) a pattern is admissible exactly when all vertices of its face are optimal. Del Pia and Khajavirad
use endpoint optimality to separate binary-valued from continuous variables [53]. Min-sum messages
are standard [50], and nonnegative reparameterizations whose zero entries describe all optimal
labelings of a discrete problem are known from max-sum inference [183, 187]. Theorem 9.7 combines
these facts and extends them to strictly concave coordinates and mixed boxes. The new part is
the factored form on the decomposition: Corollary 9.8 turns the optimal set into a tree-structured
pattern problem, which gives uniqueness, dimension, counts, distances and linear optimization over
S with 2O(p) operations per bag.

9.3    The diagonal Lagrangian certificate and unknown growth
In this subsection X = X̄ is a continuous box and F is a rational quadratic. A point x̄ ∈ X is a box
KKT point if ∂i F (x̄) ≥ 0 when x̄i = ℓi , ∂i F (x̄) ≤ 0 when x̄i = ui , and ∂i F (x̄) = 0 otherwise; every
minimizer is one. For such a point put

Lemma 9.10 (Diagonal certificate, full optimal set and invariance). Let X be a continuous box
and F a quadratic.

  (i) For every box KKT point x̄ and every x ∈ Rn ,

                 
            S = x ∈ X : (H + Λ(x̄))(x − x̄) = 0, λi (x̄)(xi − ℓi )(ui − xi ) = 0 for all i .           (9.5)

(iii) Ψ(λ) ≤ F ∗ for every λ ≥ 0, and the following are equivalent: (a) H + Λ(x̄) ⪰ 0 for some
      x̄ ∈ S; (b) H + Λ(x̄) ⪰ 0 for every x̄ ∈ S; (c) Ψ(λ) = F ∗ for some λ ≥ 0. When they hold,
      the vector λ(x̄) is the same for every x̄ ∈ S, and it is the unique maximizer of Ψ.

                                                        55
The proof is in Appendix F.

   We write D for the class of continuous box QPs that satisfy the equivalent conditions (a)–(c) of
Lemma 9.10(iii), and call it the diagonal certificate class.
Remark 9.11 (The diagonal certificate class). Convex box QPs belong to D, since Λ(x̄) ⪰ 0. The
class also contains indefinite instances with tilted and disconnected optimal sets (Example F.1 in
Appendix F). The function Ψ is the Lagrangian dual for the constraints (xi − ℓi )(ui − xi ) ≥ 0,
so condition (c) says that this dual, equivalently the basic Shor semidefinite relaxation, is exact.
Sufficient global optimality conditions of this box-multiplier form are classical [106, 125], and
stronger relaxations with complete exactness characterizations are studied in [159]. Lemma 9.10 is
therefore not a new certificate. What we use is its invariance: whichever minimizer a search finds,
the same rational test accepts it.
    Lemma 9.10 checks a candidate but does not produce one. Candidates come from proximal
stages: graded grids centered at the previous output, with a proximal term instead of filtering, and
a guess κ̂ in place of the unknown κS .

Algorithm 9.12 (PROX: proximal stages with guess κ̂). The input is a rational

and grading θ, where X ′ = X ∩ i [ci − ϱh, ci + ϱh]. It returns a minimizer y + over G = i Gi of

    Let Kθ = 8θ−1 ⌈log2 (n + 2)⌉. Every grid of PROX(κ̂) has at most Kθ nodes per coordinate, and

Algorithm 9.13 (DISC: discovery under unknown growth). For κ̂ = 1, 2, 4, . . . : let Jκ̂ be the least
j ≥ 0 with 4j τ 2 ≥ 4κ̂ns2 (τ as in REC, Algorithm 6.7); run PROX(κ̂) for stages 0, . . . , Jκ̂ and let
y = y (Jκ̂ ) . Run REC(y); if it does not fail, let x̄ be a vertex of the polytope of solutions of its linear
system, as returned by exact linear programming. If x̄ is a box KKT point and H + Λ(x̄) ⪰ 0, stop
and output x̄, F ∗ = F (x̄), Λ(x̄) and the description (9.5) of S; otherwise continue with the next
guess.

Theorem 9.14 (Discovery of a certified optimal set under unknown growth). Let X be a continuous
box and F a rational quadratic.

 (a) Every output of DISC is correct, with no assumption on the instance. If F ∈
                                                                               / D, DISC does
     not stop.

  (b) If F ∈ D, then F has set growth with some constant gS > 0, and for every such gS , DISC
      stops no later than at the first guess κ̂ ≥ κS , so with κ̂ ≤ 2κS .

  (c) Under the hypotheses of (b), DISC uses at most f ′ (p, κS ) poly(I) bit operations, where f ′ is a
      computable function that is nondecreasing and polynomial in κS for fixed p, and the polynomial
      in I has an absolute exponent. The output has bit length poly(I).

Neither S, nor gS , nor a bound on κS is supplied.


                                                     56
    The proof is in Appendix F. Since DISC does not stop outside D, an implementation needs a
work limit, and an unsuccessful guess certifies nothing about membership (Remark F.3).
    The optimal value of an instance of D equals max Ψ, the value of a convex relaxation; the
difficulty is in locating a minimizer. The Subset Sum reduction of [53, Remark 2], written with
unscaled partial sums, has bag size three, and its feasible instances belong to D. Hence, unless
P = NP, no polynomial-time algorithm returns an exact minimizer for every instance of D with
bag size three, and no choice of set-growth constants bounds κS by a polynomial in I on these
instances (Proposition F.4 in Appendix F); a parameter such as κS is needed. Deciding whether a
given minimizer of an instance of D with bag size three is the only one is coNP-hard (Appendix F),
so unless P = NP Corollary 9.8 has no counterpart in D.


10      Structural limits
The algorithms of Sections 4–6 rest on four hypotheses: a product domain, upper bounds on
coordinate curvature, weighted or point growth, and a correction that charges for rounding every
coordinate. Sections 7–9 relax some of them: Section 7 replaces the corrections of eliminated
coordinates by exact or certified value functions, Section 8 replaces the product domain by a feasible
rounding law for totally unimodular constraints, and Sections 8 and 9 use set growth instead of
growth at one minimizer. This section proves Theorem 1.2 and shows what fails when a hypothesis
is dropped without such a replacement. Section 10.1 proves part (a), Section 10.2 part (d), and
Section 10.3 parts (b) and (c). Section 10.4 shows that exact messages can be exponentially large;
Section 10.5, that set growth does not bound the grids produced by filtering, and that under set
growth a deterministic algorithm that queries values and derivatives needs at least of order ε−n/2
queries; Section 10.6, that product domains are essential; and Section 10.7, that bag-local corrections
combined with grid min-marginals are not valid lower bounds, and that matching separator moments
of any finite order gives no local error bound in terms of negative curvature.
    Del Pia and Khajavirad prove that continuous box QP is strongly NP-hard at treewidth two,
even with bounded integer coefficients, and that quartic box problems on paths are strongly NP-hard
[53]. Their instances need not have a unique minimizer, so their result does not by itself show that
κ must enter the running time.

10.1    Conditioning cannot be dropped
An instance of Subset Sum consists of positive integers a1 , . . . , am and a target a0 ; it asks whether
some subset of {a1 , . . . , am } sums to a0 . The following construction refines the simple weak NP-
hardness reduction in [53, Remark 2]: a stronger concave penalty puts every minimizer at a vertex,
a lexicographic linear term makes the minimizer unique, and the growth constant is computed
explicitly.
Proposition 10.1 (Unique minimizers without conditioning). Let a1 , . . . , am be positive integers,

Corollary 10.2 (The dependence on κ is not polylogarithmic). Unless P = NP, no algorithm
computes, for every rational continuous box QP with a supplied tree decomposition of maximum
bag size at most three and a unique minimizer, the optimal value within 2−q in time polynomial
in I + q + log κ. In particular, no algorithm achieves the guarantee of Theorem 5.7 with f (3, κ)
bounded by a polynomial in log κ.

Proof. Apply such an algorithm to the instances of Proposition 10.1 with q = ⌈log2 (4m)⌉. An
approximation within 1/(4m) separates F ∗ < 1/(2m) from F ∗ ≥ 1/m, and log κ = O(I). This
would decide Subset Sum, which is NP-complete [75], in polynomial time.
                                               √
    For fixed p, the factor f (p, κ) = c0 (c1 p κ)p κ(1 + log2 κ)2 in Theorem 5.7 is bounded by a
polynomial in κ, and the proof of Theorem 6.14 bounds f1 (p, κ) by f (p, κ) times a power of
1 + log2 κ. Hence, for fixed p, instances with a unique minimizer and κ ≤ I O(1) are solved exactly in
polynomial time, while by Proposition 10.1 the problem is NP-hard on instances with κ ≤ 2O(I) .
Adding decoupled terms t2j with tj ∈ [0, 1], each in a bag of its own, keeps the minimizer unique,
leaves p and κ unchanged, and increases I by an amount of our choice; so for every fixed δ > 0 the
                                                        δ
problem is NP-hard already on instances with κ ≤ 2I .
    The strongly NP-hard instances of [53, Theorem 3] have bags of size three and coefficients
bounded by absolute constants. Remark G.1 in Appendix G.1 shows that, already for a family of
two-item instances on which their minimizer is unique, κ and the ratio ν/g of negative curvature to
growth are exponential in the number of variables. These instances are therefore consistent with
Corollary 10.2, and they do not contradict an algorithm parameterized by κ or by ν/g.

10.2    The value-oracle model
CT (Algorithm 5.6) uses only exact values of F and the curvature bounds. In a model where F is
available only through evaluations, the exponent p/2 of κ in its running time cannot be improved.
The construction is the classical hidden-well argument [146], adapted to the present hypotheses.

Proposition 10.3 (Value-oracle lower bound). Let p ≥ 1, L > 0, κ ≥ 8p, g = L/κ, and X = [0, 1]p .
For c ∈ X put
                                              n      L         o
                                 Fc (x) = min gp, ∥x − c∥2 .
                                                     2
Each Fc has upper coordinate curvature L, the unique minimizer c with Fc (c) = 0, and growth
Fc (x) ≥ g∥x − c∥2 on X; a single bag of size p is a valid decomposition. Suppose a deterministic
algorithm receives p, L, κ and value access to Fc for an unknown c, and always returns y ∈ X

                                                 59
with Fc (y) < Lp/κ. Then on some Fc it makes at least (κ/(8p))p/2 − 1 evaluations. A randomized
algorithm that makes at most Q evaluations succeeds, for some c, with probability at most (Q +
1)(8p/κ)p/2 .
Proof. On a coordinate line, L2 ∥x − c∥2 − L2 x2i is affine in xi and gp − L2 x2i is concave; their minimum
is concave. Thus t 7→ Fc − Lt2 /2 is concave on every coordinate line. For x ∈ X, ∥x − c∥2 ≤ p, so
gp ≥ g∥x − c∥2 ; and L/2 ≥ g because κ ≥ 2. This proves growth, and c is the unique zero.
    Moreover Fc (x) = gp unless ∥x − c∥ < r, where r2 = 2gp/L = 2p/κ. Since κ ≥ 8p, 2r ≤ 1. Let
C consist of the points of X whose coordinates lie in {0, 2r, 4r, . . .}. Then |C| = (⌊1/(2r)⌋ + 1)p ≥
(2r)−p = (κ/(8p))p/2 , and the open balls B(c, r), c ∈ C, are pairwise disjoint. The guarantee
Fc (y) < Lp/κ = gp means y ∈ B(c, r).
    Consider the run of the deterministic algorithm in which every answer equals gp, with evaluation
points x1 , x2 , . . . (finitely or infinitely many). For c ∈ C let tc be the index of the first evaluation
point in B(c, r), and tc = ∞ if there is none. Since Fc = gp outside B(c, r), the run on Fc agrees
with the constant run up to and including evaluation tc . If tc = ∞, the two runs coincide, so the
constant run stops and its output lies in B(c, r); as the balls are disjoint, this happens for at most
one c. Each point lies in at most one ball, so the finite tc are distinct. At least |C| − 1 of them are
finite, so the largest is at least |C| − 1, and the run on the corresponding Fc makes at least |C| − 1
evaluations.
    For a randomized algorithm, choose c uniformly in C and fix the random bits. Consider the first
Q evaluation points of the run on the constant answers. If B(c, r) contains none of them, the run
on Fc agrees with the constant run on these evaluations; since it makes at most Q evaluations, it
stops there with the output of the constant run, and it succeeds only if that output lies in B(c, r).
Hence at most Q + 1 choices of c succeed. Averaging over the random bits gives success probability
at most (Q + 1)/|C| for a uniform c, hence for some c.

Remark 10.4 (Comparison with the algorithm). With one bag (n = p, N = 1), each stage of CT
                                                                                       √
evaluates F at most at K p grid points. By Lemma 5.5 and the trial schedule, K = O( κ log(p + 2))
nodes per coordinate in the successful trial, and the earlier trials cost no more. Thus the exponent
p/2 of κ is attained, and Proposition 10.3 shows that it cannot be improved in this model; the
                             √
remaining gap is a factor (C p log(p + 2))p times the number of stages. The lower bound is also
exponential in p once κ ≥ 32p.

10.3    Width and the exponent of κ
For explicit quadratics we use ETH and rETH, as stated before Theorem 1.2, and the strong
exponential-time hypothesis (SETH): for every ϵ > 0 there is a k such that no algorithm decides
                                      ′
k-SAT on n′ variables in time (2 − ϵ)n [44, Chapter 14]. The next construction is a variant of the
box formulation of maximum independent set [1], with a binary tie-break that makes the minimizer
unique.
Proposition 10.5 (Exponential dependence on the width at bounded conditioning). For a graph
with vertex set [n], n ≥ 1, and edge set E let

is a decomposition of Ψ. Consequently, computing F ∗ within 1/2 for box quadratics with κ ≤ 2

excludes running times (2 − ϵ)p I O(1) for every ϵ > 0, by the lower bound of [127] for independent set.
    If L = 0, CT is exact at its first stage, whose grids consist of box endpoints, and it runs in
time 2p poly(I). The multilinear part Ψ0 of the instance above has L = 0, the same unique binary
minimizer, and growth constant g = 1/n by (10.4), so κ = 1; the same reduction applies to it. For
L = 0 the dependence on p is therefore optimal up to the base of the exponential under ETH, and
optimal under SETH.
    The next proposition concerns the exponent of κ. Its reduction uses random weights, so it needs
rETH.
Proposition 10.6 (The exponent of κI cannot be o(p)). Assume rETH. There are no computable
f and computable nondecreasing unbounded ψ ≥ 1 such that some algorithm, on every integer box
quadratic with a supplied tree decomposition of maximum bag size p and a unique minimizer, outputs
a feasible point within 1/2 of F ∗ in time f (p) (κI)p/ψ(p) . In particular, no algorithm has running
time f (p)κa(p) I C with a computable f , a constant C, and a(p) ≤ p/ψ(p) for such a ψ. The bound
of Theorem 5.7 has a(p) = p/2 + O(1) and C = 5.
    The proof is in Appendix G.2. It encodes multicolored clique in the standard way, with one
integer variable of domain size N0 per color class and one pair constraint for each pair of classes.
What is new is a quadratic encoding of the pair constraints with a unique minimizer, obtained by
random weights and isolation, and with κ polynomial in kN0 . Under rETH, therefore, the exponent
p/2 + O(1) of κ in Theorem 5.7 cannot be improved to o(p) on integer instances, in the sense
a(p) ≤ p/ψ(p), while the degree in I stays fixed. The fixed degree matters: on the hard instances
both κ and I are bounded by a polynomial in kN0 , so the reduction cannot separate the exponent of
κ from the degree in I, and it does not exclude a bound such as κ3 I p . The constant in the exponent,

                                                    61
the necessity of the factor pp , and whether the same holds for purely continuous instances or with a
deterministic reduction, are open.

10.4    Exact messages can be exponentially large
For forests, exact value functions of box QP are piecewise quadratic with linearly many pieces
[53]. Dynamic programming with exact piecewise-quadratic messages on trees is also used for
total-variation problems [116, 120]; for nonconvex piecewise-quadratic costs the worst-case bound
of [120] is exponential. The next proposition gives a lower bound for the message itself: at bag
size three, exact messages can have exponentially many pieces even when κ is bounded and the
minimizer is unique. This is why CT uses grids rather than exact piecewise-quadratic messages.

Proposition 10.7 (Exponential messages at bounded conditioning). For m ≥ 2 let ξt ∈ [0, 2t − 1]
and zt ∈ [0, 1], t = 1, . . . , m, put ξ0 := 0, ρt = ξt − 2ξt−1 − zt , and
                                                 m                m
                                           2
                                                 X             1X
                              Ψm (ξ, z) = ξm +         ρ2t +         zt (1 − zt ).
                                                 t=1
                                                               8 t=1

Then:

   1. the bags {ξt−1 , ξt , zt } (ξ0 omitted) form a path decomposition with p = 3, and I = O(m2 );

   2. Ψm (ξ, z) ≥ 81 (∥ξ∥2 + ∥z∥2 ) on the box, so zero is the unique minimizer and g = 1/8 is valid;

   3. L = 10 is the exact maximum coordinate curvature, so κ ≤ 80; moreover ∇2 Ψm ⪰ − 41 I;

   4. let V (v) = min{Ψm (ξ, z) : ξm = v} for v ∈ [0, 2m − 1], the value function of ξm . Up to the
      term v 2 , it is the message sent across the separator {ξm } to a leaf bag {ξm } carrying ξm
                                                                                                 2 . If
           m
      [0, 2 − 1] is covered by Npc intervals on each of which V coincides with a quadratic polynomial,
      or if V = minl≤Npc φl for quadratic polynomials φl , then Npc ≥ 2m−1 .

By Theorem 5.7, CT nevertheless solves this family to accuracy 2−q in time polynomial in m + q.

     The proof is in Appendix G.3. For part 4, V (v) − v 2 vanishes exactly at the 2m integers of
[0, 2m − 1] and is positive elsewhere, so each quadratic piece agrees with V at no more than two of
them.

10.5    Point growth cannot be replaced by set growth
Sections 8 and 9 use set growth (Definition 3.2). The next proposition shows that set growth alone
does not bound the grids that filtering produces.

Proposition 10.8 (Set growth does not bound filtered grids). For n ≥ 2 let F (x) = n−1    i=1 (xi −xi+1 )
                                                                                                         2
                                                                                       P

on X = [0, 1]n , with the path decomposition of bags {xi , xi+1 }. The optimal set is the diagonal
S = {t1 : t ∈ [0, 1]}, F ∗ = 0, and F (x) ≥ n(n−1)2
                                                       dist(x, S)2 ; for n = 2, F = 2 dist(x, S)2 . Every
valid upper coordinate curvature satisfies Li ≥ 2, and with the smallest valid ones, κS ≤ 2n(n − 1).
Let the corrections be di (v) = Li wi (v)2 /8 (Definition 4.1).

 (a) For every grid G of X, every j and every node v ∈ Gj , the min-marginal satisfies mj (v) ≤
       L                               L
     − 8j wj (v)2 . In particular β ≤ − 8j Wj2 , where Wj is the largest length of a grid interval of Gj .



                                                   62
  (b) The filtering step (Definition 4.4), applied to a grid of X with a threshold U ≥ F ∗ , removes
      nothing. Hence every run of filtering with thresholds Uj ≥ F ∗ that starts from a grid of X, in
      particular every run of TRIAL (Algorithm 5.3) and hence of CT, uses grids of X at all stages,
                                                                         √
      and if it certifies accuracy ε, its last grid has at least 1 + 1/(2 ε) nodes in every coordinate.
                                                                                                       (0)
  (c) In every valid path certificate (Definition 4.6) with F (x̂) − β ≤ ε, every grid interval of Gi
                                  (1)                          (0)                               √
      that is not contained in Xi (every grid interval of Gi if k = 0) has length at most 2 ε.
    The proof is in Appendix G.3. For (a), it sets the other coordinates, outward along the path
from j, to the grid node nearest to the previous coordinate; because Li ≥ 2, the correction of each
such coordinate is at least the square term of its edge to the previous one.
    Part (b) covers the grids that CT returns. A general path certificate may remove intervals
whose endpoint min-marginals lie in [β, 0), and then its last grid can be small; for such certificates,
part (c) bounds only the stage-0 grid. For n = 2 and ε = 1/50, the uniform grid of step 1/5 on
X, followed by the box [0, 1/5]2 with the grid {0, 1/5}2 , β = −1/50 and x̂ = 0, is a valid path
certificate of accuracy ε: all stage-0 min-marginals equal −1/50. Its last grid has two nodes per
coordinate, while (b) requires at least five for runs of filtering, and its removed intervals have length
         √
1/5 ≤ 2 ε. We do not know whether every valid path certificate for this family has ε−Ω(1) nodes
in total. For finitely many optimal values per coordinate, unions of uniform cells give grids with
    √
O(r nκS ) nodes per coordinate (Theorem 9.4). The proposition concerns a continuum of optimal
coordinate values. It is a limit of filtered grids, not of the problem: here F is a sum of squares.
Proposition 10.9 (Value-oracle barrier for unknown set growth). Fix n ≥ 1, X = [0, 1]n and the
coordinate-curvature bound L = 1. Consider a deterministic algorithm that queries exact values,
gradients and Hessians of an unknown C 2 function f at adaptively chosen points and outputs either
a rational interval of width at most ε containing minX f , or a point x̂ with f (x̂) − minX f ≤ ε.
Suppose it is correct for every C 2 function f with ∂ii f ≤ 1 on X for all i that has set growth,
f (x) − minX f ≥ gS dist(x,
                       √ arg minn X
                                     f )2 on X for some gS > 0. Then on f ≡ 0, for which κS = 1,
it makes at least 1/(2 6ε) − 1 − 1 queries.
    The proof, in Appendix G.3, hides a C 2 bump of depth greater than ε in a subcube that
contains none of the points queried on f ≡ 0. This is the classical hidden-bump argument of
information-based complexity [146], adapted to set growth. Under point growth, CT uses a number
of nodes per coordinate that does not depend on the accuracy, so its number of evaluations grows
with the accuracy only through the number of stages, which is linear in log(1/ε) (Algorithm 5.6
and Remark 10.4). Under growth towards an unknown set, Proposition 10.9 shows that every
deterministic algorithm that queries values, gradients and Hessians needs Ω(ε−n/2 ) queries, already
for n = p = 1 on an instance with κS = 1. The barrier concerns information, not the input class:
the bumps are not quadratics. An algorithm for explicit quadratics must therefore exploit the
coefficients, for example through a certificate such as Lemma 9.10, or the structure of Section 9.2.

10.6    Product domains are essential
The next construction is the running-sum encoding of Subset Sum of Bienstock and Muñoz [24,
Appendix A]; see also [38, Example 1.1]. What we add is a unique minimizer and the growth
constant, so that κ = 1. An extra item x0 , whose size is the target, keeps the feasible set nonempty.
Proposition 10.10 (Local affine constraints). Let a0 , a1 , . . . , am be positive integers with ai ≤ a0 .
Consider binary x0 , . . . , xm , continuous y0 , . . . , ym+1 ∈ [0, 1], and the constraints
                                                                     ai
                   y0 = 0,          ym+1 = 1,          yi+1 = yi + xi (i = 0, . . . , m),
                                                                     a0

                                                   63
with objective F = 2m+1 x0 + m         i−1 x . Each constraint lies in the bag {y , x , y
                                                                                 i i i+1 }, and these
                               P
                                  i=1 2     i
bags form a path with p = 3. The feasible set is nonempty, the minimizer z ∗ is unique, and
F (z) − F ∗ ≥ ∥z − z ∗ ∥2 /(2m + 3) for every feasible z. Since F is affine, every L > 0 is valid; with
L = 1/(2m + 3), κ = 1. Moreover x∗0 = 0 if and only if some subset of {a1 , . . . , am } sums to a0 .
Consequently, unless P = NP, no algorithm solves such problems in time f (p, κ) poly(I) for any
function f , even when the optimal value is required only within 1/2.

    The proof, in Appendix G.3, is a direct check: the constraints force m
                                                                             P
                                                                              i=0 ai xi = a0 , and the
weights 2i−1 and 2m+1 give distinct feasible points distinct integer values.
    In the product setting an affine objective is solved exactly by endpoint dynamic programming.
The difficulty here comes entirely from the constraints. Independent coordinate rounding, the basis
of Proposition 4.2, does not preserve the running-sum equations.
    After scaling y by a0 , the instances of Proposition 10.10 satisfy Definition 8.1 with a path
constraint matrix, mesh unit η = 1, box widths s = a0 and two values per discrete coordinate;
the objective is affine, so every L̄ > 0 is valid, and L̄ ≤ gS gives κc = 1. By the discussion after
Theorem 8.9, the pseudopolynomial level-0 grid of TU-GRID (Algorithm 8.6) therefore cannot be
removed unless P = NP.

10.7    Local corrections and local moments
Proposition C.2 in Appendix C shows that a correction charged only to the coordinates of one bag,
while the other coordinates are optimized on the grid, cannot be valid for any fixed allowance: the
missing error grows with the number of outside coordinates and depends on the separator value.
The valid local alternative is exact or certified conditional recourse (Theorem 7.4).
    A different route to a local error bound keeps the exact positive curvature and discretizes only
the negative part: for a continuous box quadratic with ∇2 F ⪰ −νI one would like an error bound
in which L is replaced by ν. Such a bound holds for relaxations that condition all coordinates on
one global cell (Lemma G.3), but a global cell index can take K n values. Sparse relaxations keep
exact information only on bags and match moments across separators, cell by cell (Definition G.4).
Proposition G.5 shows that this does not suffice for any fixed order k of the matched moments:
for every k there are instances with bag size three, a unique minimizer, ∇2 F ⪰ −2I and a growth
constant that depends only on k, whose order-k relaxations have feasible points more than 1/8 below
F ∗ that occupy only cells of arbitrarily small length. A ν-based local error bound must therefore
use information beyond finite-order separator moments, for example inequalities that couple both
sides of a separator. Appendix G.4 gives the definitions, the construction and the proofs.


11     Implementation and computational illustration
The experiments in this section test whether the predicted grid sizes appear in practice and whether
the certificates are cheap enough to check. They are not a benchmark of competitive performance:
the implementation is a reference code in exact rational arithmetic, and all instance families with
continuous coordinates are synthetic (planted, random or constructed). The only library instances
we ran are binary (Section 11.8), so we do not test whether modelled instances with continuous
nonconvex terms have small width and a moderate condition number. Appendix H contains the
tables and details that this section summarizes.




                                                  64
11.1    Implementation
The reference implementation is written in Python with exact rational arithmetic (Python’s
fractions module); floating-point input is rejected. It handles explicit box QPs 12 xT Hx + bT x + c
with continuous and integer coordinates, supplied or heuristically computed (minimum-fill) tree
decompositions with arbitrary branching, and empty separators. It implements the corrected grids
of Section 4 with corrections max{Hii , 0}w2 /8, the two-node grids {ℓi , ui } for coordinates with
Hii ≤ 0, two-pass dynamic programming with all min-marginals, filtering, and CT (Algorithm 5.6)
in the common-mesh form of Lemma 5.9. It differs from the analysis in seven ways, none of which
affects validity (Appendix H lists them). In particular, all coordinates share the mesh hj = s2−j , so
the analysis that applies is Lemma 5.9 with the Euclidean condition number κ, not Theorem 5.7;
each trial starts with the center at the current incumbent, which exact coordinate descent provides
before stage 0 and may improve after every stage; and EX (Algorithm 6.13) uses a height constant
never smaller than the one in (6.1), starts at q = 4, doubles q after each round and restarts the grid
solver in every round. The “uniform” grids below are the grids of Definition 5.1 with θ = 0 around
one center, not the unions of cells of UC (Algorithm 9.2).
    Experiments E1–E3 below run single trials, that is, TRIAL (Algorithm 5.3) with the common
mesh, a fixed grading θ (θ = 0 for uniform grids), no cap on the nodes per coordinate and a fixed
number of stages, in E1 also without filtering; the “theorem” grading is the largest 2−µ with 8κθ2 ≤ 1
for the certified upper bound on κ, which the algorithm itself does not know. Experiment E4 runs
EX; the default schedule of E2, experiments E5 and E6, the supplementary experiment S1, the
chain and the recourse runs use CT. In E2, E5, E6 and the chain no trial was aborted by the cap:
the only restarts occurred in E2, and each followed a trial that reached its stage limit.
    Certificates store the grids, the directed messages and the filtering decisions of every stage,
which is more than Theorem 4.7 requires. A separate checker recomputes every table and message,
checks the Bellman equalities, the min-marginals, every exclusion, the incumbent values and the
final status, and never calls the grid generator, the dynamic program or the filtering code of the
solver; it shares model parsing, decomposition validation and objective evaluation with the solver.
The checker is independently written, not formally verified. On the runs of E1–E6, the chain and
the plain-grid recourse runs below with a solve time of at least 0.1 seconds, replay took between
0.81 and 2.54 times as long as the solve. Code, inputs, raw results and reproduction scripts (one
command per experiment group) accompany the paper.
    All runs below used one thread per process and at most four processes on an Intel Xeon w5-2565X
under Windows Subsystem for Linux (WSL2) with Python 3.13, on a machine shared with unrelated
long-running jobs (load average 11 to 19). Timings are single measurements and indicative only.
Every certificate on which a result in Sections 11.2–11.7 rests was replayed by its checker, and
every replay was valid; the CT runs of S1 that only measure when the gap falls below a threshold
(Section 11.4) were not replayed.

11.2    Accuracy independence, conditioning and dimension
The planted family consists of nonconvex quadratics on [−1, 1]n with Hessian H = 2I + C, where C
has path, random-tree or band sparsity. About a quarter of the coordinates are at a bound at the
planted minimizer x∗ , all with a strictly inward derivative of the same size; the other coordinates of
x∗ are of the form k/21 with |k| ≤ 10, and on them the Hessian block HJ0 J0 has smallest eigenvalue
4/κtarget , up to a rounding of the coupling scale to a multiple of 2−12 . Entries of C that touch an
active coordinate have size 2 to 4, and H is indefinite in every instance (its smallest eigenvalue is at
most −1.16). Parts (a) and (b) of Lemma 3.3 bracket κ to within 12% (Appendix H). The family is

11.3                                   The expanding-box chain
Table 3 reports CT on the family Ψm of Proposition 10.7 (Example 5.13) for target gap 10−3 . The
exact Bellman message for ξm would need at least 2m−1 quadratic pieces, but the largest coordinate
grid grows only from 5 to 11 nodes as n grows from 4 to 128, and the number of stages grows
linearly in m, because the largest box width, 2m − 1, enters the stage count logarithmically. All
runs succeeded in the first trial (θ = 1/4), although 8κθ2 ≤ 1 fails: Ψm = 1 at the point with z1 = 1
and all other coordinates 0, so every growth constant is at most 1 and κ ≥ 10. The minimizer 0 is
the lower corner of the box, the solver’s first descent start. Started at the upper corner instead,
the runs used the same numbers of stages, the same largest grid (except for m = 2: 6 instead of 5
nodes) and between 2% fewer and 8% more table entries.

11.4                                   Exact output and localized acceptance
Experiment E4 ran EX on twenty small instances: twelve random unplanted mixed-integer QPs
(n = 3, . . . , 6, one or two integer coordinates), three with two isolated minimizers, three planted
instances (n = 6) and two with segments of minimizers, with limits of 3,000 stages and 30 seconds
(5 seconds for the last two). An independent enumeration of integer assignments and continuous

                                                                                                                         67
faces gives the reference optima. Exact output was certified for 18 instances, all with the correct
value and an optimal point. Three of them (n = 3) have no continuous coordinate with positive
curvature and needed at most one stage. Because the wrapper doubles q and restarts in every round,
the number of stages of the other 15 is close to the precision q of the last round, a power of two
near the required number of bits log2 (Ω′ W ), where W is the denominator of the optimal value and
Ω′ ≥ Ω the implementation’s height constant: 67 to 534 stages for 49 to 342 bits (Appendix H);
with Ω′ = Ω from (6.1), 21 to 315 bits would suffice. The two instances with segments of minimizers
stopped at the time limit with certified gaps of about 2−11 , against about 2−53 required. They have
set growth (Remark 6.10) but no point growth, so Theorem 6.14 guarantees only termination; their
objective contains a(x0 − x1 )2 , a multiple of the instance of Proposition 10.8 with n = 2, on which
                                                                     √
every CT run that certifies accuracy ε ends with at least 1 + 1/(2 ε) nodes per coordinate.

S1: localized acceptance. The supplementary experiment S1 tests the localized acceptance rule
of Proposition 6.17 on 30 further random instances generated as in E4, with n = 4, 6, 8, 12, 16. After
every stage of one replayed CT run with ε = 2−60 , the test was applied to the face candidate of
Definition 6.18, which Corollary 6.19 covers, and to the stationary point of the face of the stage
incumbent. Both accepted the same 29 instances, the face candidate within nine stages and the
incumbent rule within five; on 12 of them the incumbent rule passes even with X in place of the
narrowed box, that is, without the filtering history. The accepted values agreed with those of EX on
all 29 instances, and on the 12 instances with n ≤ 6 also with the enumeration. The one failure has
two optimal vertices that differ in a concave coordinate, a tie outside the scope of Corollary 6.19.
EX, as implemented, used up to 542 stages on these instances. An optimal candidate passes the
test of Proposition 6.6 once the certified gap is below 1/(Ω′ W ); on the five instances on which EX
used the most stages, one CT run reached this threshold after 40 to 72 stages with Ω′ = Ω and 139
to 157 with the implementation’s constant.

11.5    Comparison with a global solver
Experiment E5 compares CT with SCIP 10.0 [98, 179] on 21 planted instances with κtarget = 4
(κ ∈ [3.99, 4.45]), with n = 8 to 128 and bag sizes 2 to 4. SCIP ran single-threaded on the epigraph
formulation min{t : t ≥ F (x)}, with the coefficients rounded to double precision, absolute gap 10−6
and a 20-second limit. CT used ε = 10−6 and a 60-second limit; it reached a replayed gap below
10−6 on all 21 instances in its first trial, in at most 6.9 seconds. SCIP reported its gap as reached on
the 12 instances with n ≤ 16 and stopped at the time limit on all nine paths with n ≥ 32 (Table 4
in Appendix H). Its dual bounds were valid, but its reported interval did not contain the optimum
in any run: every reported primal bound lay below the optimum, by 1.8 · 10−6 to 1.6 · 10−5 . SCIP’s
incumbents lay slightly outside the box: every coordinate that is at a bound in x∗ violated that
bound by about 10−8 , which SCIP’s feasibility tolerance permits. Moving such a coordinate outward
decreases F at the rate of its inward derivative, and these violations account for 51% to 94% of the
shortfall; a violation of the epigraph constraint by 9 · 10−7 accounts for the rest. Projected onto
the box, the incumbents were optimal to within 1.3 · 10−14 , and on the instances with n ≤ 16 their
exact gap to the dual bound was 2.8 · 10−6 to 6.8 · 10−6 , so the requested 10−6 was not reached.
This is the usual behavior of floating-point solvers, and rigorous bounds need safe computations
[149] or an exact check. A longer time limit, tighter tolerances or an objective offset did not change
these outcomes (Appendix H). The comparison uses one formulation, default settings and a family
that favors decompositions of small width; it is not a performance ranking.




                                                  68
11.6    Random instances without planted structure
Experiment E6 uses 40 unplanted random continuous box QPs on [−1, 1]n with path or band sparsity
(bag sizes 2 and 3), n = 8, 12, 16, 24 and five seeds each. All Hessians are indefinite, and nothing
about the minimizer or the conditioning is prescribed. CT was run at ε = 2−10 , 2−20 , . . . , 2−50 and
reached every target in its first trial (θ = 1/4). The largest grid had 8 to 18 nodes per coordinate.
From ε = 2−10 to 2−50 it stayed the same on 27 of the 40 instances and changed by at most 5 nodes
on the others, while the number of stages grew linearly in log(1/ε), from 7–9 to 27–29 (Table 5 in
Appendix H). At the stationary point x̂ of the face of the final incumbent, Lemma 3.3(a) certified
point growth on 11 instances, with certified κ ≥ 4.5; there 8κθ2 > 1 for θ = 1/4, so the first trial
succeeded although Lemma 5.9 does not guarantee it. On the other 29 instances growth and κ
remain unknown; on 28 of them the localized test of Proposition 6.17 proved a candidate optimal,
and on one no candidate is proved optimal. For n = 8 all candidates agree with the enumeration.

11.7    Recourse
Six designed instances compare plain corrected grids (CT) with the recourse pipeline at target gap
2−10 and a 20-second limit (Table 6 in Appendix H). On two affine stars (n = 5 and 17), recognition
of the globally affine responses and an exact solve of the reduced problem took at most 0.07 seconds,
while plain grids stopped at the time limit with gaps 0.019 and 1.23. On the piecewise instances
ϕM − z 2 of Example C.11, the certified convex value factor removed the dependence on M , whereas
plain grids needed 498 table entries for M = 1 and 35,600 for M = 100. On a dense instance with
n = 33 whose residual is concave in each coordinate and submodular, the minimum-cut oracle of
Section 7.4 certified the target gap in 0.08 seconds; plain grids, with a decomposition of bag size 33,
stopped at the table limit before the first stage. All certificates were replayed; the checkers of the
recourse pipeline reuse the solver’s grid, table and lifting code and are therefore not independent
in the sense of Section 11.1. These are designed examples of the structures in Section 7, not hard
instances.

11.8    Limits
The cost of the method is exponential in the bag size, and exact rational arithmetic dominates
the running time; the band with bag size four already takes several seconds. On two binary
QPLIB instances [71] the first grid is exact, so the method is plain dynamic programming over the
decomposition. QPLIB_3852 (231 variables, minimum-fill bag size 20) was solved exactly in one
stage, with 5.7 · 106 table entries, 156 seconds and 4.4 GB of memory; the certified bounds coincide
at −234, the library’s value after the sign change to minimization. We did not replay this certificate,
because the reference checker is too slow at this size. For QPLIB_5881 (120 variables, bag size 94)
the first stage would need about 8 · 1028 table entries. Large width, not the accuracy, is the binding
limit; Section 10 shows that this is unavoidable in the worst case.


12     Conclusions and open problems
Small treewidth alone does not make sparse mixed-integer problems tractable. Upper bounds
on the coordinate curvature turn finite dynamic programming into a valid lower bound and its
min-marginals into a safe filter, and quadratic growth keeps the filtered grids at a size independent of
the accuracy. Under weighted growth, CT (Algorithm 5.6) certifies accuracy 2−q in f (p, κ̄)(I +q +1)5
bit operations, and under point growth exact output takes f1 (p, κ)(I + 1)C1 , without knowledge


                                                  69
of the growth constant; the certificates are checked by recomputation in exact arithmetic and are
valid without any growth assumption. By Theorem 1.2, neither parameter can be dropped unless
P = NP, and under rETH the exponent of κ in a bound f (p)κa(p) poly(I) cannot be o(p).
    A corrected-grid bound on a subbox is a valid node bound for spatial branch-and-bound, and the
min-marginal filter is certified bound tightening; whether either pays off inside a solver is untested.
A replayable certificate also turns a floating-point incumbent into a certified one: the exact value
of its projection onto the box and a grid certificate give a rigorous gap, which matters because
floating-point solvers satisfy bounds and constraints only within tolerances (Section 11). In the
experiments, grid sizes stay nearly constant as the accuracy grows, also on random instances on
which growth could not be certified, and the width of the available decomposition, not the accuracy,
is the binding limit.
    Several questions remain open.

 (1) Negative curvature. Let g be a point-growth constant, ν = max{0, −λmin (∇2 F )} and κν =
     max{1, ν/g}. Can continuous box QP be solved to accuracy 2−q in time f ′ (p, κν ) poly(I + q)
     for a computable function f ′ ? Theorem 7.9 and Corollary C.10 (Appendix C) give such a
     bound, with p the bag size after elimination, when certified responses of private convex blocks
     reduce every coordinate curvature to at most C0 ν for a constant C0 . Proposition 7.11 shows
     that recourse alone cannot ensure this, and Proposition G.5 (Appendix G.4) that matching
     local moments of any finite order is not enough. These obstructions suggest measuring
     curvature only where near-optimal points can lie, or using inequalities that couple both sides
     of a separator.

 (2) Constants
       √        in the exponent. Under weighted growth, each         √ bag table of CT has at most
     (C κ̄ log(n + 2))p entries. The factor pp in f (p, t) = c0 (c1 p t)p t(1 + log2 t)2 comes only from
     moving the logarithmic factor into the polynomial part (inequality (5.4)); the value-oracle
     lower bound of Proposition 10.3 has no such factor. Can the logarithmic factor, and with it pp ,
     be avoided, and is the exponent p/2 of κ optimal for explicit inputs, as it is for value oracles?

 (3) Coupling constraints. Is there a feasible rounding scheme for totally unimodular, or more
     general, sparse constraints that works with graded grids? Curvature-only corrections on
     misaligned grids and on a common graded pattern give invalid bounds (Proposition E.11 and
     Example E.12 in Appendix E), so a different correction is needed. A partial step, which we
     have not worked out, would keep the aligned uniform mesh only on coordinates that occur
                                                                                              p/2
     in a constraint and use graded grids on the others; this should replace the factor nc of
     Theorem 8.9 by npc /2 , where pc is the largest number of constrained continuous coordinates in
     a bag.

 (4) Optimal sets. Suppose that the optimal set is finite and that r bounds the number of optimal
     values per coordinate. Can exact output then be obtained in f ′ (p, κS , r) poly(I) bit operations
     for a computable function f ′ ? For optimal sets with continuous components, the grids produced
     by filtering can need a number of nodes that grows with the accuracy (Proposition 10.8); which
     compact descriptions beyond the classes of Section 9 can be computed with a decomposition?

 (5) Exact polynomial output. For continuous polynomial objectives the minimizer can be irrational.
     Certificates that keep correlations between coordinates, rather than signs over coordinate
     rectangles, seem necessary under weak complementarity (Proposition B.6 in Appendix B.2);
     no construction of parameterized size is known.

A     Proofs for Section 5
Proof of Lemma 5.2. (a) Consider the side towards u′i ; the other is symmetric. The grid interval
between the nodes at distances tk < tk+1 has length at most σ(tk ). For i ∈ IC this is h + θtk . For
i ∈ IZ and a length of at least two, σ(tk ) ≥ 2, so σ(tk ) = ⌊h + θtk ⌋ ≤ h + θtk ; intervals of length
one are ignored in wi . The node at distance tk has adjacent intervals starting at distances tk−1 and
tk , both at most tk = |v − ci |; the intervals adjacent to the center have length at most h.
     (b) Let t1 < · · · < tm = R be the nodes on that side, and t0 = 0. Every step except the last is
unclipped, and an unclipped step from t has length σ(t) ≥ (ĥ + θt)/3: for i ∈ IC trivially; for i ∈ IZ
with h ≥ 1 because ⌊y⌋ ≥ y/2 for y ≥ 1; for i ∈ IZ with h < 1, either θt < 2 and σ ≥ 1 > (1 + θt)/3,
or θt ≥ 2 and ⌊h + θt⌋ ≥ θt/2 ≥ (1 + θt)/3. Hence tk+1 + ĥ/θ ≥ (1 + θ/3)(tk + ĥ/θ) for k ≤ m − 2,
and (1 + θ/3)m−1 ≤ (tm−1 + ĥ/θ)θ/ĥ < 1 + θR/ĥ. So m − 1 < ln(1 + θR/ĥ)/ ln(1 + θ/3), and
ln(1 + x) ≥ 23                         1              72
              24 x for 0 ≤ x = θ/3 ≤ 12 gives m ≤ ⌈ 23θ ln(1 + θR/ĥ)⌉, which implies the bound.
     (c) Each side has length R ≤ h, and σ(0) ≥ R (for i ∈ IZ , R is an integer and ⌊h⌋ ≥ R), so the
first step reaches the endpoint.

and φ(m) ≤ 10⌈log2 (m + 2)⌉ for all m ≥ 1, so the bound is at most K(θ, nP ).

Bit lengths in Theorem 5.7. Let ΓX be the product of the denominators of the box endpoints
and ΓF that of the coefficients, after substituting fixed coordinates; both have O(I) bits, and
|ϖi |, |E| = O(I). Fix a trial µ and let α = jmax + maxi |ϖi | + |E| + µKµ . Every node of every grid
of the trial has a denominator dividing ΓX 2α . Indeed, the initial center and the original endpoints
have denominators dividing ΓX , and a new node is a center coordinate plus or minus

                           (1 + θ)k − 1           (2µ + 1)k − 2µk

are nodes of earlier stages of the same trial or original data. So the bound does not compound
from stage to stage; it depends on the stage limit only through the term jmax in α. Nodes lie in
X̄, so their numerators have O(I + α) bits. Every factor value, correction Li w2 /8, table entry
and message has a denominator dividing 8ΓF Γ2X 4α and is a sum of at most I + n terms, each a
coefficient times a product of at most two nodes or of two differences of nodes (a message is the
value of such a sum at a minimizing assignment). Over this common denominator every number is
an integer of b = O(I + α) bits, the dynamic program uses only integer additions and comparisons,
and a factor evaluation costs O(b2 ) bit operations per monomial. Since jmax + 1 ≤ c(I + q + 1) and√

growth the schedule still halts, and every number it forms is bounded as above for its trial.

Proof of Corollary 5.11. Uniform rule, center. Suppose a stage is centered at 0 with box X ′ ∋ 0. Its
grid in coordinate i consists of the multiples of hj in Xi′ and the endpoints of Xi′ . All grid intervals
have length at most hj , and wi (0) is at least the length of every interval: if some interval is not

                                                      86
adjacent to 0, the side of 0 that contains it is longer than hj , so the interval between 0 and ±hj on
that side has length hj . Hence wi (v) ≤ wi (0) for all nodes v, so D(x) ≤ D(0) and, for every grid
point x ≠ 0, Q(x) − Q(0) ≥ F (x) − F (0) ≥ g∥x∥2 > 0. The corrected minimizer is 0, and so is the
next center; by induction every stage is centered at 0.
     Uniform rule, filtered box. Put Rj = min{1, 2(k0 + 1)hj }; we show by induction that X (j) ⊇
[−Rj , Rj ]n . This holds for j = 0, since X (0) = [−1, 1]n . At stage 0 (h0 = 2) the grid is {−1, 0, 1}
in every coordinate, with wi (0) = 1; by Proposition 5.10 with h = 1 and R0 = 1 the node
0 passes, so both intervals are retained and X (1) = [−1, 1]n ⊇ [−R1 , R1 ]n . Let j ≥ 1. Then
hj ≤ 1, hence hj ≤ Rj , so ±hj are nodes and wi (0) = hj . Proposition 5.10 with h = hj
and R0 = Rj retains every interval with an endpoint khj , where |k| ≤ k0 and |k|hj ≤ Rj . If
(k0 + 1)hj ≤ 1, then (k0 + 1)hj ≤ Rj , and the retained intervals [khj , (k + 1)hj ], −k0 − 1 ≤ k ≤ k0 ,
give X (j+1) ⊇ [−(k0 + 1)hj , (k0 + 1)hj ]n = [−Rj+1 , Rj+1 ]n . If (k0 + 1)hj > 1, then Rj = 1 and
X (j) = [−1, 1]n ; moreover 1/hj is an integer with 1/hj < k0 + 1, so the nodes ±1 = ±(1/hj )hj pass,
the intervals [1 − hj , 1] and [−1, −1 + hj ] are retained, and X (j+1) = [−1, 1]n ⊇ [−Rj+1 , Rj+1 ]n .
This completes the induction. When (k0 + 1)hj ≤ 1 and stage j + 1 is run, its grid is centered
at 0 with spacing hj /2 and contains p every multiple of hj /2 in [−(kp 0 + 1)hj , (k0 + 1)hj ], that is,
4(k0 + 1) + 1 nodes; and k0 + 1 ≥ (n − 2)κ/16 gives 4k0 + 5 ≥ (n − 2)κ + 1.
     Graded rule. Apply Proposition 5.10 with h = hj and R0 = R. Every point t ∈ [−R, R] lies
in a grid interval [a, b]; if t ≥ 0 then 0 ≤ a ≤ R (the node 0 lies in the grid), and if t < 0 then
−R ≤ b ≤ 0, so the interval has an endpoint of absolute value at most R and is retained. Hence the
filtered box contains [−R, R] in every coordinate. The next grid starts from its center c′ inside this
box and uses unclipped steps h′ + θt with h′ = hj /2, so after k steps the distance from c′ is at most
h′ ((1 + θ)k − 1)/θ. One side of c′ has length at least R within the box and must be covered; hence
at least ln(1 + θR/h′ )/ ln(1 + θ) nodes lie on that side, besides c′ .

B.2    Exact implicit output for polynomial boundary optima
This subsection makes precise what exact output means for the explicit polynomials of Section 6.6
when the minimizer can be irrational and lie on the boundary. Throughout, F is an explicit rational
polynomial of fixed degree d ≥ 2 with factor scopes in the bags, on a mixed box X, with a verified
rational bound L ≥ max{0, maxi supX̄ ∂ii F }, for example the largest bound of Lemma 6.20. Fix
rationals C2 , C3 ≥ 1 with
                    X                           X
                        sup |∂ij F | ≤ C2 ,          sup |∂ijk F | ≤ C3    (i ∈ IC ).
                    j∈IC X̄                    j,k∈IC X̄

Monomialwise interval bounds give such numbers with bit length poly(I) at fixed d.
   In this subsection every coordinate uses the single curvature bound L, that is, Li := L for all i;
these are valid upper coordinate curvatures. If L > 0, then P contains every coordinate, so every
coordinate is gridded and filtered, and the localization bound (G4) of Lemma 5.9 applies to every
coordinate. If L = 0, the first stage uses endpoint grids, is exact and has zero gap. We use TRIAL
(Algorithm 5.3) with a common mesh hj = s2−j as in Lemma 5.9. Under point growth (3.2) with
                                                                     √          √
8κθ2 ≤ 1, (G4) places every retained interval of stage j within 4.2 nκ hj ≤ 5 nκ hj of (yj )i , plus
                                                 √
one for integer coordinates; we use the radius 5 nκ hj below.

Three sound tests. The trials are run with one extra filter.

Lemma B.1 (Integer-label filter). (i) Suppose at some stage every grid interval of an integer
coordinate i has length one. Then deleting the nodes v with mi (v) > U and taking the integer hull
of the remaining nodes removes no point with objective at most U , and keeps the incumbent and the
corrected minimizer. The stages remain a valid path certificate (Definition 4.6): the second alternative
of condition (C1) holds for every grid interval of coordinate i that the step removes. (ii) Assume a
                                                                                            √
unique minimizer x∗ , point growth (3.2), and 8κθ2 ≤ 1. At every stage with hj ≤ 1/(10 nκ), part
(i) applies to every integer coordinate and fixes it to x∗i .

Proof. (i) Since every grid interval of coordinate i has length one, the integers of the current
interval are exactly the nodes. For feasible x in the current box with xi = v, Proposition 4.2(c) gives
F (x) ≥ mi (v). Hence a deleted node carries no point with value at most U , in particular no minimizer
and not the incumbent. The corrected minimizer y survives because mi (yi ) ≤ Q(y) = β ≤ F ∗ ≤ U .
A removed grid interval of coordinate i has length one, hence effective width zero, and each of
its endpoints outside the new interval is a deleted node v with mi (v) > U ≥ β; this is the second
alternative of (C1).
    (ii) If j = 0, then s = h0 < 1 and there is no integer coordinate. Otherwise, by (G4) at stage
                                                        √                   √
j − 1, the current integer interval lies within 1 + 5 nκ hj−1 = 1 + 10 nκ hj ≤ 2 of the center
                                                                                       1
ci = (yj−1 )i . All steps max{1, ⌊hj + θt⌋} with t ≤ 2 equal one, because hj + θt ≤ 10   + 12 < 1, so (i)


                                                   89
applies. A retained node v has mi (v) ≤ U , so the grid point that attains mi (v) has Q ≤ U , and
(G2) gives |v − x∗i |2 ≤ 17    2              ∗                           ∗
                         15 κnhj < 1; so v = xi . The extra filter keeps x and the centers and only
shrinks boxes; the proof of Lemma 5.9 uses nothing else about the boxes, so its bounds remain valid
for the modified trial.

Lemma B.2 (Monotone face reduction). Let X ′ be a product box with fixed integer coordinates, i a
free continuous coordinate whose original bound ℓi is an endpoint of Xi′ , and suppose ∂i F ≥ 0 on
X ′ . Then minX ′ F = minX ′ ∩{xi =ℓi } F , and if ∂i F > 0 on X ′ , every minimizer over X ′ has xi = ℓi .
The same holds for ui with ∂i F ≤ 0. With c the midpoint and r the largest half-width of the free
continuous coordinates of X ′ , ∂i F (c) − C2 r > 0 certifies ∂i F > 0 on X ′ , and ∂i F (c) + C2 r < 0
certifies ∂i F < 0.

Proof. Replacing xi by ℓi keeps a point of X ′ in X ′ and, by the mean value theorem along the
segment, does not increase F , and decreases it under a strict sign unless xi = ℓi . For the test,
|∂i F (x) − ∂i F (c)| ≤ j sup |∂ij F | |xj − cj | ≤ C2 r.
                       P


    Any other sound sign enclosure may be used as well, for example Bernstein coefficient bounds
[76] of each factor derivative on its own scope, which have at most dp coefficients. Such monotonicity
tests are standard in interval global optimization [5, 86, 87]; the termination proof below uses only
the midpoint test.

Lemma B.3 (Uniform convexity patch). Let X ′ be a box with fixed integer coordinates and some
fixed continuous coordinates, Jf its free coordinates, c its midpoint and r the largest free half-width.
If HJf Jf (c) − (C3 r + σ)I ≻ 0 for some σ > 0, then F restricted to X ′ is strongly convex in xJf and
has a unique minimizer over X ′ .

Proof. Each entry satisfies |Hjk (x) − Hjk (c)| ≤ r l sup |∂jkl F |, so every row sum of |HJf Jf (x) −
                                                      P

HJf Jf (c)| is at most C3 r. The spectral norm of a symmetric matrix is at most its largest absolute
row sum. Hence HJf Jf (x) ≻ σI on X ′ .

The procedure. Trial µ ≥ 2 runs TRIAL with a common mesh, grading θ = 2−µ , the cap
8θ−1 ⌈log2 (n + 2)⌉ of Lemma 5.9(G5) and the filter of Lemma B.1, for stages j = 0, 1, 2, . . . without
a stage limit, aborting if the cap would be exceeded; step (iii) of TRIAL is replaced by the following
test. After stage j the trial succeeds if U = βj (a zero certified gap; the incumbent is then an
exact rational minimizer). Otherwise it filters, and if every integer coordinate of the retained box
X (j+1) is a singleton, it copies X (j+1) , applies sound reductions of Lemma B.2 to the copy until
none applies, and succeeds if no free coordinate remains or if the test of Lemma B.3 holds with
σµ = L/4µ+1 . The copy never influences later filtering. The procedure runs phases k = 2, 3, . . .; in
phase k it restarts trial µ for each 2 ≤ µ ≤ k and stops it after 2k−µ bit operations, stopping at the
first success.

Theorem B.4 (Exact implicit boundary output with a complementarity margin). (a) On success,
the output is either the incumbent after a zero certified gap, which is then a rational minimizer, or a
fixed integer assignment, a set of continuous coordinates fixed at original bounds, and a rational box
for the remaining coordinates on which F is strongly convex; in the second case the unique minimizer
x̂ of F over this face box is a global minimizer of the original problem, and if no coordinate is free,
x̂ is an explicit rational minimizer. This holds with no assumption on the instance.
     (b) Suppose F has a unique minimizer x∗ with point growth (3.2), and every coordinate in
A = J∂ ∩ IC , the continuous coordinates at which x∗ is at a bound, is strictly complementary: its
inward derivative, ∂i F (x∗ ) at a lower bound or −∂i F (x∗ ) at an upper bound, is positive. Let λA be

                                                    90
the smallest such inward derivative and Bλ = ⌈log2 max{2, C2 /λA }⌉ (Bλ = 1 if A = ∅). Then the
procedure succeeds after fd′ (p, κ) poly(I + Bλ ) bit operations, for a computable function fd′ . Neither
x∗ , g, nor λA is supplied.
Proof. (a) A zero certified gap makes the incumbent optimal (Theorem 4.7). Otherwise the retained
box contains every minimizer (Proposition 4.5 and Lemma B.1(i)), and its integer coordinates are
singletons, so its minimum is F ∗ . Each reduction preserves the minimum over the current box
(Lemma B.2). On the final face box F has minimum F ∗ and, by Lemma B.3 or because it is a single
point, a unique minimizer, which is therefore globally optimal.
    (b) If L = 0, trial 2 succeeds at stage 0 with zero gap, and the phase accounting at the end of
this proof applies with µ∗ = 2 and j∗ = 0; assume L > 0. Let µ∗ be the least µ ≥ 2 with 4µ ≥ 8κ,
so that 4µ∗ ≤ 32κ and trial µ∗ satisfies 8κθ2 ≤ 1; by Lemma 5.9(G5) it never reaches its cap. Let j∗
be the least stage with
                             1            √        λA            √               L
                    hj ≤     √ ,         5 nκ hj ≤     ,        5 nκ hj ≤              ,
                           10 nκ                   4C2                      8 · 4µ∗ C3
omitting the first condition without integer coordinates and the second if A = ∅. Then j∗ =
poly(I) + O(log κ + Bλ ), since log s, log C2 , log C3 and log(1/L) are polynomial in I. We show that
trial µ∗ succeeds by stage j∗ .
    At stage j∗ the integer coordinates are fixed to x∗ (Lemma B.1(ii)) and every continuous retained
                               √
interval has half-width r ≤ 5 nκ hj∗ (Lemma 5.9(G4)). A sound reduction never removes x∗ : if a
reduction that fixes coordinate i at a bound ξi ∈ {ℓi , ui } is justified on a box X ′ ∋ x∗ , then setting
x∗i to ξi gives a point of X ′ with value at most F ∗ , which by uniqueness is x∗ ; so x∗i = ξi and
i ∈ A. Hence the current face box always contains x∗ , agrees with it on fixed coordinates, and has
∥c − x∗ ∥∞ ≤ r. For i ∈ A at ℓi that is not yet fixed, ℓi is an endpoint of the current interval and
∂i F (c) − C2 r ≥ λA − 2C2 r ≥ λA /2 > 0, so the midpoint test fixes it; the upper case is symmetric.
When no reduction applies, the free set Jf consists exactly of the continuous coordinates that
are interior at x∗ . If Jf = ∅ the trial succeeds. Otherwise x∗ ± tv ∈ X for v ∈ RJf and small t,
so (3.2) and Taylor’s formula give ∇Jf F (x∗ ) = 0 and HJf Jf (x∗ ) ⪰ 2g I. By the row-sum bound,
HJf Jf (c) ⪰ (2g − C3 r)I, and
                           HJf Jf (c) − (C3 r + σµ∗ )I ⪰ (2g − 2C3 r − σµ∗ )I ≻ 0,
because 2C3 r ≤ L/(4 · 4µ∗ ) ≤ g/32 and σµ∗ = L/(4 · 4µ∗ ) ≤ g/32. Exact symmetric elimination
detects this.
    Through stage j∗ , trial µ∗ performs the table work of Lemma 4.3 with the cap K = 8·2µ∗ ⌈log2 (n+
2)⌉ on numbers of bit length poly(I + j∗ + µ∗ K) at fixed d (Section 6.6), plus at most n2 sign tests
and one elimination per stage. Call this number of bit operations M∗ ; it is at most a computable
function of (p, κ) times poly(I + Bλ ). Phase k allots kµ=2 2k−µ < 2k−1 operations, and trial µ∗
                                                          P
                                                                                       √
completes in phase k∗ = µ∗ + ⌈log2 M∗ ⌉. The total is at most 2k∗ ≤ 2 · 2µ∗ M∗ = O( κ M∗ ), plus
clock overhead polylogarithmic in this bound, which proves (b).

     The output is an exact implicit description, not a rational coordinate claim: for (x2 − 12 )2 +y +xy 2
                                                       √
on [0, 1]2 the face is y = 0 and the minimizer is (1/ 2, 0). Weak reductions preserve the minimum
but not necessarily every minimizer, so without uniqueness the certificate proves global optimality
of x̂, not uniqueness in X. Trials must be dovetailed: a trial with 4µ < 8κ may run forever without
exceeding its cap, and increasing µ once per unsuccessful stage would make the grading depend on
λA .
     The margin term and strict complementarity are both needed for certificates of this form, as the
next two examples show.
