Proposition B.6 (Weak complementarity defeats sign and patch tests). On X = [ 12 , 1] × [0, 1]2
with variables (x, w1 , w2 ) let u = x2 − 21 and

     Ψ = u2 + w12 + w22 + 3w1 w2 + u(w1 − w2 ) = 21 (u2 + w12 + w22 ) + 12 (u + w1 − w2 )2 + 4w1 w2 .
                                                                                           √
Then Ψ has degree four and one bag of size three; its unique minimizer is x∗ = (1/ 2, 0, 0) with
F ∗ = 0; point growth holds with g = 12 and L = 12 (κ = 24); and ∇w Ψ(x∗ ) = 0, so both active
coordinates are weakly complementary. For every rational box X ′ ⊆ X containing x∗ , none of ∂x Ψ,
∂w1 Ψ, ∂w2 Ψ has constant sign on X ′ , and ∇2 Ψ(x∗ ) is not positive semidefinite. Hence no trial of
the procedure ever succeeds on Ψ.

                                                      92
Proof. The identity is direct expansion. On X it gives Ψ ≥ 12 (u2 + ∥w∥2 ), and u2 = (x − x∗1 )2 (x +
x∗1 )2 ≥ (x − x∗1 )2 because x + x∗1 > 1. So Ψ ≥ 21 ∥(x, w) − x∗ ∥2 , with zero only at x∗ . Also
∂x2 Ψ = 12x2 − 2 + 2(w1 − w2 ) ≤ 12 and ∂w 2 Ψ = 2. The x-interval of X ′ contains the irrational x∗
                                            i                                                       1
in its interior. On the segment w = 0 inside X ′ , ∂x Ψ = 4xu, ∂w1 Ψ = u and ∂w2 Ψ = −u, and u
changes sign at x∗1 . So no coordinate can be fixed by a sound test, and the patch test on all three
coordinates would need ∇2 Ψ(x∗ ) ≻ 0; but v = (0, 1, −1) gives v T ∇2 Ψ(x∗ )v = 2 + 2 − 6 = −2. A
zero certified gap is impossible because x∗ is irrational.

   The identity in Proposition B.6 is itself a short global certificate that F ∗ = 0 and that x∗ is the
unique minimizer. Certificates that keep such correlations, rather than certify signs over independent
coordinate rectangles, are the natural route to exact output under point growth alone; no general
construction of them with parameterized size is known.


C     Value functions, local corrections and convex recourse: proofs
      and supplements
This appendix contains the proofs deferred from Sections 7.1–7.3 and the supplementary results
summarized there. The notation is that of these sections. For a rational vector w we write ⟨w⟩ for
its encoding length.

C.1    Proof of Theorem 7.2(b),(c)
(b) Let πL (I) ≥ I be the polynomial bound on the encoding lengths of the LVi , and π0 the polynomial
such that a factor of F0 or the oracle at a rational point w takes at most π0 (I + ⟨w⟩) bit operations;
its output then has encoding length at most π0 (I + ⟨w⟩). As noted after Definition 3.2, point growth
of V with constant g gives weighted growth of V with κ̄ ≤ κV . As in the proof of Theorem 5.7(b),
                                      √
CT succeeds at a trial with 2µ ≤ 6 κV , and the number of table operations, factor evaluations and
                                                            √
oracle calls over all trials is at most c(jmax + 1)pI 2 (60p κV )p , now with |A| + r ≤ 2I factors. The
integer exponents that define the meshes hij in Section 5 are now O(πL (I)), so jmax = O(πL (I) + q),
and the bit analysis of Appendix A, with O(I) replaced by O(πL (I)), shows that every grid node
of a trial with grading 2−µ has encoding length at most c(1 + µ2µ )(πL (I) + q + 1); moreover
1 + µ2µ ≤ c′ κV for the trials that run. Only the cost of one operation must be bounded anew,
because values of value factors have no common denominator. A table entry is a sum of at most
|A| + r + n ≤ 3I exact values and corrections at one grid point v, each of encoding length at most
π0 (I + ⟨v⟩), and ⟨v⟩ ≤ cc′ nκV (πL (I) + q + 1). So each addition, comparison, factor evaluation and
                                               ′
oracle call costs at most (c′′ κV (I + q + 1))C bit operations, where C ′ and c′′ depend only on π0 and
πL . Multiplying by the number of operations, and using I + q + 1 ≥ 2 to absorb constant factors
into the exponent, gives the bound f (p, κV )κC               C
                                                 V (I + q + 1) .
    (c) Each run of CT on V halts, because the proof of Theorem 5.7(a) uses only Definition 3.1.
Its incumbent lifts to a feasible point x̂ = (v̂, y(v̂)) of (3.1) with F (x̂) − β ≤ 2−q and a certified
β ≤ F ∗ . REC and the test of Proposition 6.6 are applied to F at x̂, so validity and termination
follow as in the proof of Theorem 6.14. For the bound, let F have point growth with constant
g. If LV = 0, CT is exact at its first stage, x̂ is optimal, and REC returns an optimal point,
which passes the test (Lemma 6.8). Otherwise point growth of F is set growth with S = {(v ∗ , y ∗ )},
and Theorem 6.9(a) applies once 2−q ≤ εS , that is, for some q ≤ poly(I) + O(log κV ), because
g ≥ LV /κV and log(1/LV ) ≤ poly(I). With (b) and O(log q) runs, absorbing powers of 1 + log κV
into κC 1
       V proves the bound.

          ◦

    If the cells of a multilevel scheme cover X̄B at the top level and each retained cell is replaced by

in Proposition 4.5.
    Theorem 7.4 also certifies a gap; for that, valid lower bounds and feasible completions are needed.
For filtering alone, exact values are not needed: only the oscillation of their error over the corners
matters. A common additive error, such as a normalization constant of messages, is therefore
harmless.

Proposition C.1 (Only the error oscillation matters). Let Q, e and εor be as in Theorem 7.4, and
suppose some cell of Q contains x∗B for a global minimizer x∗ . Let V be the set of all corners and
Ṽ : V → R a function such that, for some constants δ− ≤ δ+ with δ+ − δ− ≤ εor ,

                                 δ− ≤ VB (v) − Ṽ (v) ≤ δ+                  (v ∈ V).

The constants need not be known. Retain C if and only if mincorners of C Ṽ ≤ minV Ṽ + e + εor . Then
every cell containing x∗B for a global minimizer x∗ is retained, and every retained cell has a corner
with VB (v) ≤ F ∗ + 2e + 2εor .

Proof. Let C ∗ contain x∗B . By Lemma 7.3,

                             min          Ṽ ≤       min           VB − δ− ≤ F ∗ + e − δ− ,
                         corners of C ∗          corners of C ∗

while VB ≥ F ∗ gives minV Ṽ ≥ F ∗ − δ+ . The difference is at most e + δ+ − δ− ≤ e + εor , so C ∗ is
retained. If C is retained, it has a corner v with VB (v) ≤ Ṽ (v) + δ+ ≤ minV Ṽ + e + εor + δ+ , and
minV Ṽ ≤ mincorners of C ∗ Ṽ ≤ F ∗ +e−δ− . Thus VB (v) ≤ F ∗ +2e+εor +(δ+ −δ− ) ≤ F ∗ +2e+2εor .

Grid min-marginals are not certified recourse. Grid min-marginals minimize over the outside
coordinates on the grid, not over their original domain, and their error varies with the bag value.
No allowance that depends only on the bag, the curvature and the mesh makes them valid.
    Let h = 2−j and G = hZ ∩ [0, 1]. On [0, 1]1+m with coordinates (x, y1 , . . . , ym ) and the
uniform product grid G1+m , put UG = minG1+m F , the grid value function V G (a) = min{F (z) :
z ∈ G1+m , zx = a} of the coordinate x, and, for the bag B = {x, y1 }, the grid min-marginal
mF (v) = min{F (z) : z ∈ G1+m , (zx , zy1 ) = v}. A constant-correction test with constant c retains a
bag cell C of the grid if and only if some corner v of C has mF (v)−c ≤ UG . With a common curvature
bound L, every node of a uniform grid has correction Lh2 /8 in every coordinate, so the corrected
grid of Section 4 retains a grid interval [a, a + h] of x if and only if min{V G (a), V G (a + h)} − c ≤ UG

                                                              94
with c = (1 + m)Lh2 /8; a bag-local budget would take c = |B|Lh2 /8. In both families below, the
corner minimum of each relevant bag cell equals min{V G (a), V G (a + h)}, where [a, a + h] is the
projection of the cell on x, so the thresholds below hold for both tests.

Proof of Proposition C.2. (A) Write ωA = mh2 /4 − 1 − ϵ > 0. All coordinate second derivatives
equal 2 and FA is quadratic, so L = 2 is valid. For fixed x, each leaf term y 2 − hxy is strictly convex
with minimizer hx/2 ∈ [0, 1] and minimum −h2 x2 /4. Hence miny FA (x, y) = (1−mh2 /4)x2 +ωA x =:
V (x), which is strictly concave because mh2 > 4, with V (0) = 0 and V (1) = −ϵ. For x ∈ (0, 1),
strict concavity gives V (x) > (1 − x)V (0) + xV (1) ≥ −ϵ. So x = 1 is the unique minimizing center
and yi = h/2 the unique leaves; F ∗ = −ϵ. The origin has FA = 0 = F ∗ + ϵ and squared distance
1 + mh2 /4 from the minimizer, which bounds g.
    On the grid, y(y − hx) ≥ 0 for y ∈ G and x ∈ [0, 1], since either y = 0 or y ≥ h ≥ hx. Also
x2 + ωA x ≥ 0 for x ≥ 0. Hence FA ≥ 0 on G1+m with equality at the origin, so UG = 0, and every
leaf’s grid minimum is 0. The point (1, h/2) has x at the right endpoint and h/2 interior to [0, h], so
C is the only grid cell of the bag containing it. At its corners, mF (x, y1 ) = x2 + ωA x + (y12 − hxy1 ).
For x = 1 − h this equals (1 − h)(1 − h + ωA ) = (1 − h)(mh2 /4 − h − ϵ) at y1 = 0 and exceeds it by
h3 at y1 = h. For x = 1 it equals mh2 /4 − ϵ at both y1 = 0, h, which exceeds the previous value by
h(mh2 /4 − ϵ) + h(1 − h) > 0. This proves the corner minimum; it also equals min{V G (1 − h), V G (1)},
because every leaf’s grid minimum    is attained at 0. Since UG = 0 and L = 2, the threshold for c is
as stated, and (1 + m)h /4 (1 − h)(mh /4 − h − ϵ) → (1 − h)−1 .

C.3    Certificates: energy, existence, evaluation and height
In the setting of Lemma 7.7, put W = {d : Gs d = 0 (s ∈ E)}. For every coordinate j,

                                (B T CB)jj = − min dT Cd + 2dT Γej .
                                                                           
                                                                                                    (C.1)
                                                 d∈W

Indeed, the function d 7→ dT Cd + 2dT Γej is convex. By Lemma 7.7(b), Bej ∈ W, and by
the w-linear part CB + Γ + GT Λ = 0 of (7.7), for d ∈ W half the gradient at Bej satisfies
(CBej + Γej )T d = − s∈E (Λs ej )(Gs d) = 0. A convex function whose gradient at a point of a
                        P

linear subspace is orthogonal to that subspace attains its minimum over the subspace there, so the
minimum equals (B T CB)jj + 2(B T Γ)jj = −(B T CB)jj . Formula (C.1) says that a piece cancels
direct curvature exactly by the energy that the private response spends to follow a unit change of
wj , using only directions that keep every constraint with a nonzero multiplier active. If all private
coordinates coupled to wj are held at bounds with nonzero multipliers, the piece cancels nothing.

Proof of Proposition 7.8(b). Let J = [t− , t+ ] with t− < t+ ; a point interval is trivial. The sets
JΠ′ = {t ∈ J : w + tej ∈ Π′ } are closed intervals that cover J. The union of the nondegenerate ones
is closed, and its complement in J is relatively open and contained in the finitely many degenerate
ones, which are points; hence it is empty. The endpoints of the nondegenerate JΠ′ therefore split J
into finitely many closed subintervals. Some nondegenerate JΠ′ contains the midpoint of a given
subinterval, and then contains the whole subinterval, because no endpoint of JΠ′ lies strictly inside
it. On that subinterval ψ(w + tej ) = qΠ′ (w + tej ) has second derivative −(BΠ  T CB ′ ) ≤ −σ by
                                                                                  ′   Π jj       j



                                                      96
Lemma 7.7, so χ(t) = ψ(w + tej ) + σj t2 /2 is concave there. The function χ is continuous, and at
each breakpoint the jump χ′+ − χ′− equals that of the concave function t 7→ ψ(w + tej ), which is
nonpositive. So χ′+ is nonincreasing, and χ is concave.
Proposition C.4 (Finite certificates exist). Let Y = [ℓY , uY ] with ℓY < uY , and let Π ⊆ Rk be a
rational box with nonempty interior. Then ψ has a certificate on Π in the sense of Definition 7.6.
Proof. A pattern J = (Jℓ , Jf , Ju ) is a partition of the private coordinates. Let MJ be the set of
(w, y, λlow , λup ) with w ∈ Π, ℓY ≤ y ≤ uY , λlow , λup ≥ 0, Cy + Γw + c = λlow − λup , and yi = ℓYi ,
λup
  i = 0 for i ∈ Jℓ ; λi
                        low = λup = 0 for i ∈ J ; y = uY , λlow = 0 for i ∈ J . Each M is a rational
                               i               f   i     i   i                u            J
polyhedron, and every point of it satisfies the KKT conditions, including complementarity. Its
projection ΠJ to the w coordinates is a rational polytope. Every w ∈ Π has an optimal y and, since
the constraints are linear, KKT multipliers; these fit some pattern. Thus the ΠJ cover Π. The
union of the full-dimensional ΠJ is closed and contains Π minus finitely many lower-dimensional
polytopes, a dense subset of Π; hence it equals Π.
    Triangulate each full-dimensional ΠJ into k-simplices with rational vertices. At each vertex w′ of
a simplex Σ, choose a rational point (w′ , yw′ , λw′ ) ∈ MJ . On Σ define (ȳ, λ) by affine interpolation
of these vertex values in barycentric coordinates. For w ∈ Σ the point (w, ȳ(w), λ(w)) is a convex
combination of points of the convex set MJ , so it lies in MJ . Hence (ȳ, λ) is a certificate on Σ.
    Let H be the finite set of hyperplanes spanned by facets of all these simplices. Starting from Π,
split every current leaf whose interior meets a hyperplane of H by that hyperplane, until no leaf
interior meets any of them. Both halves of every split have nonempty interior, and no hyperplane
splits two nodes on one root path, so the process stops. Let Π′ be a final leaf. Its interior is connected
and misses every hyperplane of H, so for each simplex Σ either int Π′ ⊆ int Σ or int Π′ ∩ Σ = ∅. The
full-dimensional simplices cover Π, so the first case occurs for some Σ, and Π′ ⊆ Σ. Restricting the
certificate of Σ to Π′ completes the proof.
    The same argument applies to a polytope Y , with patterns given by sets of active rows.
Lemma C.5 (Exact tables with value factors). For rational w ∈ Rkt , the value ψt (w) and a
minimizer y (t) (w) are rationals of encoding length poly(I + ⟨w⟩), computable in that time, where I is
the encoding length of the data. In CT applied to V , every message, bag-table entry and min-marginal
is a sum of at most m0 = |A| + r + |K| factor values, value-factor values and corrections at one grid
point v, hence has encoding length at most m0 poly(I + ⟨v⟩).
Proof. A convex quadratic program with rational data is solvable exactly in polynomial time, and
it has a rational optimal solution and value of polynomial size [119]. If a certificate is supplied, the
value is instead qΠ′ (w) and the minimizer ȳ(w) for a leaf Π′ containing w, found by descending the
partition tree. In the message recurrence and in the bag tables, every entry equals the value of a
sum of factor values and unary corrections at one assignment, with each factor and each correction
                                                                                                 ′
appearing at most once. If each of m0 rationals has a numerator of absolute value at most 2b and a
                         ′                                          ′
denominator at most 2b , their sum has denominator at most 2m0 b and numerator of absolute value
                 ′
at most m0 2m0 b . Comparisons and additions on such numbers are polynomial in their length. No
common denominator across different active sets is needed.
                                                                             ′

and Λ0 = (2n′ βH2 )n′ . Then some global minimizer x∗ of F on P has x∗ = z/ρ with z ∈ Zn′ and
                                                               P
an integer 1 ≤ ρ ≤ Λ0 ; if the minimizer is unique, it has this form. The minimum minP FP is a
rational with denominator at most 2∆P Λ20 .

Proof. Global minimizers exist because P is compact. Among them choose x∗ whose active set
E = {s : As x∗ = as } has maximal cardinality, and put W = {d : AE d = 0}. For d ∈ W and small

Proof of the exact-output part of Theorem 7.9(iii). Fix the integer retained coordinates at any in-
teger values. The remaining problem minimizes a quadratic over the bounded rational polytope
formed by the continuous retained box and the sets Yt . Scale its constraint rows to integers and
multiply the objective by a common denominator ∆P of all coefficients of F . The fixed integer
values change only the linear and constant terms, which stay integral, so the numbers βH and ∆P
of Lemma C.6 are the same for every integer slice. That lemma gives computable integers Λ0 and
Ω0 = 2∆P Λ20 of polynomial encoding length: every slice optimum, in particular F ∗ , has denominator
at most Ω0 , and a unique minimizer has continuous coordinates with denominators at most Λ0 .
    Run CT on V with ε = 2−q for q = 1, 2, 4, . . .. After each run, with incumbent v̂ and bound
β, replace each continuous coordinate of v̂ by the unique rational with denominator at most Λ0
within distance 1/(4Λ20 ), if one exists; continued fractions find it in polynomial time. If every such
coordinate is replaced and the result ṽ lies in XK , compute y(ṽ) and V (ṽ) = a/W in lowest terms,
and stop with (ṽ, y(ṽ)) if V (ṽ) − β < 1/(Ω0 W ). By Proposition 6.6 with the denominator bound Ω0
for F ∗ , a returned point is optimal: if V (ṽ) > F ∗ , then V (ṽ) − β ≥ V (ṽ) − F ∗ ≥ 1/(Ω0 W ). This
uses neither growth nor uniqueness.
    If F has point growth with constant g, its minimizer (v ∗ , y ∗ ) is unique, so the continuous
coordinates of v ∗ have denominators at most Λ0 . Once ε ≤ min{1/(2Ω20 ), g/(32Λ40 )}, Lemma 7.1(b)
gives ∥v̂ − v ∗ ∥2 ≤ (V (v̂) − F ∗ )/g ≤ 1/(32Λ40 ). Every coordinate of v̂ is then within 1/(4Λ20 ) < 1 of
the corresponding coordinate of v ∗ , so integer coordinates agree, and two distinct rationals with
denominators at most Λ0 differ by at least 1/Λ20 , so ṽ = v ∗ . Then V (ṽ) = F ∗ = a/W with W ≤ Ω0
and V (ṽ) − β ≤ ε < 1/(Ω0 W ), so the procedure stops. Since 1/g ≤ κV /L+ and log(1/L+ ), log Λ0 ,
log Ω0 are polynomial in I, this happens with q ≤ poly(I) + O(log κV ), and doubling overshoots
by a factor at most two. Each run costs at most f (p, κV )κC                      C
                                                                   V (I + q + 1) bit operations by the
approximation part, the reconstruction and the evaluation of V (ṽ) cost poly(I + q), and there are
O(log q) runs; absorbing the powers of 1 + log κV into κC    1                            C1
                                                            V gives the bound f (p, κV )κV (I + 1) .

                                                    98
C.4    Recognition of affine selectors
A globally affine response, that is, a one-leaf certificate (Remark 7.10), need not be supplied. Let
Y = [ℓY , uY ] with ℓY < uY , let Π = {w : Aw ≤ a} be a rational polytope with nonempty interior
and a rational interior point w0 (for a scope box, its center), and let ϕ(w, y) = 12 y T Cy + y T Γw + cT y
with C ⪰ 0. An affine optimal selector is an affine map ȳ with ȳ(w) ∈ arg minY ϕ(w, ·) for every
w ∈ Π.

Theorem C.7 (Recognition of affine selectors). One exact convex quadratic program and one linear
program decide whether an affine optimal selector exists. If one exists, they return a rational selector
of polynomial size together with affine multipliers forming a one-leaf certificate on Π. Singular C
and nonunique responses are allowed.

Proof. Common central gradient. Let ŷ be a minimizer at w0 . For any minimizer y ′ at w0 , the
function t 7→ ϕ(w0 , ŷ + t(y ′ − ŷ)) is a convex quadratic that is constant on [0, 1], because the optimal
set is convex. Its quadratic coefficient (y ′ − ŷ)T C(y ′ − ŷ) vanishes, so C(y ′ − ŷ) = 0 since C ⪰ 0. All
central minimizers therefore have the same gradient ζ0 = C ŷ + Γw0 + c; this is a classical property
of convex programs [132]. Let Iℓ = {i : ζ0i > 0}, Iu = {i : ζ0i < 0} and I0 = {i : ζ0i = 0}. By the
box KKT conditions, every central minimizer has yi = ℓYi for i ∈ Iℓ and yi = uYi for i ∈ Iu .
    Necessity. We use one fact: if an affine function is nonnegative on Π and vanishes at an interior
point, it vanishes identically. Let ȳ be an affine optimal selector and write ζ(w) = C ȳ(w) + Γw + c,
an affine map. Since ȳ(w0 ) is a central minimizer, ζ(w0 ) = ζ0 . For i ∈ Iℓ , ȳi − ℓYi ≥ 0 on Π and
vanishes at w0 , so ȳi ≡ ℓYi ; optimality at every w then needs ζi ≥ 0 on Π. Symmetrically, ȳi ≡ uYi
and ζi ≤ 0 for i ∈ Iu . Let j ∈ I0 , so ζj (w0 ) = 0. If ȳj ≡ ℓYj , optimality needs ζj ≥ 0 on Π, so ζj ≡ 0;
the case ȳj ≡ uYj is symmetric. Otherwise ȳj is not identically at a bound, so by the fact above
it attains no bound at an interior point. Then optimality gives ζj = 0 on the interior of Π, hence
ζj ≡ 0. Thus every affine optimal selector satisfies

                                 ȳi ≡ ℓYi , ζi ≥ 0 on Π      (i ∈ Iℓ ),
                                          Y
                                 ȳi ≡ ui , ζi ≤ 0 on Π       (i ∈ Iu ),                                (C.2)
                                 ζj ≡ 0, ℓYj ≤ ȳj ≤ uYj on Π (j ∈ I0 ).

    Sufficiency. If an affine ȳ satisfies (C.2), define lower multipliers ζi on Iℓ and upper multipliers
−ζi on Iu , and zero otherwise. These are affine and nonnegative on Π, and satisfy stationarity and
complementarity identically. This is a certificate on Π; by Lemma 7.7(a), ȳ(w) is optimal for every
w ∈ Π.
    Linear program. Write ȳ(w) = ȳ0 +Bw. Then ζ(w) = (C ȳ0 +c)+(CB+Γ)w has coefficients linear
in (ȳ0 , B). Identities in (C.2) are linear equations. Each condition “an affine function is nonnegative
on Π” is replaced by its Farkas certificate π ≥ 0, AT π = −(linear part), aT π ≤ (constant part),
which is linear in (ȳ0 , B, π). For a box Π, auxiliary variables bounding |Bij | and |(CB + Γ)ij | give the
exact ranges instead. The resulting system has polynomial size. It is feasible exactly when an affine
optimal selector exists. Exact convex quadratic programming [119] and exact linear programming
[82] run in polynomial time and return rational solutions of polynomial size. If k = 0, any central
minimizer is a constant selector.

    Fixing the active bounds of an arbitrary central minimizer is not enough when C is singular. For
(y1 + y2 − w)2 with y ∈ [0, 1]2 and w ∈ [0, 2], the central minimizer (0, 1) would fix both coordinates,
yet the common central gradient is zero and the theorem returns the selector (w/2, w/2). For
(y − w)2 with y ∈ [0, 1] and w ∈ [−1, 2], the clipped response is not affine, and the linear program is
infeasible.

                                                     99
    A negative answer rejects the affine class for this block, not the optimization problem; the block
may still enter Theorem 7.9 with a piecewise certificate or with σt = 0. Choosing a useful partition
into private blocks is not addressed.

Corollary C.10 (A negative-curvature regime). In Theorem 7.9(iii), if the full Hessian of F has
ν = max{0, −λmin } > 0 and L+ ≤ C0 ν, then κV ≤ max{1, C0 } max{1, ν/g}. Membership in this
regime can be tested up to a factor two: a rational ν̂ with ν ≤ ν̂ < 2ν is computable in polynomial
time, and L+ ≤ C0 ν̂/2 implies L+ ≤ C0 ν.
Proof. The bound on κV is immediate, and ν̂/2 < ν gives the implication. For ν̂, write H = ∇2 F
and let ∆H be a positive integer with ∆H H integral. First test H ⪰ 0 exactly; if it fails, ν > 0.
Start from ν̂0 = maxi j |Hij | ≥ ∥H∥ ≥ ν and halve ν̂ while H + (ν̂/2)I ⪰ 0. The final ν̂ satisfies
                        P

H + ν̂I ⪰ 0 and H + (ν̂/2)I ̸⪰ 0, that is, ν ≤ ν̂ < 2ν. If d = rank H, the product of the nonzero
eigenvalues is, up to sign, a coefficient of the characteristic polynomial with denominator dividing
∆dH , so its absolute value is at least ∆−d H . Each nonzero eigenvalue is at most ν̂0 in absolute
value, so ν ≥ ∆−d  ν̂

elimination.
   The algorithm itself never uses ν or ν̂; it computes L+ . The hypothesis L+ ≤ C0 ν is substantive:
Proposition 7.11 shows that it can fail for every admissible choice of private blocks.

Remark C.13 (The certified cancellation is unstable). For the block objective M (y − w)2 + ϵy with
y, w ∈ [0, 1], the response is y = max{0, w − ϵ/(2M )}. For ϵ = 0 it is y = w, so σ = 2M . For
every ϵ > 0 the piece [0, ϵ/(2M )] has response y = 0, so σ = 0 by Proposition 7.8(c), and the value
has second derivative 2M there. The full Hessian, and hence ν, does not depend on ϵ. The same
happens near z = 0 in Example C.11 if ϵy2 is added: on a short interval both private coordinates sit
at their lower bounds, and σ = 0. The certified curvature is a maximum over pieces, however small
the piece.

Put ξ1 = x − 1 ∈ [−1, 1] and ξ2 = y − 1 ∈ [0, 2], so FM = M (ξ1 − ξ2 )2 − 18 (ξ1 + ξ2 )2 + 2ξ2 . Using
M ≥ 1 and 2ξ2 ≥ ξ22 on [0, 2],

This proves growth 18 , uniqueness and F ∗ = 0. The Hessian                   −2M −1/4 2M −1/4
                                                                                                         has eigenvalues
4M on (1, −1) and − 21 on (1, 1); so ν = 12 , and K = ∅ is not admissible. The admissible sets are
{x, y}, {y} and {x}. Note FM (2, 2) = 23 .
    K = {x, y}: V = FM has LK = 2M − 41 . Under x = ω1 x′ , y = ω2 y ′ the coordinate curvatures
become ωi2 (2M − 14 ), and the point (2, 2) shows that every growth constant in the new coordinates
is at most 32 /(ω1−2 + ω2−2 ) ≤ 32 mini ωi2 .
    K = {y}: FM is strictly convex in x, and ∂x FM (2, y) = 2M (2 − y) − y/4 ≤ 0 for y ≥ yc =
4M/(2M + 14 ), where 1 < yc < 2. So on [yc , 3] the response is x = 2, and V (y) = FM (2, y) has
second derivative 2M − 41 . Hence LK ≥ 2M − 14 , while V (2) ≤ 32 and V (1) = 0 give gK ≤ 32 .
    K = {x}: FM is strictly convex in y, and ∂y FM (x, 1) = 2 − (2M + 14 )(x − 1) ≥ 0 for x ≤ xc =
1 + 2/(2M + 14 ), where 1 < xc ≤ 2. So on [0, xc ] the response is y = 1, and V (x) = (M − 18 )(x − 1)2
has second derivative 2M − 41 . Again V (2) ≤ 32 gives gK ≤ 32 .
    In one dimension a rescaling multiplies curvature and growth by the same factor. In all cases
LK /gK ≥ (2M − 14 )/ 32 = (4M − 12 )/3 ≥ M .
    The obstruction is a clipped piece: a private coordinate that cancels the stiffness hits a bound
on a full-dimensional set of parameters. Such a piece can be arbitrarily short (Remark C.13). In the
proof above, every upper bound on gK comes from a point with objective gap at least 18 . A bound
in terms of p and ν/g along this route therefore cannot use the global constants LK and gK ; one
possibility is to measure curvature and growth only where near-optimal points can lie (Section 12,
question 1).




                                                        102
D     Cut-based recourse: exact output, concave–convex residuals
      and proofs
This appendix uses the notation of Section 7.4: F (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + c is a rational quadratic, K
is a core of k continuous coordinates with domain [0, 1]k , R is the residual, and αi , δi and y(z) are
as defined before Proposition 7.14.

D.1    Exact output with a cut residual
Let ∆K = Λc Λ2e , where Λc is the least positive common denominator of the monomial coefficients of
F and Λe that of the residual bounds after inward rounding; both have O(I) bits. For a residual
endpoint vector y put Fy (v) = F (v, y) and Ψ(y) = minv∈[0,1]k Fy (v).

Lemma D.1 (Uniform height over residual endpoint vectors). Assume (R1). Let ΩK be the constant
Ω of (6.1) computed for Fy on [0, 1]k with the common denominator ∆ = ∆K . Then ΩK does not
depend on y, log ΩK = O(I 2 ), and for every residual endpoint vector y the value Ψ(y) is a rational
with reduced denominator at most ΩK ; so is F ∗ .

Proof. The monomial coefficients of Fy are 12 Hii for i ∈ K, Hij for i < j in K, the linear coefficients
bi + j∈R Hij yj and the constant c + j∈R (bj yj + 12 Hjj yj2 ) + j<l, j,l∈R Hjl yj yl . Each is a sum of
     P                                 P                          P

products of a coefficient of F with at most two residual bounds, so ∆K is a common denominator
of all of them and of the endpoints 0, 1. The Hessian of Fy is HKK for every y, and the constant
Ω of (6.1) depends only on ∆ and the Hessian, so ΩK does not depend on y; its bit length is
O(kI) = O(I 2 ), as in Section 6.1. Corollary 6.4(b), applied to the box QP Fy on [0, 1]k , bounds the
reduced denominator of Ψ(y) by ΩK . By Lemma 7.13, F ∗ = minv miny F (v, y) with y ranging over
residual endpoint vectors, so F ∗ = Ψ(y ∗ ) for some such y ∗ .

   The square of Λe is needed: the term 12 yi yj at residual endpoints yi = yj = 12 equals 18 , while
the data have common denominator 2.

Theorem D.2 (Exact output with a cut residual). Assume (7.11) with continuous core [0, 1]k
and rational data, and run CORE (Algorithm 7.17) with the oracle of Theorem 7.15, L =
maxi∈K max{Hii , 0}, accuracy ε = Ω−2    K /2 and level limit J the first level with eJ ≤ ε. Let yU
be the residual part of the final incumbent and β = min{λj , U } the returned bound. Minimize FyU
exactly over [0, 1]k by enumerating the 3k faces of the cube, solving the stationarity system on each
face whose free Hessian block is nonsingular, and keeping the solutions that lie in the face. The best
candidate v̂ makes (v̂, yU ) a global minimizer, and (v̂, yU ) passes the test of Proposition 6.6 with the
denominator bound ΩK and this β. A checker needs only the trace of CORE, ΩK , and the feasibility,
exact value and reduced denominator of the output. If in √   addition V has core growth with constant g
as in Proposition 7.19, the total bit complexity is 8 (1 + kκK )k poly(I) with an absolute exponent,
                                                     k

and g is not an input.

Proof. By Theorem 7.18(b), CORE stops at level J at the latest, with U − β ≤ ε < Ω−2        K . Apply
Lemma 6.3 to the box QP FyU on [0, 1]k : let v ◦ be a minimizer and v a vertex of its stationary
polytope. By part (b), v is a minimizer, and by part (c) the Hessian block of FyU on the set J0 (v)
of coordinates of v in (0, 1) is positive definite. So v is the unique solution of the stationarity
system on the face of the cube that fixes the coordinates outside J0 (v) at their values in v, and the
enumeration contains it. All candidates lie in [0, 1]k , so the best one attains Ψ(yU ).
    The oracle returns endpoint vectors, so yU is one. With (vU , yU ) the final incumbent, F (v̂, yU ) =
Ψ(yU ) ≤ F (vU , yU ) = U , and β ≤ F ∗ by Theorem 7.18(d). By Lemma D.1, F ∗ has denominator at

                                                   103
most ΩK and F (v̂, yU ) = a/W in lowest terms with W ≤ ΩK . Hence F (v̂, yU ) − β ≤ U − β < Ω−2   K ≤
1/(ΩK W ), and Proposition 6.6 shows that (v̂, yU ) is a global minimizer. The checker verifies β ≤ F ∗
by Theorem 7.18(d) and then the inequality F (v̂, yU ) − β < 1/(ΩK W ), which proves optimality by
Proposition 6.6.
    For the complexity, if L = 0 then J = 0. Otherwise log(kL) = O(I) and log ΩK = O(I 2 ), so
J = O(log ΩK + √ log(kL) + 1) = O(I 2 ). Under core growth, Proposition 7.19 bounds the queries
by 2k + 8k J(1 + kκK )k . Each query is a cut problem whose data have bit length polynomial in
I + kJ, hence poly(I) by Theorem 7.15, with an absolute exponent. The face enumeration costs
3k poly(I), and 2k + 3k ≤ 2 · 8k .

   Without core growth the procedure still terminates, after at most J = O(I 2 ) levels, but
Example 7.20 shows that the number of queries can then be 2Ω(kJ) .

D.2      Concave–convex residuals
This subsection proves the claims of Remark 7.21. Fix a rational core point v. Let R = R− ∪ R+
with Hii ≤ 0 for i ∈ R− , signs o with oi oj Hij ≤ 0 for all i =
                                                               ̸ j in R, continuous coordinates in R+
and HR+ R+ ⪰ 0. For S ⊆ R− let y− (S) ∈ R      R − have coordinates αi + δi [i ∈ S], and put
                                               n                               Y             o

                                                                               i∈R+

With R+ = ∅ this is the class (7.11); with R− = ∅ the residual problem is convex.

Proposition D.3 (Submodular conditional value). (a) The minimum of F (v, ·) over the continuous
residual box and over the mixed residual domain equals minS⊆R− Φ(S). (b) Each Φ(S) is the value
of a rational convex box QP. (c) For all S, S ′ ⊆ R− , Φ(S) + Φ(S ′ ) ≥ Φ(S ∩ S ′ ) + Φ(S ∪ S ′ ).

Proof. (a) As in Lemma 7.13, move each coordinate of R− to an endpoint without increasing F ;
the resulting point is in the mixed domain because R+ is continuous. (b) After the coordinates
in R− are fixed, the objective in y+ has Hessian HR+ R+ ⪰ 0. (c) Substitute out the coordinates
with δi = 0 and put yi = αi + δi ti with t ∈ [0, 1]R . The transformed objective F̃ has off-
diagonal Hessian entries δi δj Hij = oi oj (ui − ℓi )(uj − ℓj )Hij ≤ 0. For t, t′ ∈ [0, 1]R , F̃ (t) + F̃ (t′ ) ≥
F̃ (t ∧ t′ ) + F̃ (t ∨ t′ ): the terms in one coordinate cancel, because {ti , t′i } = {(t ∧ t′ )i , (t ∨ t′ )i };
for a pair i, j the two sides agree if ti − t′i and tj − t′j do not have opposite signs, and otherwise
ti tj + t′i t′j − (t ∧ t′ )i (t ∧ t′ )j − (t ∨ t′ )i (t ∨ t′ )j = (ti − t′i )(tj − t′j ) ≤ 0, which, multiplied by a nonpositive
                                                              ′
coefficient, is nonnegative. Let tS and tS attain Φ(S) and Φ(S ′ ) in the transformed coordinates of
                                          ′                          ′
R+ . Then (1S , tS ) ∧ (1S ′ , tS ) = (1S∩S ′ , tS ∧ tS ), similarly for ∨ with S ∪ S ′ , and both completions
                                                                         ′                        ′
are feasible. Hence Φ(S)+Φ(S ′ ) ≥ F̃ (1S∩S ′ , tS ∧tS )+ F̃ (1S∪S ′ , tS ∨tS ) ≥ Φ(S ∩S ′ )+Φ(S ∪S ′ ).

    Positive semidefiniteness is not used in (c); it makes each Φ(S) computable exactly in polynomial
time [119]. Let m = |R− | and Φ̂(S) = Φ(S) − Φ(∅). For an ordering π of R− with prefixes
                        π = R , the greedy vector aπ ∈ RR− has aπ

D.3     Proofs for Section 7.5
Proof of Lemma 7.23. List Gi as ai,0 , . . . , ai,ki , in increasing order if oi = 1 and in decreasing order
if oi = −1, so that δi,l = ai,l − ai,l−1 has the sign of oi . Encode yi = ai,l by zi ∈ {0, 1}ki with zi,l′ = 1
exactly for l′ ≤ l. This is a bijection between Gi and the nonincreasing vectors zi,1 ≥ · · · ≥ zi,ki ,
and on these vectors
                              X                                           X                       
                yi = ai,0 +       δi,l zi,l ,       φ(yi ) = φ(ai,0 ) +       φ(ai,l ) − φ(ai,l−1 ) zi,l
                              l                                           l

for every function φ on Gi . Apply the second formula to φi (t) = 12 Hii t2 + bi t + χi (t), and substitute
the first into each cross term Hij yi yj , i < j. On nonincreasing vectors this writes Fχ as a function
Φ(z) of the binary vector z = (zi,l ) of the form of Proposition 7.14(a), after the pairs (i, l) are
numbered, with pair coefficients Hij δi,l δj,l′ ≤ 0 for i ̸= j and none within one coordinate. Take the
network of Proposition 7.14(a) for Φ and add an arc (i, l + 1) → (i, l) of infinite capacity for each
i and 1 ≤ l < ki . A cut Sz has finite capacity exactly when no such arc leaves it, that is, when

                                                           105
z is nonincreasing in each coordinate; its capacity is then given by (7.12). The all-zero vector is
nonincreasing, so a minimum cut is finite and decodes to a minimizer of Fχ , and a flow of equal
value certifies it by weak duality. Fixing yi = v is the same construction with Gi = {v}, and the
restricted function is still balanced.

Proof of the work bounds in Theorem 7.24(b). If L = 0, then P = ∅ and CT (Algorithm 5.6) makes
one cut computation. Otherwise point growth with constant g gives weighted growth with γ = g/L,
because ∥d∥2L ≤ L∥d∥2 , so√κ̄ ≤ κ. By the proof of Theorem 5.7(b), CT succeeds at the latest at
                       ∗            √                    √
the trial µ∗ with 2µ ≤ 6 κ̄ ≤ 6 κ, so at most log2 (6 κ) trials are run; the trial with θ = 2−µ
                               √
has at most Kµ ≤ Kµ∗ = O( κ log n) nodes per coordinate, and each trial has O(I + q + 1) stages.
A stage performs at most 1 + nKµ cut computations on networks with at most nKµ + 2 nodes
and 3nKµ + n2 Kµ2 arcs. As in Appendix A, grid nodes have denominators dividing ΓX 2α and
the values of F and of the corrections at grid points have denominators dividing 8ΓF Γ2X 4α , with
α = O(I + q + µKµ ). Each capacity of Lemma 7.23 with χi = −di is a sum of O(nKµ ) terms, each a
coefficient of F times at most two grid nodes or differences of grid nodes, a correction value, or half
of such a term. So the capacities have a common denominator dividing 8ΓF Γ2X 4α and numerators
of O(I + α + log(nKµ )) bits. Give each arc of infinite capacity the capacity one plus the sum of all
finite capacities; this changes neither the minimum cuts nor the maximum-flow value. After scaling
by the common denominator, the Edmonds–Karp algorithm performs a number of augmentations
polynomial in the network size, each on integers bounded by the total capacity. Multiplying these
polynomial bounds gives the bound for CT. Under point growth, the proof of Theorem 6.14 shows
that EX (Algorithm 6.13) stops at an accuracy q = poly(I) + O(log κ) after O(log q) calls of CT,
and that REC then needs only Gaussian elimination; this gives the bound for EX.


E     Coupling constraints: checks, exact output and examples
This appendix gives the checks of the model of Section 8.1, an example for the filter of Section 8.4,
the algorithm TU-EXACT with the proof of Theorem 8.10, and the details for Section 8.7.

E.1    Checking the model
Remark E.1 (Directional versus coordinate curvature). With W = Rnc , (8.2) implies, for each z ∈ Z,
the coordinate curvature condition of Definition 3.1 with Li = L̄ for the continuous coordinates, but
not conversely (Example 8.5). The following checks of (8.2) are rational, take time polynomial in I,
and change no factor scope. Two of them use that the largest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix is
at most its largest absolute row sum.
  (i) Explicit polynomial factors of fixed degree, any W. For continuous coordinates i, i′ write
      ∂ii′ F = α cα v α , a sum over finitely many exponent vectors α, let [µα , µα ] be the range of the
              P

      monomial v α on the box [ℓ, u] × k [min Zk , max Zk ], computed as in the proof of Lemma 6.20,
                                        Q

      and put                                  X          
                                        bii′ =   |cα | max |µα |, |µα | .
                                               α
      Then |∂ F | ≤ b on that box, which contains [ℓ, u] × Z. Hence every positive rational that is
              ii′     ii′
      at least maxi i′ bii′ bounds the largest eigenvalue of ∇2xx F (x, z) for all x ∈ [ℓ, u] and z ∈ Z,
                    P

      and is a valid L̄ for every subspace W. There are n2c bounds bii′ , each a sum of at most as
      many terms as F has monomials, and for factors of degree at most d the range endpoints have
      bit length O(dI). For a quadratic, bii′ = |(Hxx )ii′ |, and the bound is the largest absolute row
      sum of Hxx .

                                                   106
 (ii) Quadratic factors, W = ker C. Then L̄ is valid if and only if ΞT C (L̄I − Hxx )ΞC ⪰ 0 for a basis
      matrix ΞC of ker C; no orthonormality of ΞC is needed, since w = ΞC c gives wT (L̄I − Hxx )w =
      cT ΞT                                                                         T    T −1
          C (L̄I − Hxx )ΞC c. Alternatively, if C has full row rank and ΠC = I − C (CC ) C, every
      positive rational that is at least the largest absolute row sum of ΠC Hxx ΠC is valid, because
      ΠC w = w for w ∈ ker C.

Proposition E.2 (Checking alignment without enumerating Z). Fix z 0 ∈ Z and let Bk be the kth
column of B. Condition (8.1) holds if and only if ℓ/η and u/η are integral, (b − Bz 0 )/η ∈ Zm , and
Bk (t − zk0 )/η ∈ Zm for every k and every t ∈ Zk . This takes 1 + k |Zk | vector tests.

E.2     Union versus hull filtering
Example E.3 (Two minimizers per block: union versus hull). For one block take continuous
x1 , x2 , x3 ∈ [0, 1] with the TU equality x1 + x2 = x3 and

                                    Fb (x) = (2x1 − x3 )2 + 41 x3 (1 − x3 ).

Both terms are nonnegative on the feasible set Xb of the block, so Sb = {(0, 0, 0), ( 12 , 12 , 1)} and F ∗ = 0.
On Xb put w = x1 − x3 /2, so x2 = x3 /2 − w and Fb = 4w2 + x3 (1 − x3 )/4. If x3 ≤ 1/2, the squared
distance to (0, 0, 0) is 2w2 + 32 x23 and x3 (1−x3 )/4 ≥ x3 /8 ≥ x23 /4, so Fb ≥ 4w2 +x23 /4 ≥ 16 (2w2 + 32 x23 ).
If x3 ≥ 1/2, the same computation with 1 − x3 in place of x3 and the minimizer ( 12 , 12 , 1) gives the
same inequality. Hence gS = 1/6. The Hessian has rows (8, 0, −4), (0, 0, 0), (−4, 0, 3/2), so L̄ = 12
is valid. For nb independent blocks, nc = 3nb , and the bags are the blocks (p = 3). The optimal
set is the product of the block sets, so squared distances to it add over blocks and gS = 1/6 and
L̄ = 12 are unchanged; every projection Si has two points, and there are 2nb minimizers. With
η = 1, the hull variant keeps [0, 1] in the coordinate x3 of every block at every level, because both 0
and 1 are optimal values, so its level-j grid has 2j + 1 nodes there. The union variant has at most
         √
2(5 + ⌊2 216nb ⌋) nodes per coordinate at every level j ≥ 1 by Theorem 8.8.

E.3     Stationary face polytopes and rational height

subsection. For z ∈ Z put ϕz (x) = F (x, z), so that ∆∇ϕz (x) = Ĥxx x + cz ; for a set J of rows,
ÂJ consists of the rows of Â in J . If z is integral and x ∈ ρ−1 Znc for a positive integer ρ, then
∆ρ2 F (x, z) ∈ Z, since ∆ clears every monomial coefficient.

Corollary E.6 (Rational height with TU coupling). (a) Some global minimizer has continuous
coordinates in ρ−1 Z for an integer 1 ≤ ρ ≤ ∆η RTU , and every isolated global minimizer has this
property. (b) F ∗ = a/W in lowest terms with 1 ≤ W ≤ ΩTU .

Proof. (a) For ṽ ∈ S the polytope P(ṽ) has a vertex, which is a minimizer by Lemma E.5(b) and
has the stated height by Lemma E.5(c). If ṽ is isolated in S, the convex set P(ṽ) × {z} ⊆ S contains
no other point near ṽ, so P(ṽ) = {x̃} and x̃ is its vertex. (b) Evaluating F at a minimizer of height
ρ ≤ ∆η RTU gives ∆ρ2 F ∗ ∈ Z, so W ≤ ∆(∆η RTU )2 = ΩTU .

    Corollary E.6 is an explicit-constant instance of the classical fact that quadratic programs over
polyhedra have optimal solutions of polynomial size [177]; for mixed-integer quadratic programs see
[55].

E.4    The algorithm TU-EXACT and snapping on rows
TU-EXACT, described in Section 8.6, is the following algorithm.


                                                   108
Algorithm E.7 (TU-EXACT: exact output with TU coupling). Run TU-GRID (Algorithm 8.6)
without the stopping test of step (2). Between steps (2) and (3) of every level j, let (y, z) = y (j)
and:

  (i) let J = {r : (b̂z )r − Âr y ≤ τTU }, and compute a basis matrix ΞJ of ker ÂJ by Gaussian
      elimination;

 (ii) find by exact linear programming a point x̂ of

                          Pz (J ) = x ∈ Pz : ÂJ x = (b̂z )J , ΞT
                                     
                                                                J (Ĥxx x + cz ) = 0 ,

      or continue with step (3) if Pz (J ) is empty;

 (iii) if F (x̂, z) − βj < 1/(ΩTU W ), where W is the reduced denominator of F (x̂, z), return (x̂, z),
       F (x̂, z) and the certificate of levels 0, . . . , j; otherwise continue with step (3).

    The large denominators of y enter only the comparisons that define J . The rest of steps (i)
and (ii) uses only the original data, the index set J and the integer vector z, so it takes time
polynomial in I [82, Theorem 6.4.12].

Remark E.9 (Unique minimizers without linear programming). Assume (8.3) with S = {v ∗ }. Then
the hull variant can return v ∗ by continued fractions alone. By Corollary E.6(a), vx∗ ∈ ρ−1 Znc with
ρ ≤ ∆η RTU . By Proposition 8.7(d) and Theorem 8.8(c), the retained hull in coordinate i contains
vi∗ and has length at most 2aj + 2hj , and every retained value of a discrete coordinate k lies within
aj of vk∗ . Two distinct rationals with denominators at most ∆η RTU differ by at least (∆η RTU )−2 ,
so once 2aj + 2hj < (∆η RTU )−2 and aj < 1, the rational of least denominator in each continuous
hull is vi∗ , and each value set is {vk∗ }, since values are integers. Once also Ej < Ω−2
                                                                                       TU , the candidate
passes the test of step (iii) of TU-EXACT. A candidate is returned only if it is feasible and passes
this test, so the test, not the reconstruction, carries the proof.

                      min Fλ (y) − d1 (y1 ) − d2 (y2 ) : y ∈ (G1 × G2 ) ∩ X > F ∗ .

F.2    Coordinatewise concave quadratics
Proof of Lemma 9.6. Since Bt↑′ ⊆ Bt for a child t′ , the bracket is a function of vBt , and rt ≥ 0
because Mt is its minimum over the labels of Bt \ Bt↑ .
    Telescoping. Let v ∈ E([n]). Every nonroot table Mt occurs in t rt (vBt ) once with a plus sign,
                                                                       P

in the residual of its parent, and once with a minus sign. Hence t rt (vBt ) = t qt (vBt ) − Mt0 =
                                                                      P             P

F (v) − Mt0 .
    Expectation. Fix x ∈ X̄ and let Y be the endpoint rounding of Section 9.2. Then EYi = xi ,
                                                    ̸ j, and EYi2 = x2i + Var Yi . Therefore EF (Y ) =
Var Yi = (xi − ℓi )(ui − xi ), EYi Yj = xi xj for i =
         1P
F (x) + 2 i Hii (xi − ℓi )(ui − xi ), while Ert (YBt ) = v rt (v)πt (v; x). Taking expectations in the
                                                         P

telescoping identity gives (9.3) with Mt0 in place of F ∗ .
    Value. Every term on the right of (9.3) is nonnegative on X̄, so F ≥ Mt0 on X̄ ⊇ X. Trace
back: choose labels of Bt0 attaining Mt0 ; for each child t′ of a processed bag t, choose the labels
of Bt′ \ Bt↑′ attaining Mt′ (vB ↑ ). By running intersection, a variable belongs to Bt \ Bt↑ exactly for
                              t′
the bag t nearest the root among those containing it, and to Bt↑ for every other bag containing it.
Hence every variable receives exactly one label, and the resulting endpoint assignment v ∗ has all
residuals zero. By telescoping F (v ∗ ) = Mt0 . Both endpoints of every Xi are feasible, so v ∗ ∈ X and
F ∗ = Mt0 .
    Checking. The argument uses only the nonnegativity of the residuals and one endpoint assignment
with zero residuals, so any tables with these two properties give (9.3).
    Cost. A bag table has at most 2p entries, and children are combined by addition. By induction
on the tree, every message entry is the sum of the assigned factors over the subtree at one endpoint
assignment. Its bit length is therefore bounded by the bit length of a common denominator of all
endpoint monomial values plus log2 of the number of monomials times their maximum magnitude,
both polynomial in I.

Proof of Corollary 9.8. (i) Condition (a) of Theorem 9.7 says χi (x) ̸= ∗ when Hii < 0; and χi (x) = ∗
is possible only if Xi has a point strictly between its endpoints. Condition (b) says that every v
with πt (v; x) > 0 has rt (v) = 0, that is, cube(χ(x)Bt ) ⊆ Zt .
    (ii) Replacing ∗ by an endpoint shrinks every cube. If χ is admissible and x ∈ X ∩ face(χ), then
χ(x) is obtained from χ by replacing some ∗ entries by endpoints, so x is optimal by (i). Conversely
x ∈ S lies in face(χ(x)). A pattern χ determines the set {x ∈ X : χ(x) = χ}, which is nonempty by
the definition of Σi .


                                                  113
    (iii) Each Admt is computed by testing at most 3p patterns against at most 2p residual entries.
A constraint problem whose relations live on the bags of a tree decomposition is solved by nonserial
dynamic programming over (T, (Bt )) in the min-sum, max-sum and sum-product semirings, with
O(p 3p ) operations per bag [50]; each unary cost is charged once, at the bag nearest the root that
contains its variable. The minimizer is unique if and only if exactly one pattern is admissible and
it has no ∗, by the disjointness in (ii). The dimension of S is the maximum number of ∗ entries
on continuous coordinates. When S is finite, |S| = χ i:χi =∗ |Xi ∩ (ℓi , ui )|. By (ii), dist(y, S)2 is
                                                     P Q

the minimum over admissible χ of the sum of the unary costs (yi − ℓi )2 , (yi − ui )2 and dist(yi , Xi )2
for χi = L, U and ∗. A linear objective attains its minimum over each X ∩ face(χ) at an endpoint
pattern, so it is minimized by the same recursion over {L, U} labels. All numbers are rationals of
polynomial bit length.

F.3    The diagonal certificate and the proximal stage
As in Section 9.3, X is a continuous box and F a rational quadratic.

Proof of Lemma 9.10. (i) Both sides are quadratic polynomials in x that vanish at x̄. Their Hessians
are H and H + Λ − Λ = H. The ith partial derivative of the right side at x̄ is 12 λi (x̄)(ui + ℓi − 2x̄i ).
This equals |∂i F (x̄)| = ∂i F (x̄) when x̄i = ℓi , equals −|∂i F (x̄)| = ∂i F (x̄) when x̄i = ui , and is
0 = ∂i F (x̄) at an interior coordinate. Two quadratics with equal value, gradient and Hessian at one
point coincide.
    (ii) On X both terms on the right of (9.4) are nonnegative, so x̄ is optimal, and x ∈ X is optimal
exactly when both vanish. A positive semidefinite quadratic form vanishes exactly on the kernel of
its matrix.
    (iii) For x ∈ X the bracket in Ψ is at most F (x), so Ψ(λ) ≤ F ∗ . If (a) holds at x̄, then by (i) the
bracket with λ = λ(x̄) equals F (x̄)+ 12 (x− x̄)T (H +Λ(x̄))(x− x̄), whose infimum over Rn is F (x̄) = F ∗ ;
this gives (c). Suppose (c) holds for some λ, and let x̄ ∈ S be arbitrary. Write L(x) for the bracket.
Then F ∗ = Ψ(λ) ≤ L(x̄) ≤ F (x̄) = F ∗ . Hence λi (x̄i − ℓi )(ui − x̄i ) = 0 for all i, and x̄ minimizes   the
                      n        2                                                                  ℓi +ui 
quadratic L over R . So ∇ L = H + diag(λ) ⪰ 0 and 0 = ∂i L(x̄) = ∂i F (x̄) + λi x̄i − 2 . At
an interior coordinate, complementarity gives λi = 0 = λi (x̄). At x̄i = ℓi the stationarity equation
gives λi = 2∂i F (x̄)/(ui − ℓi ) = λi (x̄), and similarly at x̄i = ui . Thus λ = λ(x̄) and H + Λ(x̄) ⪰ 0.
Since x̄ ∈ S was arbitrary, this proves (b) and the uniqueness and invariance statements. Finally
(b) implies (a) because S =  ̸ ∅.

Example F.1 (Tilted and disconnected optimal sets in D). Let F = (x − y + t/2)2 + 18 t(1 − t) on
[0, 1]3 , and a = (1, −1, 21 ). Both terms are nonnegative on the box and vanish together exactly when
t ∈ {0, 1} and x − y + t/2 = 0. So F ∗ = 0, and S consists of the two segments {(v, v, 0) : 0 ≤ v ≤ 1}
and {(v, v + 12 , 1) : 0 ≤ v ≤ 21 }. The Hessian H = 2aaT − diag(0, 0, 14 ) has diagonal (2, 2, 14 ) and is
indefinite, since w = (0, 1, 2) has aT w = 0 and wT Hw = −1. At every minimizer ∂x F = ∂y F = 0
and ∂t F = 18 (1 − 2t), which is 18 at t = 0 and − 18 at t = 1. So every minimizer is a box KKT point
with Λ = diag(0, 0, 14 ), and H + Λ = 2aaT ⪰ 0; hence F ∈ D.

Proposition F.4 (Discovery within D is hard without the growth parameter). For integers
a0 , a1 , . . . , am with A = mi=1 |ai | ≥ 1 and |a0 | ≤ A, let ξ0 = 0 and
                             P

                     F (x, ξ) =         (ξi − ξi−1 − ai xi )2 + (ξm − a0 )2 +         xi (1 − xi )
                                  i=1                                           i=1

on x ∈ [0, 1]m , ξ ∈ [−A, A]m . The bags {ξ1 , x1 } and {ξi−1 , ξi , xi } (2 ≤ i ≤ m) form a path
decomposition with bag size at most 3. If some x̂ ∈ {0, 1}m has m                                  ∗

                                                                  P
                                          ˆ                                ˆ
and S consists exactly of the points (x̂, ξ) with x̂ such a solution and ξ its partial sums. Otherwise
F ∗ > 0. Consequently, unless P = NP:

  (i) no polynomial-time algorithm returns an exact minimizer for every instance of D with bag
      size three, even though F ∗ = 0 is known in advance for these instances;

                                                        116
 (ii) there is no polynomial bound κS ≤ poly(I) that holds, for a suitable set-growth constant, on
      every instance of this form in D.

   The objective is the Subset Sum reduction of [53, Remark 2] with unscaled partial sums; what
the proposition adds is that its feasible instances belong to D.

Proof of Proposition F.4. F ≥ 0 on the box, and F = 0 exactly when all squares and all products
xi (1 − xi ) vanish, that is, for binary x with partial sums ξ and ξm = a0 ; the partial sums lie in
[−A, A]. If no solution exists, F > 0 on the compact box. In the feasible case let ẑ = (x̂, ξ)      ˆ be
optimal. The sum of squares is a quadratic Φ with Φ(ẑ) = 0 and ∇Φ(ẑ) = 0, because each square
is of an affine function vanishing at ẑ; so Φ(z) = 12 (z − ẑ)T Hsq (z − ẑ) with Hsq = ∇2 Φ ⪰ 0. Also
                    1P                                           1        T               1P
   i xi (1 − xi ) = 2 i 2(xi − 0)(1 − xi ). So F (z) − F (ẑ) = 2 (z − ẑ) Hsq (z − ẑ) + 2 i 2xi (1 − xi ).
P

At ẑ the gradient is 1 − 2x̂i = ±1 in xi and 0 in ξ, so ẑ is a box KKT point with λi (ẑ) = 2 on x and
0 on ξ, and H + Λ(ẑ) = Hsq ⪰ 0. Thus F ∈ D.
     Subset Sum with binary-encoded integers is NP-complete [75], also when restricted to instances
with A ≥ 1 and |a0 | ≤ A, because the other instances are decided trivially; and the instance F
has size polynomial in that of the Subset Sum instance. (i) An algorithm as in (i), stopped at its
polynomial time bound and followed by a check that its output z is feasible with F (z) = 0, would
decide Subset Sum. (ii) If such a polynomial bound existed, then by Theorem 9.14(c), whose bound
is nondecreasing and polynomial in κS for fixed p = 3, DISC would stop within a polynomial time
bound on every feasible instance; stopping DISC at that bound and checking whether it has output
F ∗ = 0 would decide Subset Sum.

    Unless P = NP, Corollary 9.8 has no counterpart in D. Take a0 = 0 in Proposition F.4. The
origin is then a minimizer, so the instance belongs to D, and the origin is the only minimizer exactly
when no nonempty subset of a1 , . . . , am sums to zero. Deciding whether such a subset exists is
NP-complete: Subset Sum with positive items b1 , . . . , bk and target T > 0 reduces to it by adding
the item −T , because a nonempty subset with sum zero must contain −T . Hence deciding whether
a given minimizer of an instance of D with bag size three is the only one is coNP-hard, even when
the description (9.5) is given.


G     Proofs and supplements for Section 10
This appendix computes the conditioning of the bounded-coefficient reduction of Del Pia and
Khajavirad, proves Proposition 10.6 and the propositions of Sections 10.4–10.6, and gives the local
moment relaxations of Section 10.7.

G.1     Conditioning of the bounded-coefficient reduction
Remark G.1 (Conditioning of the bounded-coefficient reduction). Consider the construction of [53,
Theorem 3] for the two-item instance a1 = c, a2 = c − 1 with target a0 = c, where c ≥ 3, with
objective Ψ (their (20)–(24), all weights one) on the unit box. In their notation, ℓ = ⌈log2 (2c)⌉,
D = 2ℓ ≥ 2c, and there are 5ℓ + 2 variables. Every term of Ψ is nonnegative. A zero of Ψ encodes a
binary solution, and (1, 0) is the only one; all copied and state variables are then determined by the
recurrences. Hence Ψ has a unique minimizer v ∗ with value zero, and by Lemma 6.12 some growth
constant g > 0 is valid.
    Encode instead the choice (0, 1) with every copy, digit and running-sum recurrence exact. Only
the final square (s2 − wℓ )2 = ((c − 1 − c)/D)2 is nonzero, so Ψ(y ′ ) = D−2 . All ℓ copies of both



                                                   117
choice bits change by one, so ∥y ′ − v ∗ ∥2 ≥ 2ℓ and every valid growth constant satisfies g ≤ 1/(2ℓD2 ).
The largest diagonal entry of ∇2 Ψ is ten, attained by the digit-state variables. Therefore

                                                κ ≥ 20ℓD2 ≥ 80ℓc2 .

For c = 2k , k ≥ 2, there are 5k + 7 variables and coefficients bounded by five, so κ ≥ 80(k + 1)4k
is exponential in the number of variables. The instance has O(k) monomials and bags, listed by
variable indices of Θ(log k) bits, so I = Θ(k log k) and κ ≥ 2Ω(I/ log I) , consistent with Corollary 10.2.
    Negative curvature is also large relative to growth. Let ν be the smallest number with ∇2 Ψ ⪰ −νI.
Encode the fractional choice (1/c, 1); since c · c−1 + (c − 1) · 1 = c, every square residual can be kept
at zero with all variables in [0, 1], and Ψ(y) = ϱ/c with ϱ = 1 − 1/c. The displacement d = y − v ∗
annihilates every affine residual, so dT ∇2 Ψ d = −2(ϱ2 + 1), coming from the two endpoint penalties.
Every valid growth constant has g ≤ ϱ/(c∥d∥2 ), while ν ≥ 2(ϱ2 + 1)/∥d∥2 . Hence
                                               ν         1
                                                 ≥ 2c ϱ +    ≥ 4c.
                                               g          ϱ
This bound on ν/g also holds for arbitrary positive weights on the squares and a common positive
weight on the two endpoint penalties: both estimates scale with the penalty weight, and the square
weights do not enter. The reduction therefore does not contradict an algorithm parameterized by
ν/g either.

G.2     Proof of Proposition 10.6
Lemma G.2 (Isolation [140]). Let F be a nonempty family of subsets of a finite set U, and let
w : U → {1, . . . , ρ} be uniformly random. Then the minimum of w(Z) = e∈Z w(e) over Z ∈ F is
                                                                      P

attained by a unique set with probability at least 1 − |U|/ρ.
Proof. For e ∈ U let αe = min{w(Z) : Z ∈ F, e ∈     / Z} − min{w(Z \ {e}) : Z ∈ F, e ∈ Z} (with
min ∅ = +∞); αe does not depend on w(e). If two distinct minimizing sets exist, some e lies in
exactly one of them, and then w(e) = αe . For fixed e this event has probability at most 1/ρ, because
w(e) is uniform and independent of αe . A union bound over e ∈ U gives probability at most |U|/ρ
that the minimizing set is not unique.

encodings of multicolored cliques, and each clique has exactly one encoding because the pairs in Ecd

    Conditioning. Suppose the minimum-weight clique is unique. The values of Ψw are integral and
its minimizer z ∗ is unique, so Ψw (z) − F ∗ ≥ 1 for z ̸= z ∗ . All domains have length at most N0 , and
there are n ≤ k + 2k 2 N02 variables, so ∥z − z ∗ ∥2 ≤ nN02 and g = 1/(nN02 ) is a valid growth constant.
The diagonal Hessian entries are at most W0 (2 + 4N02 + 2k). Hence κ ≤ W0 (2 + 4N02 + 2k)nN02 ,
and both κ and I are at most (kN0 )c2 for an absolute constant c2 .
    Reduction. Run the assumed algorithm for f (p)((kN0 )2c2 )p/ψ(p) steps. If it outputs a feasible
ẑ with Ψw (ẑ) < W0 , then Φ(ẑ) = 0 and ẑ encodes a clique; answer yes. Otherwise answer no.
There are no false positives. If a clique exists, Lemma G.2, with U = E, ρ = 2|E| and F the
family of edge sets of multicolored cliques (which determine the cliques since k ≥ 2), makes the
minimum-weight clique unique with probability at least 1/2, and then the algorithm halts in time
with Ψw (ẑ) ≤ W0 − 1/2. For k ≥ 7 this is a one-sided-error randomized algorithm for multicolored
                                   2c k/ψ(k)+O(1)
clique with running time f ′ (k)N0 2                 for a computable f ′ .
    Clique reduces to multicolored clique with the same k: take k copies of the vertex set as color
classes, and join the copies of u and v in different classes when uv is an edge; the classes then
have N0 elements for a graph on N0 vertices. Under ETH, Clique on graphs with N0 vertices
                                                o(k)
has no algorithm with running time f (k)N0             for a computable f [36], [44, Theorem 14.21]; the
exponent 2c2 k/ψ(k) + O(1) is computable and o(k). The proof of this lower bound composes the
sparsification lemma with reductions from 3-SAT to Clique, all deterministic. Composing them and
the reduction from Clique to multicolored clique with the algorithm above, run twice to reduce the
error probability to 1/4, gives a randomized algorithm for 3-SAT with error probability at most 1/3
                        ′
and running time 2o(n ) on n′ variables, which contradicts rETH.
    The reduction uses only instances with p = k, and k may be restricted to k ≥ k0 for any
                                                                                    o(k)
fixed k0 , because finitely many values of k do not affect a bound N0 . So the first claim
holds even for algorithms whose time bound is required only for p ≥ k0 . For the last claim,

and put ψ ′ (p) = max{1, min{ψ(p), p/C}}. Then ψ ′ is computable, nondecreasing, unbounded
and at least one, and for p ≥ C it equals min{ψ(p), p/C}, so that p/ψ ′ (p) ≥ max{a(p), C} and,
                                                   ′
because κ, I ≥ 1, f (p)κa(p) I C ≤ f (p)(κI)p/ψ (p) . The first claim with k0 = max{7, C} excludes
this running time. Finally, Theorem 5.7 with q = 1 runs in time f (p, κ)(I + 2)5 ≤ 35 f (p, κ)I 5 ,
                          √
where f (p, κ) = c0 (c1 p κ)p κ(1 + log2 κ)2 ≤ 4c0 (c1 p)p κp/2+2 because (1 + log2 κ)2 ≤ 4κ. So
a(p) = p/2 + O(1) and C = 5.

    In the first representation, on an interval with nonempty interior, V0 coincides with a polynomial
of degree at most two. It is not identically zero, since V0 > 0 off the integers. So it has at most two
zeros, and the interval contains at most two integers. A degenerate interval contains at most one.
Hence 2Npc ≥ 2m . In the second representation, each integer j has some l with φl (j) = V (j). Then
φl − v 2 ≥ V0 ≥ 0 on [0, 2m − 1] and vanishes at j. If φl − v 2 ≡ 0, then V0 ≤ 0, which is false; so
φl − v 2 vanishes at most at two integers, and again 2Npc ≥ 2m .

G.4    Local moment relaxations
This subsection gives the details behind Section 10.7. Throughout, F (x) = 12 xT Hx + bT x + F (0) is
a quadratic on a continuous box X with H ⪰ −νI for some ν ≥ 0. An error bound in which L is
replaced by ν holds when all coordinates are conditioned on one global cell.

    A global cell index can take K n values when every coordinate is split into K intervals. Sparse
relaxations keep exact information only on bags and match moments across separators.

   The two bag chains realize the even and the odd part of a binomial law on the separator, whose
moments agree up to order 2r − 1. The example does not contradict an error bound in terms of L,
which is of order r/h2 here. It also does not survive exact matching of separator laws: on a tree
decomposition, bag laws that agree on every separator glue to a probability measure on X [180],
whose value is at least F ∗ . The following remark shows that an inequality coupling both sides of a
separator detects the gap.

H      Additional computational results
This appendix gives the details and tables that Section 11 summarizes.

Implementation (Section 11.1). The implementation differs from the analysis in the following
ways; none of them affects validity.
  (a) All coordinates share the mesh hj = s2−j , and the analysis that applies is Lemma 5.9 with
      the Euclidean condition number κ, not Theorem 5.7.
  (b) The cap of trial µ is 100 · 2µ ⌈log2 (n + 2)⌉, 12.5 times the cap of Lemma 5.9, and the stage
      limit is the least J with 78 Lns2 4−J ≤ ε instead of 16
                                                            9
                                                              Lns2 4−J ≤ ε, so a trial may run one stage
      longer.
  (c) Each trial starts with the center at the current incumbent instead of ℓ. Validity and the rate
      analysis allow any first center (the bound on ∥c − x∗ ∥ in (G2) of Lemma 5.9 holds at j = 0 for
      every c ∈ X); the bit-length analysis of Appendix A does not cover it.
  (d) Before stage 0, two sweeps of exact coordinate descent from ℓ, u and the box midpoint provide
      the first incumbent; after each stage the same descent, within the current box, starts from
      the grid minimizer yj . An optional presolve certifies convex instances by an exact LDLT
      factorization and a rational KKT point.
  (e) The lower bound used in the success test and reported at the end is the largest bound of all
      stages and trials, including the initial interval bound, rather than βj of the last stage.
  (f) The “uniform” grids of the experiments are the grids of Definition 5.1 with θ = 0 around one
      center, not the unions of cells of UC (Algorithm 9.2).
  (g) The exact-output wrapper, which implements EX (Algorithm 6.13), uses the acceptance rule of
      Proposition 6.6 with the row-sum height constant of Remark 6.5, with a common denominator
      that also clears Hij /2; this constant is never smaller than the one in (6.1). It starts at
      q = 4, doubles q after each round, restarts the grid solver from the full box in every round
      with the incumbent as its first center and, in place of ℓ, as a start of the descent, and tests
      three candidates after each round: the incumbent, its coordinatewise rational reconstruction
      (Remark 6.15) and the stationary point of REC (Algorithm 6.7).

                                                             124
Further modules, each with its own checker, implement exact linear and convex box-QP oracles
with primal, dual or Farkas witnesses, affine recourse recognition and lifting, minimum-cut and
convex value-factor recourse, the TU-constrained grids, the endpoint optimal-set descriptor and
explicit polynomial factors. The convex value-factor and recourse checkers reuse the solver’s grid,
table and lifting code, so only the main checker is independent in the sense of Section 11.1.

Planted family (Section 11.2). Lemma 3.3(a) gives the certified lower bound g0 on the growth
constant, by an exact LDLT test of H + 2M − 2g0 I, and part (b) with a rational approximate
eigenvector of HJ0 J0 gives an upper bound; with L = 2 this brackets κ to within 12%.
    The following observation shows when a free coordinate of x∗ can be a grid node. Within a
                                                                                               (0)
trial every node of coordinate i is either a dyadic rational or the sum of the first center ci and
a dyadic rational, because the endpoints ±1, the meshes and the gradings are dyadic, filtering
moves endpoints only to nodes, and every later center is a node. A coordinate x∗i = k/21 with
                                    (0)
k ̸= 0 is therefore a node only if ci , which the coordinate descent provides, differs from x∗i by a
dyadic rational. In the 78 single-trial runs of E1 with filtering and of E3 with κtarget = 4, 153 free
coordinates of x∗ were nodes of the last grid: 146 had already been set to x∗i by the initial descent,
3 had x∗i = 0, and in the other 4 the first center differed from x∗i by a nonzero dyadic rational.

Experiments E1–E3 (Section 11.2). The graded runs of E1 with filtering and the runs of E2
and E3 with the theorem’s grading satisfy 8κθ2 ≤ 1. At all 876 stages of these runs, the gap D(yj )
                                9                                                   √
was at most 0.16 Lnh2j (bound 16  Lnh2j ), the retained radius at most 0.18 of 4.2 nκ hj , and the
number of nodes per coordinate at most 5% of the cap 8θ−1 ⌈log2 (n + 2)⌉ (and at most 0.4% of the
implementation’s cap). The radius ratio uses the lower end of the certified interval for κ, which can
only
   pincrease it. On the separable instances of E3 (κtarget = 2), filtered uniform grids used exactly
4(⌊ n/4⌋ + 1) + 1 nodes; Section 11.2 reports the coupled case because the separable one does not
test coupling.

Exact output (Section 11.4). In E4, the 15 certified instances that needed more than one stage
needed 67–77, 132–144, 275 and 534 stages for 49–65, 80–120, 208 and 342 bits, with last-round
precisions q = 64, 128, 256 and 512.

Comparison with SCIP (Section 11.5). Table 4 gives the results of E5 per instance class.
The planted objectives have constants between 94 and 1.6 · 103 and optimal value 0, so the absolute
target 10−6 is between 6 · 10−10 and 1.1 · 10−8 of the constant; the inward derivatives at the active
coordinates are between 23 and 192.5. A rerun of the 21 SCIP runs reproduced every primal bound.
For SCIP’s solution (x, t), let x̄ be the projection of x onto the box; then F (x̄) − t is the shortfall
up to at most 1.3 · 10−14 . The rerun evaluated F exactly and split F (x̄) − t into the bound part
F (x̄) − F (x) and the epigraph part F (x) − t. In every run the violated bounds were exactly those
of the coordinates at a bound in x∗ , each violated outward by about 10−8 , and SCIP’s own check
accepted the solution. The bound part was 51% to 94% of the shortfall, and the epigraph part
was between 9.00 · 10−7 and 9.03 · 10−7 . In separate runs, giving SCIP 60 seconds (paths with
n ≥ 32), setting its primal and dual feasibility tolerances to 10−9 (paths with n ≥ 32), or moving
the objective constant into an offset (all 21 instances) did not change the outcome: SCIP stopped
at the time limit in all 27 runs on paths with n ≥ 32 (with 60 seconds, at dual bounds between
−7.5 · 10−4 and −5.1 · 10−5 ); with the offset, the exact gap on the 12 instances with n ≤ 16 was still
1.9 · 10−6 to 5.8 · 10−6 ; and its reported interval contained the optimum in none of the 39 runs.

Random instances (Section 11.6). The instances of E6 have diagonal entries k/2 with k uniform
in {−4, . . . , 8}, interaction entries ±k/4 with k ∈ {1, . . . , 6}, linear entries k/4 with k ∈ {−8, . . . , 8},
and band width 2 for the band instances. The candidate x̂ is the stationary point of the face of
the final incumbent at ε = 2−50 . When the sufficient condition of Lemma 3.3(a) holds at x̂, it
proves that x̂ is the unique minimizer and gives a lower bound g0 on the point-growth constant;
an upper bound comes from part (b) and from the definition of growth at the points obtained by
moving one active coordinate of x̂ to its other endpoint. With L = maxi max{Hii , 0} this brackets
κ. The condition held on 11 instances, with the certified intervals [6.2, 6.6], [11.6, 15.7], [14.0, 22.3],
[6.1, 24.4], [10.0, 28.6], [8.1, 31.6], [5.1, 55.8], [9.5, 67.3], [6.8, 141.9], [4.5, 182.8] and [8.0, 6662.2] for κ
(rounded outward). On the other 29 instances the matrix H + 2M was not positive definite. On 28 of
them the localized test of Proposition 6.17, applied to the same certificate with the stationary point
of the face of the stage incumbent as candidate, proved that candidate optimal; on one instance
neither test succeeded. At ε = 2−50 the 11 instances with certified growth had 9 to 11 nodes per
coordinate, the other 29 had 8 to 18. The candidates are mostly off the grids: at ε = 2−50 , 42 of the
213 free coordinates of the candidates were nodes of the last grid. Table 5 gives the grid sizes per
instance class.

Recourse (Section 11.7). The affine stars have a core coordinate whose own term −x20 is concave
and 4 or 16 leaves coupled to it by stiff convex squares 64(yi − x0 /2)2 , with the variables in random
order; recognition finds the globally affine responses, and the reduced problem is solved exactly. The
piecewise instances are the functions ϕM − z 2 with ϕM from Example C.11, for M = 1 and M = 100.
The dense instances (n = 7 and 33) have one convex core coordinate and a residual that is concave
in each coordinate and submodular, with a complete interaction graph, so the decomposition used
by plain grids has a bag of size n. Table 6 gives the results.
